Staircase patterns in Hirzebruch surfaces Thanks: NSF Graduate Research Grant DGE-1650441 Thanks: NSF Research Grant 2103245
Nicki Magill, Dusa McDuff, Morgan Weiler

Abstract
. The ellipsoidal capacity function of a symplectic four manifold X X measures how much the form on X X must be dilated in order for it to admit an embedded ellipsoid of eccentricity z z . In most cases there are just finitely many obstructions to such an embedding besides the volume. If there are infinitely many obstructions, X X is said to have a staircase. This paper gives an almost complete description of the staircases in the ellipsoidal capacity functions of the family of symplectic Hirzebruch surfaces H b H_{b} formed by blowing up the projective plane with weight b b . We describe an interweaving, recursively defined, family of obstructions to symplectic embeddings of ellipsoids that show there is an open dense set of shape parameters b b that are blocked, i.e. have no staircase, and an uncountable number of other values of b b that do admit staircases. The remaining b b -values form a countable sequence of special rational numbers that are closely related to the symmetries discussed in Magill–McDuff (arXiv:2106.09143). We show that none of them admit ascending staircases. Conjecturally, none admit descending staircases. Finally, we show that, as long as b b is not one of these special rational values, any staircase in H b H_{b} has irrational accumulation point. A crucial ingredient of our proofs is the new, more indirect approach to using almost toric fibrations in the analysis of staircases by Magill (arXiv:2204.12460). In particular, the structure of the relevant mutations of the set of almost toric fibrations on H b H_{b} is echoed in the structure of the set of blocked b b -intervals.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
53D05, 11Y99

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Overview and statement of main theorem

The ellipsoidal capacity function
c_{X}:[1,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
for a general four-dimensional target manifold (
X,{\omega})
is defined by

c_{X}(z):=\inf\left\{\lambda\ \Big|\ E(1,z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\hookrightarrow}}\lambda X\right\},

where
z\geq 1

is a real variable,
\lambda X:=(X,\lambda\omega)
, the ellipsoid
E(c,d)\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the set

E(c,d)=\left\{(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\ \big|\ \pi\left(\frac{|\zeta_{1}|^{2}}{c}+\frac{|\zeta_{2}|^{2}}{d}\right)<1\right\},

and we write
E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\lambda}X
if there is a symplectic embedding of
E
into
{\lambda}X
.

It is straightforward to see that

c_{X}(z)
is bounded below by the volume constraint function

V_{X}(z)=\sqrt{\frac{{\rm vol\,}E(1,z)}{{\rm vol\,}(X,{\omega})}}.

Using techniques developed in McDuff
[
M
]
, McDuff–Schlenk
[
McS
]
gave the first complete computation of this function for the case when
X
is the standard 4-ball, or, equivalently,
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. They found that the graph of this function has infinitely many nonsmooth points at values of
z
that are ratios of Fibonacci numbers.
The results of
[
M
]
were generalized by
Cristofaro-Gardiner
[
CG
]
, whose work implies that if
(X,{\omega})
is a
four-dimensional toric manifold or rational convex toric domain the function
z\mapsto c_{X}(z)
is piecewise linear when not identically equal to the volume constraint curve. When, as in the case of the ball, its graph has infinitely many nonsmooth points lying above the volume curve,
(X,{\omega})
is said to have a
staircase
.
1
1

1

This is often referred to as an infinite staircase in the literature, but we presuppose that a staircase has infinitely many steps. On the other hand a staircase need not contain all the nonsmooth points in a neighborhood of the accumulation point.

Four manifolds with staircases seem rather rare: Frenkel–Muller
[
FM
]
used the methods of
[
McS
]
to find a staircase for the monotone product
(S^{2}\oplus S^{2},{\omega}\times{\omega})
, while
in
[
CG-HMP
]
, Cristofaro-Gardiner–Holm–Mandini–Pires used methods from ECH (embedded contact homology) to find staircases for the monotone blowup of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
by up to four points. Their conjecture
[
CG-HMP
, Conj. 1.23]
proposes (among other things) that that these are the only
rational toric four manifolds with staircases. In contrast, Usher
[
U
]
found infinitely many irrational
\beta
such that the nonmonotone product
(S^{2}\times S^{2},{\omega}\oplus\beta{\omega})
admits a staircase.

The analogous question for the family of Hirzebruch surfaces

H_{b}:=\mathbb{CP}^{2}(1)\#\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}(b),0\leq b<1

was first investigated by
Bertozzi, Holm, Maw et al. in
[
BHM
]
, with work continued in Magill–McDuff
[
MM
]
. The current paper completes this circle of ideas and provides an almost complete
answer to the question of which
H_{b}
admit staircases.
Our main result, Theorem
1.1.1
below, is the key to the proof of a special case of
[
CG-HMP
, Conj. 1.23]
that is given in the forthcoming Magill–Pires–Weiler
[
MPW
]
. Finally, the structure explained here will, we hope, provide a guide for classifying the ellipsoidal capacity functions for more general toric blowups, see Remark
1.2.10
.

Throughout, we denote by
H_{b}
the one-point blowup of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
with line of area
1
by a ball of capacity
b
.
2
2

2

Since
H_{b}
is a rational four manifold, its symplectomorphism class is unique; see
[
MS
, Ex.7.1.16]
.
Its volume constraint is

V_{b}(z)=\sqrt{\frac{z}{1-b^{2}}},

where
1-b^{2}
is the appropriately normalized volume of
H_{b}
.
A key result from
[
CG-HMP
, Thm.1.13]
is that
if
c_{H_{b}}
has a staircase, then the
steps
(i.e. nonsmooth local maxima) of this staircase accumulate to the
unique solution
z=\mathrm{acc}(b)>1
of the following quadratic equation involving
b
:

(1.1.1)

\displaystyle z^{2}-\left(\frac{\left(3-b\right)^{2}}{1-b^{2}}-2\right)z+1=0.

Note that the coefficient of
z
in this equation is determined by the shape of the moment polytope:

1-b^{2}
is its normalized volume, while
3-b
is the affine length of its perimeter. The function
b\mapsto\mathrm{acc}(b)
is
2
-to-
1
in general and takes its minimum value

a_{\min}=3+2\sqrt{2}
at
b=1/3
, the only positive rational value of
b
that is known to admit a staircase; see Fig.
1.1
.
We say a staircase is
ascending
(resp.
descending
) if its steps have increasing (resp.
decreasing
)
z
-coordinates.

Another key point proved in
[
CG-HMP
]
is that when
H_{b}
has a staircase, there is no obstruction at its accumulation point, i.e.

(1.1.2)

\displaystyle c_{H_{b}}(\mathrm{acc}(b))=V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b)).

More generally, a pair
(z,b)
with
z=\mathrm{acc}(b)
such that
c_{H_{b}}(\mathrm{acc}(b))=V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))
is called
unobstructed
; otherwise
(z,b)
(or simply
z
or
b
) is said to be
blocked
.

Here is our main result.

Theorem 1.1.1
.

(i)

The set

{\it Block}:=\bigl\{b\in[0,1)\ \big|\ c_{H_{b}}(\mathrm{acc}(b))>V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))\bigr\},

is an open dense subset of
[0,1)
that is invariant under the action of the symmetries defined below.

(ii)

All other
b
-values, except possibly for those where
\mathrm{acc}(b)
is one of the special rational points

(6,35/6,204/35,\dots
in (
1.1.3
) below, admit staircases. If
b\neq 1/3
is not an endpoint of a connected component of
{\it Block}
,
then
b
admits both an ascending and a descending staircase; while if
b
is an endpoint of a connected component of
{\it Block}
, then
b
admits either an ascending or descending staircase with steps lying outside the corresponding blocked
z
-interval.

(iii)

For
n\geq 0
define

\displaystyle{\it Block}_{[2n+6,2n+8]}:=\bigl\{b\in{\it Block}\ \big|\ \mathrm{acc}(b)\in[2n+6,2n+8],\ b>1/3\bigr\}.

For each
n\geq 0
there is a homeomorphism of
{\it Block}_{[2n+6,2n+8]}
onto the complement
[-1,2]{\smallsetminus}C
of the middle third Cantor set
C\subset[0,1]
.

(iv)

The only rational numbers
z=p/q
that might be staircase accumulation points are the special rational points. Any
such staircase would have to be descending.

The
symmetries
studied in

[
BHM
,
MM
]
are an
integral part of the structure of the set
Block
and its disjoint counterpart

{\it Stair}:=\bigl\{b\in[0,1)\ \big|\ H_{b}\mbox{ has a staircase}\bigr\}.

These symmetries stem from the arithmetic properties of the quadratic function (
1.1.1
), and their existence reduces the problem of calculating
Block
and
Stair
to calculating the restriction of these sets to
b\in[5/11,1)
, in other words to
b
with
\mathrm{acc}(b)=z>7
. The sequence of points

(1.1.3)

\displaystyle z=6,\ 35/6,\ 204/35,\dots

given by the images of
z=6
under repeated applications of the shift symmetry
S:p/q\mapsto(6p-q)/p
play a special role. These are called
the special rational points
, as are
the
b
-values that correspond to these points via the function
b\mapsto\mathrm{acc}(b)
.
These
b
-values
are also rational by
[
MM
, Lem.2.1.1]
, and
are described more fully in
equation (
2.3.9
). A definition of the symmetries can be found in §
2.3
.
They are generated by the shift
S
and a reflection
R
.

The proof of Theorem
1.1.1
(i) and (iii) is completed in §
4.2
, while those of (ii) and (iv) are completed in §
3.2
and §
4.3
respectively. Since this work, the further developments in
[
MPW
]
finish the cases of Theorem
1.1.1
(ii) and (iv), thus giving a complete computation of
Stair
. We also expect Theorem
1.1.1
to generalize to other toric domains. Evidence of this has been seen in the case of the polydisk in Farley–Holm–Magill et al.
[
FHM
]
and
[
U
]
, and a two-fold blowup of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
in Magill
[
M2
]
. See Remark
1.2.10
for more details.

The main work of the paper lies in constructing the set
Block
and in
computing infinitely many values of
c_{b}(z)
for each
b
claimed to be in
Stair
. As explained in detail in §
1.2
, specific homology classes in various blowups of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, called
exceptional classes
and denoted
{\bf{E}}
, give lower bounds
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)\leq c_{b}(z)
for the embedding function. These obstructions
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)
vary continuously in
b
and
z.
We find particular exceptional classes
{\bf{E}}
called
perfect blocking classes
such that there is a maximal interval
J_{\bf{E}}\subset[0,1)
where for all
b\in J_{\bf{E}},

V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))<\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z).

Thus, because
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)
is a lower bound of
c_{b}(z)
, it follows from (
1.1.2
) that
J_{\bf{E}}\subset{\it Block}
.

We calculate
Block
by finding all such perfect blocking classes; see Proposition
1.2.2
for a precise statement.
These are built from the sequence of perfect blocking classes found in

[
BHM
, Thm.56]
.
In Section
2.1
, we organize these into triples of classes
called
generating triples
that satisfy various compatibility conditions, and then define a way to
mutate
these triples to produce new triples in a recursive structure; see Example
1.2.4
for an illustration. We then use the existence of this whole family of
perfect blocking classes to show that each

J_{\bf{B}}
forms a connected component of
Block
.
The argument here
relies on
the use by Magill
[
M1
]
of almost toric fibrations to construct
full fillings
3
3

3

that is, symplectic embeddings
{\rm int\,}E(1,\mathrm{acc}(b))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\hookrightarrow}}H_{b}

of
H_{b}
by ellipsoids
E(1,\mathrm{acc}(b))
when
b
is the lower endpoint of
J_{\bf{E}}
. Thus these values of

b
are unobstructed, and
by Lemma
3.1.4
this implies that all the classes obtained by mutation are exceptional classes. Hence, by the results of
[
MM
]
quoted in Proposition
3.1.7
, infinitely many of these classes are
live
(that is, the corresponding obstructions are visible in the
capacity function
c_{H_{b}}
) at the relevant limiting
b
value
b_{\infty}
.

Theorem
1.2.6
states how the triples are generated and how each triple corresponds to two staircases.
The structure of these triples allow us to conclude that
{\it Block}_{[2n+6,2n+8]}
is homeomorphic to the complement of the Cantor set. One crucial compatibility condition is called
adjacency
, which expresses the relation of a staircase to the perfect blocking class that blocks an interval ending at its accumulation point; see Remark
2.1.4
.

Remark 1.1.2
.

(i) Theorem
1.1.1
(iii) implies that for each
n
there is an order-preserving bijection from the centers of the steps in
[6,8]
to those in
[2n+6,2n+8]
that takes staircases with accumulation points in
[6,8]
(and
b>1/3
) to those with accumulation points in
[2n+6,2n+8]
. However, this bijection
does not seem to have a natural extension to a homeomorphism.
It is better thought of as an algebraic move (with an arithmetic description in terms of continued fractions) that is related to the process of
v
-mutation described in
[
M1
]
; see §
1.3
.

(ii) Our conjecture that no special rational point has a staircase
is related to the question of whether
b=1/3
has a descending staircase. It is shown in
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.12]
that when
b=1/3
either there is a descending staircase or
there is

\varepsilon>0
so that for
\mathrm{acc}(b)<z<\mathrm{acc}(b)+\varepsilon
the capacity function
c_{H_{b}}(z)
equals the obstruction from the class
{\bf{E}}=3L-E_{0}-2E_{1}-E_{2\dots 6}
.
This obstruction plays a special role because, by
[
BHM
, Ex.32]
and
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.7]
, it goes through the accumulation point for all special rational
b
except for
1/5
. We extend the conjecture in Theorem
1.1.1
(iv) by claiming that for all such special
b
as well as
b=1/3
the capacity function should be given by this obstruction at points just above the corresponding special rational
z
. (This result is now proven in
[
MPW
]
.)

(iii) Our staircases need not be sharp in the sense of Casals–Vianna
[
CV
]
; in other words the inner corners between the staircase steps need not lie on the volume obstruction
z\mapsto V_{b}(z)
. In fact, descending stairs with
z
-accumulation points
<6
are never sharp because of the obstruction coming from the class
{\bf{E}}=3L-E_{0}-2E_{1}-E_{2\dots 6}
; see
[
BHM
, Ex. 32 and Fig.5.3.1]
. We did not explore this property for the other staircases. However, it is known that the Fibonacci staircase is sharp while the staircase at
b=1/3
is not; see
[
CG-HMP
]
. Notice also that, although
[
M1
]
does use almost toric fibrations to construct a full filling at the lower endpoint
z_{\infty}
of a blocked
z
-interval, this full filling occurs for the corresponding
b
-value
\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(z_{\infty})
rather than at one of the
b
-values for which the blocking class is a staircase step.

This plot found in

, Fig.1.1.4]
shows the location of the accumulation point
(z,\lambda)=(\mathrm{acc}(b),V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b)))
for
0\leq b<1
.
The blue point with
b=0
is at
(\tau^{4},\tau^{2})
and is the accumulation point for the Fibonacci stairs defined in

. The green point
with
z=3+2\sqrt{2}=:a_{\min},b=1/3

H_{1/3}
, and is the minimum of the function
b\mapsto\mathrm{acc}(b)
.

is the place where
V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))
takes its minimum.

1.2.
Further results

After summarizing some definitions and foundational results from
[
BHM
,
MM
]
, we state the other main results of this paper.
Note that there are two approaches to calculating
c_{H_{b}}
. One is to work with ECH capacities as in
[
CG-HMP
]
, which corresponds to identifying the
J
-holomorphic curves in
X\setminus E(1,z)
counted by the ECH cobordism map. Here, we instead work with closed curves in a blowup of
H_{b}
, which in many cases neck-stretch to the ECH curves (see
[
CGHM
]
and §
4.4
).

By
[
M
]
, an ellipsoid
E(1,z)
with rational eccentricity
z=p/q
embeds into
{\lambda}H_{b}
if and only if a certain finite collection
\sqcup_{i=1}^{n}B^{4}(w_{i})
of balls embeds into

{\lambda}H_{b}
. This sequence
(w_{i})
is called the
weight expansion
of
p/q
, see (
A.7
).
By
[
MP
]
, the embedding of balls exists if and only if there is a symplectic form
{\omega}
on the
n
-fold blowup
H_{b}\#n\overline{\mathbb{CP}}\,\!^{2}=\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#(n+1)\overline{\mathbb{CP}}\,\!^{2}
of
H_{b}
that takes the value
{\lambda}
on the line,
{\lambda}b
on the exceptional sphere
E_{0}
of
H_{b}
,
4
4

4

This is the embedded
2
-sphere with self-intersection
-1
obtained by
blowing
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
up once, that is by removing an open
4
-ball and collapsing its boundary to a
2
-sphere via the Hopf map;
see
[
MS
, Ch7.1]
for more detail.

and
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}

on the other exceptional spheres
E_{1},\dots,E_{n}
. Thus
{\omega}
should lie in the class
{\alpha}
where
{\alpha}(L)={\lambda},{\alpha}(E_{0})={\lambda}b,{\alpha}(E_{i})=w_{i}
.
As explained more fully in
[
M
]
, it follows from

[
Li
]
that a class
{\alpha}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#(n+1)\overline{\mathbb{CP}}\,\!^{2})
has a symplectic representative if and only if

-

the volume is positive:
{\alpha}^{2}>0
,

-

the integral
{\alpha}({\bf{E}})
of
{\alpha}
over every exceptional class
{\bf{E}}
in

\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#(n+1)\overline{\mathbb{CP}}\,\!^{2}
is positive.

Thus the significant constraints on the class
{\alpha}
come from the
exceptional classes
, which are defined to be the set of elements in
H_{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#(n+1)\overline{\mathbb{CP}}\,\!^{2})

that are represented by symplectically embedded spheres of self-intersection
-1
.
These classes,
denoted by

{\bf{E}}=dL-mE_{0}-{\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}}m_{i}E_{i},

are characterized by the fact that they
satisfy the
Diophantine equations

(1.2.1)

3d-m=\sum_{i}m_{i}+1\mbox{ and }d^{2}-m^{2}=\sum_{i}m_{i}^{2}-1,

(these record certain restrictions on their first Chern class and self-intersection), and
also reduce correctly under Cremona moves; see
[
McS
, Def.1.2.11ff]
.

Since
{\alpha}({\bf{E}})=(d-mb){\lambda}-\sum m_{i}w_{i}
,
the condition
\alpha({\bf{E}})>0
implies that each exceptional class
{\bf{E}}
with center
p/q

determines
an
obstruction

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q):=\frac{\sum m_{i}w_{i}}{d-bm}

such that
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)\leq{\lambda}
.
Therefore,

c_{H_{b}}(p/q)\geq\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)\mbox{ for all exceptional }{\bf{E}}.

These obstruction functions extend to nearby
z

and, as in (
1.2.2
) below,
are piecewise linear. Moreover, the
above discussion implies that

c_{H_{b}}(z)=\sup_{{\bf{E}}\text{ exceptional}}\{\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z),V_{H_{b}}(z)\}.

We say that an exceptional class
{\bf{E}}
is
live at
z,b
if it achieves this supremum, i.e.
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)=c_{H_{b}}(z)
. Further,
{\bf{E}}
is called
obstructive
at
z,b
if

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)>V_{b}(z)
.

It turns out that the most relevant obstructions are given by exceptional classes
{\bf{E}}
whose coefficients

\mathbf{m}=(m_{i})

equal the integral weight expansion

(q,\dots)
of some rational number
p/q
. We call such classes
perfect classes
and say that
p/q
is their
center
. If such a class is only known to satisfy the Diophantine equations, we say the class is
quasi-perfect
.

If
{\bf{E}}
is quasi-perfect,
[
BHM
, Lem.16]
shows that the obstruction function
5
5

5

Although we call this the obstruction function, we only know that

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)\leq c_{H_{b}}(z)
for perfect classes
{\bf{E}}
.

in the neighborhood of
p/q
for which
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)>V_{H_{b}}(z)
is given by the formula

(1.2.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)=\frac{qz}{d-mb},\quad z\leq p/q,\qquad\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)=\frac{p}{d-mb},\quad z\geq p/q.

These obstructions are illustrated in
Fig.
1.2
.
These functions are piecewise linear with break point (i.e. nonsmooth point) at the center
z=p/q
. But note that until we know that a given quasi-perfect class
{\bf{E}}
is in fact perfect, we cannot claim that
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)\leq c_{H_{b}}(z)
.
Our staircases are formed by infinite sequences of perfect classes whose break points
z_{n}
(often called
step centers
or simply
steps
) converge.
Here are the key facts about these classes.

Lemma 1.2.1
.

(i)

If
{\bf{E}}
is perfect,
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}
is live at its center
z=p/q
(i.e.
c_{H_{b}}(p/q)=\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)
) when
b\approx m/d
.

(ii)

If
{\bf{E}}
is quasi-perfect,
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}
is obstructive at
z=p/q
iff
|bd-m|<\sqrt{1-b^{2}}
.

Proof.

The claim in (i) is proved in
[
BHM
, Prop.21]
; (ii) is proved in
[
BHM
, Lem.15]
.
∎

We observed in
[
MM
, §2.2]
that the degree coordinates of all quasi-perfect classes have the following more precise form

(1.2.3)

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{8}\bigl(3(p+q)+\varepsilon t\bigr),\quad m=\tfrac{1}{8}\bigl(p+q+3\varepsilon t\bigr),\quad\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\},

where the positive integer
t
is defined by
t:=\sqrt{p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}+8}
, and
\varepsilon=1
if and only if
m/d>1/3
.
6
6

6

No perfect class has
m/d=1/3
by
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.13]
.
For example if
p/q=[7;4]=\frac{29}{4}
then
{\bf{m}}=(4^{\times 7},1^{\times 4})
,
t=13
and
(d,m)=(14,9)
.
Thus we often describe a quasi-perfect class by an ordered subset of the ordered
6
-tuple
(d,m,p,q,t,\varepsilon)
. We will also use the notation
{\bf{E}}_{CF(p/q)}
, where
CF
refers to the continued fraction. Conflating the notions of perfect classes, centers, and step centers, we will also refer to quasi-perfect classes as steps.

By
[
MM
, Prop.2.2.9]
, a quasi-perfect class
{\bf{E}}
with center
7
7

7

Note that
3+2\sqrt{2}
is the minimum value of
b\mapsto\mathrm{acc}(b)
.

p/q>3+2\sqrt{2}=:a_{\min}
is always a
blocking class
, i.e. the corresponding obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)

is always nontrivial at its center point
z=p/q
when
b=\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(p/q)
.
8
8

8

Here we take the branch of the inverse
\mathrm{acc}^{-1}
given by the value of
\varepsilon
; thus

\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(p/q)>1/3
exactly if
m/d>1/3
.

This implies that the interval

J_{{\bf{E}}}:=\bigl\{b\ \big|\ \mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))>V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b)\bigr\}

is an open neighborhood of
\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(p/q)
. We
call this the
b
-blocked interval
of
{\bf{E}}
, and
define
{\partial}^{+}(J_{\bf{E}}):=\sup J_{\bf{E}}
and
{\partial}^{-}(J_{\bf{E}}):=\inf J_{\bf{E}}.
The interval
I_{\bf{E}}:=\mathrm{acc}(J_{\bf{E}})
is the corresponding
z
-blocked interval
.

We prove the following result in §
4.2
.

Proposition 1.2.2
.

{\it Block}\subset[0,1)
is the disjoint union of the intervals
J_{{\bf{E}}}
as
{\bf{E}}
ranges over the set of all perfect classes with centers
>a_{\min}=3+2\sqrt{2}
.

Remark 1.2.3
.

The proof of Proposition
1.2.2
allows us to find all perfect classes with center
>a_{\min}
; see Corollary
4.2.3
. In Lemma
4.3.1
, we show that the only perfect classes with centers
<a_{\min}
are those appearing in the staircase of
c_{H_{1/3}}(z)
.
{\Diamond}

As noted in Theorem
1.1.1
, when
{\bf{E}}
is perfect and with center
>a_{\min}
the endpoints of
J_{\bf{E}}
admit staircases. We now give an example of how the structure of the blocking classes relate to the staircases at the endpoints.

Figure 1.2.
We have depicted part of three staircases to illustrate Example
1.2.4
: the descending staircase with
b=\partial^{+}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{[6]}})
and the ascending staircases with
b=\partial^{-}(J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}})
and
b=\partial^{-}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}})
. The orange curve is the volume obstruction and steps of the same color are given by the obstruction from the same perfect class, labeled by the continued fraction of its center. Note we only prove that the embedding function equals the solid lines on a neighborhood of the nonsmooth point, and we are not claiming that between those intervals there are no other obstructions (in fact, we know that there are).

Example 1.2.4
.

This example is visualized in Figure
1.2
. As proved in
[
BHM
]
, there are two perfect classes
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}=(d,m,p,q)=(3,2,6,1)
with
t=3
and
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}=(4,3,8,1)
with
t=5
that both block an interval of
b
-values. The
b
-values at the left (resp. right) endpoint of these intervals have ascending (resp. descending) staircases. Let
b^{u}_{[6]}:={\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}})
and
b^{\ell}_{[8]}:={\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}}).

For each of these
b
-values, there is an infinite sequence of perfect classes
({\bf{E}}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
, which are shown in
[
BHM
]
to be live for
c_{b}(z)
. The centers of these classes
p_{k}/q_{k}
are determined by a recursion parameter
\nu
and initial conditions

p_{0}/q_{0},p_{1}/q_{1}
such that for
x=p,q
:

x_{k+1}=\nu x_{k}-x_{k-1}.

For the staircase at
b^{u}_{[6]}
(which is called

{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0}
in
[
BHM
]
), the initial conditions are
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}
with
\nu=3
(note
\nu=3
is the
t
-parameter of
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
);
and for the staircase at
b^{\ell}_{[8]}
(called
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,1}
in
[
BHM
]
) the initial conditions are

{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}
with
\nu=5,
(note
\nu=5
is the
t
-parameter of
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}
). The obstructive functions are live and have a nonsmooth point at the center
z=p/q.
See Figure
1.2
to visualize this.

Key features of these staircases are:

•

The step centers
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]},\dots
of
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0}
decrease to
\mathrm{acc}(b^{u}_{[6]})
. When
b=b^{u}_{[6]}
, the class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
is live to the left of the accumulation point.

•

The step centers
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]},\dots
of
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,1}
increase to
\mathrm{acc}(b^{\ell}_{[8]})
. When
b=b^{\ell}_{[8]}
, the class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}
is live to the right of the accumulation point.

•

The staircases share the step
{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}
.

The features can be described using “adjacency” and “
t
-compatibility” properties. When such classes satisfy these properties, we say they form a generating triple and notate this as

\mathcal{T}:=({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1})
. These properties are defined and discussed in §
2.1
.
This triple contains all of the necessary information to prove there are staircases at
b^{u}_{[6]}
and
b^{\ell}_{[8]}
. For the notation in Theorem
1.2.6
, we let
{\mathcal{S}}^{\ell}_{\mathcal{T}}:={\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,1}
(resp.
{\mathcal{S}}^{u}_{\mathcal{T}}:={\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0}
) denote the staircase at
b^{\ell}_{[8]}
(resp.
b^{u}_{[6]}
).

It turns out that the shared step
{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}
is also a blocking class, and by using the formulas for the
x
-mutation and
y
-mutation defined above Proposition
2.1.9
, we can mutate the triple to get two new generating triples:

x\mathcal{T}:=({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{[7;5,2]},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]})\mbox{ and }y\mathcal{T}:=({\bf{E}}_{[7;4]},{\bf{E}}_{[7;3,6]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}).

Here, the middle entry
{\bf{E}}_{[7;5,2]}
in
x\mathcal{T}
is the third step in the staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0}
at
b^{u}_{[6]}
, while the middle entry
{\bf{E}}_{[7;3,6]}
in
y\mathcal{T}
is the third step in the staircase at

b^{\ell}_{[8]}
.
Just as before, each of these new triples determines two staircases
that share one step,
and this allows us to propagate each triple to two new triples. This process continues forever.

Finally, we explain the effect of the symmetries. Applying the shift
S:p/q\mapsto(6p-q)/p
to the centers of each of the classes in the triple
\mathcal{T}=({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1})
gives three new classes:
S^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}):=(S^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}),S^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}),S^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}))
. In fact,
S^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T})
is a generating triple as the symmetry preserves the compatibility conditions of a triple. As described above, this generating triple has two associated staircases, which were first shown to be live in
[
BHM
, Cor.60(iii)]
. By repeatedly applying the shift, the triples
(S^{i})^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T})
are also generating triples for all
i\geq 0
. There is another symmetry, the reflection
R
, that also sends generating triples to generating triples,
but this relationship is a little more complicated to explain since
R
is not globally defined and acts on
z
by an order reversing transformation; see §
2.3
for more details.
{\Diamond}

We now make some definitions which are generalizations from the example to state Theorem
1.2.6
.

Definition 1.2.5
.

A sequence of quasi-perfect classes
{\bf{E}}_{k}=\bigl((d_{k},m_{k},p_{k},q_{k})\bigr)_{k\geq 0}
is said to form a
pre-staircase
if there are numbers
z_{\infty}>1
and
b_{\infty}\in[0,1)
such that

p_{k}/q_{k}\to z_{\infty}
and
m_{k}/d_{k}\to b_{\infty}
. The pre-staircase is said to be
live
if, for some
k_{0}\geq 0
, the obstructions from the classes
{\bf{E}}_{k},k\geq k_{0},
are live at
b_{\infty}
, so that they
form a staircase in
c_{H_{b_{\infty}}}
.

Thus a staircase is a live pre-staircase.
A pre-staircase is called
fake
if its classes are known not to be perfect; for examples
see
Remark
2.1.15
. As noted in Lemma
2.1.16
,
z_{\infty}=\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})
for any pre-staircase.

As explained in Example
1.2.4
, the staircases found it
[
BHM
,
MM
]
consist of a sequence
{\mathcal{S}}:=\big({\bf{E}}_{k}\bigr)_{k\geq 0}
of perfect classes (with
\varepsilon
constant) whose coefficients
x_{k}:=p_{k},q_{k},d_{k},m_{k},t_{k}
satisfy a recursion of the form

x_{k+1}=\nu x_{k}-x_{k-1},\quad k\geq 1,\;\;\nu\geq 3.

Thus the steps are determined by the two initial classes (called
seeds
) together with the recursion coefficient
\nu
. We proved that these pre-staircases are live for
b:=b_{\infty}=\lim_{k}m_{k}/d_{k}
and have accumulation point
\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty}):=z_{\infty}=\lim_{k}p_{k}/q_{k}
.
Finally, it turned out that each staircase
{\mathcal{S}}
is
associated to a blocking class

{\bf{B}}
with blocked
b
-interval
J_{\bf{B}}
and corresponding blocked
z
-interval
I_{{\bf{B}}}=\mathrm{acc}(J_{\bf{B}})
in the following way:

-

for an ascending staircase, the limit point
(b_{\infty},z_{\infty})
has
z_{\infty}
equal to the lower endpoint
{\partial}^{-}(I_{\bf{B}})
(so that, if
m/d>1/3
,
b_{\infty}={\partial}^{-}(J_{\bf{B}})
, while if
m/d<1/3
,
b_{\infty}={\partial}^{+}(J_{\bf{B}})
);

-

for a descending staircase, the limit point
(b_{\infty},z_{\infty})
has
z_{\infty}
equal to the upper endpoint
{\partial}^{+}(I_{\bf{B}})
(so that, if
m/d>1/3
,
b_{\infty}={\partial}^{+}(J_{\bf{B}})
, while if
m/d<1/3
,
b_{\infty}={\partial}^{-}(J_{\bf{B}})
);

-

the recursion parameter
\nu
of the staircase equals the

t
-coordinate of
{\bf{B}}
.

Here
{\partial}^{+}
, resp.
{\partial}^{-}
, denotes the supremum (resp. infimum) of an open interval
J_{\bf{B}}
or
I_{\bf{B}}
. Notice that if
J_{\bf{B}}\subset(1/3,1)
then
\mathrm{acc}
sends
{\partial}^{+}(J_{\bf{B}})
to
{\partial}^{+}(I_{\bf{B}})
and
{\partial}^{-}(J_{\bf{B}})
to
{\partial}^{-}(I_{\bf{B}})
; otherwise it switches them. (For no
{\bf{B}}
do we have
J_{\bf{B}}\ni 1/3
because
c_{H_{1/3}}
has a staircase
[
CG-HMP
]
).

In
[
MM
, Cor.3.2.3]
we defined the
staircase family

{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
to consist of the blocking classes
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}:=(n+3,n+2,2n+6,1,2n+3)
, for
n\geq 0
, together with the two seeds
9
9

9

These seeds satisfy the conditions in (
1.2.3
) and should be considered as formally perfect, though clearly
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed}
does not correspond to a geometric class; see also Remark
2.1.14
.

{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed}:=(1,1,1,1,2)
,

{\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed}:=(-2,0,-5,-1,2)
and the associated staircases:

(1.2.4)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\mbox{ for each $n\geq 1$, the ascending staircases ${\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n}$ with seeds
${\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n-1}$};

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\mbox{ for each $n\geq 0$, the descending staircases ${\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n}$ with seeds
${\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1}$}.

As observed in
[
MM
, Rmk.3.2.4(ii)]
for each
n
the staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n+1}
share exactly one step
{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7;2n+4]}
with center at
[2n+7;2n+4]
. This is the next step after the two seeds. These are the classes seen in Example
1.2.4
for
n=0
and
n=1
.

The main result in
[
MM
]
was that there is a set of symmetries
S^{i}R^{\delta},i\geq 0,{\delta}\in\{0,1\}
that act on
z=p/q

by fractional linear transformations and fix
t
, so that the action extends to the
d,m
coordinates of perfect classes.
Here
S
is the shift
p/q\mapsto(p-6q)/q
that fixes the point
a_{\min}=3+2\sqrt{2}
, and
R
is the reflection
p/q\mapsto(6p-35q)/(p-6q)
that fixes
7
. Both
S
and
R
change the sign of
\varepsilon
.
We showed that the image of
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
under each of these transformations
T
is another staircase family; in particular
T
takes blocking classes to blocking classes, and staircases to staircases.
(For example, the reflection
R
takes the descending stairs
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0}
to the Fibonacci stairs in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}=H_{0}
; see
[
MM
, Rmk.3.2.4,Cor.3.2.7]
.) This reduces the study of staircases to those with
6<\mathrm{acc}(b)
and
b>1/3
.

Figure 1.3.
This schematic figure shows the graph of the function
z\mapsto V_{b}(z)
together with the
obstruction functions given by a triple
\mathcal{T}
and its left and right mutations for some appropriate value of
b
.

A central result of the current paper can be stated as follows; the required definitions
and the proof of (i), (ii) may be found in §
2.1
and §
2.3
, while (iii) is proved in §
4.2
.

Theorem 1.2.6
.

(i)

Each triple
10
10

10

Here the subscripts
{\lambda},\mu,\rho
stand for ‘left’, ‘middle’ and ‘right’.
of classes

\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}:=\bigl({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho}\bigr):=\bigl({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7;2n+4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1}\bigr)

is a generating triple and generates two other triples called its
left and right mutations

x\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}:=\bigl({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{x\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}\bigr),\qquad y\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}:=\bigl({\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{y\mu}{\bf{E}}_{\rho}\bigr),

which also are generating triples, and hence can be mutated further to form new triples
\mathcal{T}
.
Moreover, each such triple
\mathcal{T}
determines two staircases,

-

an ascending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
with seeds

{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
and blocking class
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
,

-

a descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
with seeds

{\bf{E}}_{\rho},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
and blocking class
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
.

(ii)

The symmetries act on these triples. Moreover the symmetries are compatible with mutation. More precisely, if a symmetry takes
\mathcal{T}
to
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and preserves (resp. reverses) the order on the
z
-axis, then it takes
x\mathcal{T},y\mathcal{T}
to
x\mathcal{T}^{\prime},y\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
(resp.
y\mathcal{T}^{\prime},x\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
).

(iii)

Every perfect class with center
>a_{\min}
is a step in at least one ascending and one descending staircase that is the image under an appropriate symmetry of one of the staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
in (i) above.

In view of this result, we make the following definition.

Definition 1.2.7
.

The
complete family

{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
consists of the staircase family
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
defined as above, plus all staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
determined as in Lemma
2.1.12
by any triple

\mathcal{T}
derived from one of the basic triples
\bigl({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7;2n+4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1}\bigr)
.
Any staircase that is the image of

{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
or
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
under a symmetry
S^{i}R^{\delta}
, where
i\geq 0,{\delta}\in\{0,1\}
, is called a
principal staircase.

Theorem
1.2.6
parts (ii) and (iii) imply that each perfect class with center
>a_{\min}
belongs to a unique family
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
. We will see later that the principal staircases are distinguished from those described in Remark
1.2.8
(ii) below by the fact that each is recursively defined and has a blocking class.

Remark 1.2.8
.

(i)
Instead of thinking of the complete family as a set of interconnected staircases, we can think of it as a countable family of classes ordered according to the position of their centers, labelled by ternary decimals; for details see §
2.2
. As explained in
[
M1
]
, one can
associate to each triple
\mathcal{T}
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
an almost toric fibration whose base diagram is a quadrilateral
Q_{\mathcal{T}}
in such a way that
there is direct correspondence between the left/right derived triples
x\mathcal{T},y\mathcal{T}
and two of the possible mutations of
Q_{\mathcal{T}}
. This fact is a key step in the proof that the tuples
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q,t)
we consider do in fact correspond to perfect classes that are represented by exceptional spheres.

(ii)
(Further staircases)

Once we give finite decimal labels to the classes in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
, it becomes clear that they can be organized into different convergent sequences, one for each infinite ternary decimal whose expansion does not contain the digit
1
. For a precise description, see Definition
2.2.7
. The principal staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
described above are those that exist at the endpoints of the intervals in
Block
, while these new staircases correspond to all of the uncountably many other unblocked
b
-values in the Cantor-type set
[0,1){\smallsetminus}{\it Block}
except the special rational points.
We will prove in §
3.2
that all the steps in the new ascending pre-staircases are live for the limiting
b
-value, while in the descending case all but finitely many are live for the limiting
b
-value.

{\Diamond}

Remark 1.2.9
.

(Properties of Staircases)

(i) The capacity functions
c_{H_{b}}(z),b\in[0,1),
are not entirely determined by perfect classes, since there
are other obstructions given by exceptional classes that are not perfect. For instance,
the exceptional class
B=6L-3E_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{7}2E_{i}
is not a perfect class even though the coefficients of the
E_{i}
are multiples of the weights of
7
. One can check that this class is live at
z=7
for some values of
b
. Furthermore, it is a blocking class, but the
b
-interval that it blocks is contained in the interval blocked by the perfect class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}=3L-2E_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{6}E_{i}
. See Remark
4.2.2
for more discussion. Because it is not clear how non-perfect exceptional classes relate to the symmetries, we cannot claim that the capacity functions themselves are invariant under the symmetries, but only that the images of the perfect staircase classes remain live. This question can also phrased in terms of ECH capacities: is it possible to understand the action of the symmetries from the point of view of ECH capacities?

(ii) It turns out that if
H_{b}
has a staircase then it has one whose steps are given by perfect classes, as long as
b
is not a special rational point. Moreover, if
b
is blocked, it is blocked by a perfect class. As we show in Proposition
4.4.3
, the
argument in
[
CGHM
]
then applies to show that such staircases stabilize, at least to dimension
6
. Thus the function

c_{H_{b},1}(z)=\inf\ \bigl\{{\lambda}\ \Big|\ E(1,z)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\lambda}H_{b}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\bigr\}

has a staircase for each
b\notin{\it Block}
that is not one of the special rational points, and has no staircase if
b\in{\it Block}
. It would be interesting to explore how this fits in with the stabilized folding construction in
[
Hi
,
CGHS
]
for example. In the case of the ball or the monotone polydisk, the staircases ascend and the stabilized embedding function is conjectured to exhibit a ‘phase change’ at the accumulation point of the staircase. For example in the case of the ball
it is conjectured to agree with the folding curve
z\mapsto\frac{3z}{1+z}
for
z>\tau^{4}
. Notice that in this case the folding curve crosses the volume constraint precisely at the accumulation point. However, there are some

H_{b}
with descending stairs that stabilize, and so something rather different must happen in this case.
{\Diamond}

Remark 1.2.10
.

(Further developments and possible generalizations)

(i) Since this paper was first posted, there have been further developments about the Hirzebruch surface. In
[
MPW
]
, the authors prove that
b=1/3
admits only an ascending staircase and that the special rational points do not have descending infinite staircases. These points complete the leftover cases of Theorem
1.1.1
(ii) and (iv). Furthermore, they prove the only quadratic irrational numbers that might be staircase accumulation points are the endpoints of connected components of
Block
. By the fact that the accumulation point function is the root of a quadratic equation, this proves that
b=0,1/3
are the only rational values of
b
with infinite staircases, proving
[
CG-HMP
, Conj. 1.20]
for Hirzebruch surfaces.

(ii) The connections between the Hirzebruch surface and the polydisk have also become more clear. In
[
FHM
]
following work of
[
U
]
, the authors provide evidence that the structure of Theorem
1.1.1
also occurs for
S^{2}(1)\times S^{2}(\beta)
. In
[
U
]
, Usher considered ellipsoid embeddings into the polydisk
P(1,b)
(which by
[
CG-HMP
, Thm 1.4]
is equivalent to
S^{2}(1)\times S^{2}(\beta)
). While he did not use the language of blocking classes, we have verified that the staircases that he found do lie at the endpoints of blocked intervals determined by perfect classes. Looking at a few examples, it seems that the other endpoint of the blocked interval has a corresponding descending staircase. (Note that Usher only considered ascending staircases.) He also found symmetries arising from the relevant Diophantine equation that are closely related to those of
[
MM
]
that are discussed in §
2.3
below. The further work about the polydisk in
[
FHM
]
suggests:

•

Given a staircase in
c_{H_{b}}
,
there should be an infinite staircase for
S^{2}(1)\times S^{2}(\beta)
, where

\beta=\mathrm{acc}_{S^{2}}^{-1}\circ CF^{-}\circ\mathrm{acc}.

Here
\mathrm{acc}_{S^{2}}^{-1}
denotes the accumulation point function for
S^{2}(1)\times S^{2}(\beta)
and
CF^{-}
denotes the operation which sends a number with continued fraction
[s_{0},\dots,s_{n}]
to
[s_{0}-1,\dots,s_{n}-1]
.

•

In the cases that he studied, Usher conjectured in
[
U
, Conj. 4.23]
a complete description of the ellipsoidal capacity function of
S^{2}(1)\times S^{2}(\beta)
below the accumulation point.
The method of proof in
[
FHM
]
via almost toric mutations suggests that
this conjecture is true, and that a similar description holds for the ascending principal staircases of
H_{b}
.

This provides evidence that the work done here to find all perfect classes is easily generalizable to the case of the polydisk.

(iii)
In
[
M2
]
, a similar structure is found for
embeddings into
X_{a,b}:=

\mathbb{CP}^{2}(1)\#{\overline{{\mathbb{C}}P}}\!\,^{2}(a)\#{\overline{{\mathbb{C}}P}}\!\,^{2}(b)
where
0<a\leq b
and
a+b<1
. In this example, rather than having blocked intervals, there are blocked regions of
a,b
values, and staircases have been found on their boundaries. Now, the regions blocked by two different perfect classes may intersect, in contrast with the disjointness found in Proposition
4.2.1
. Thus, the curve that bounds a particular blocked region is not entirely made up of
b
-values with staircases: indeed it seems that the set of such parameter values with staircases has much the same structure that we find for
z
-intervals such as
[6,8]
. Preliminary work suggests that there is an analogous definition of a generating triple, which we can mutate to find more generating triples resulting in a similar fractal of staircases.

(iv) More generally, we expect our results to generalize to the examples in
[
CG-HMP
]
that compactify to a
k
-fold blowup of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, where
k\leq 4
and we allow for irrational blowups.
Simple extensions of these situations are still mysterious. It remains unclear if there are any staircases in a five-fold blowup of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}

or in a convex toric domain such as an irrational ellipsoid
E(1,x),x\in\mathbb{R}{\smallsetminus}\mathbb{Q},
with irrational normal vectors. For forthcoming work on these questions, see Cristofaro-Gardiner–Magill–McDuff
[
CGMM
]
.

(v) It would also be interesting to know whether echoes of the combinatorial structures discovered here can be perceived in other situations. For example, Casals–Vianna develop in
[
CV
]
another approach to some of the staircases considered in
[
CG-HMP
]
via almost toric fibrations, emphasizing the relation to tropical geometry and quiver combinatorics. Even though we are not working in a monotone manifold, it is possible that the structure of the generating triples developed here, with their tight connections to
the almost toric fibrations in
[
M1
]
, have repercussions in these areas.

{\Diamond}

1.3.
Nature of the proofs

The proofs involve two kinds of arguments; algebraic/arithmetic to show that the classes
{\bf{E}}
do satisfy all the required compatibility conditions, and geometric to show that the corresponding obstruction functions
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}
are live for the appropriate
b
-value. The point here is that a quasi-perfect class
(d,m,p,q,t)
may not have
{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}\geq 0
for every class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}\neq{\bf{E}}
that is represented by an exceptional sphere; see Corollary
3.1.3
. If
{\bf{E}}
fails this test,
11
11

11

Although there certainly are plenty of fake classes (i.e. quasi-perfect classes that are not perfect),
Remarks
2.1.15
and
2.3.8
give intriguing hints that one might be able to express the distinction in purely arithmetic terms. For example, there may be no set of (integral) fake classes that satisfy all the compatibility conditions required of a generating triple.

then although the function

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}
is obstructive at its center
p/q
for
b=m/d
(i.e.
\mu_{{\bf{E}},m/d}(z)>c_{H_{m/d}}(p/q)
) there is no guarantee that

(a)

it gives the maximal obstruction at
b=m/d,z=p/q
, or that

(b)

it still gives the maximal obstruction at
z=p/q
when
b=b_{\infty}
, the limiting
b
-value of the
proposed staircase.

In fact, once we know (a) for all its steps,
then (at least in the case of the principal pre-staircases) results that can be quoted from
[
MM
]
imply that
the pre-staircase is live provided that
the slope estimate (
3.1.4
) holds.
Moreover, by Lemma
1.2.1
we can establish (a) by showing that
{\bf{E}}
is perfect.
The standard way to do this
is to show as in
[
BHM
,
MM
]
that
its components reduce to those of a known exceptional class under Cremona transformations. It turns out (see
[
MM
, §4.1]
) that the symmetries act very nicely on these components, so that it suffices to prove this for the staircases in the family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
. This was done by hand in
[
BHM
]
for the staircases
with blocking classes
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},n\geq 0
.
However, this approach seems impractical in the current context, since the coefficients of the staircase classes in the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
increase so rapidly and the reduction process does not seem to behave nicely under mutations.

Instead we first develop a more efficient way to determine when a quasi-perfect class in the complete family

{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
is perfect. Namely, we show in Lemma
3.1.4
that a class
{\bf{E}}
with
m/d>1/3
is perfect provided that the lower endpoint
z^{-}_{\infty}:={\partial}^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}})
is unobstructed, i.e.

c_{H_{b^{-}_{\infty}}}(z^{-}_{\infty})=V_{b^{-}_{\infty}}(z^{-}_{\infty})
, where
\mathrm{acc}({b^{-}_{\infty}})=z^{-}_{\infty}
.
We then apply the main result in Magill
[
M1
]
which shows that these points are unobstructed by using almost toric fibrations to construct a full filling for these values of
b
and
z
.
This is possible because there is an ascending sequence of quasi-perfect classes in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
(i.e. a pre-staircase) with limit

(b^{-}_{\infty},z^{-}_{\infty})
. To do this, one starts with the toric model of
H_{b}
and performs a sequence of mutations at three of its corners. There are three possibilities here: when suitably iterated, a mutation at the corner in the first quadrant (called a
v
-mutation) corresponds to the translation
[6,8]\to[2n+6,2n+8]
,
while mutations at the points on the
x
- and
y
- axis turn out to correspond precisely to the corresponding moves for the triples
\mathcal{T}
that are described in Proposition
2.1.9
.

The upshot is that we use the existence of the whole interweaving family of quasi-perfect classes to prove inductively that all the classes in the family are in fact perfect. One then needs additional arguments to show that the staircase steps are live at the limiting
b
-value. This turns out to be straightforward in the case of the ascending staircases, but more problematic for the descending ones; see §
3.1
and §
3.2
. It is important in several places of our arguments that the limiting values
(b_{\infty},z_{\infty})
of the principal staircases are both irrational.

We thank all the other members of our original group
M. Bertozzi, T. Holm, E. Maw, G. Mwakyoma, and A. R. Pires for helping us initiate this work, as well as AWM and ICERM for providing the initial organization. Nicki Magill also thanks Tara Holm as her research advisor for introducing her to almost toric fibrations and encouraging her to use them as a proof method. We also thank the anonymous referee for very helpful comments.

2.
Description of the classes

This is the algebraic heart of the paper.
We define the notion of a generating triple in Definition
2.1.6
and show that they propagate under left/right mutations in Proposition
2.1.9
. Along with Lemma
2.1.12
, this allows us to prove Theorem
1.2.6
(i). In §
2.2
we describe the structure of the ensuing set of interwoven classes, and prove in Proposition
2.2.6
that they block a dense set of
z
-values (and hence a dense set of
b
-values). The non-recursive pre-staircases
are described in Definition
2.2.7
. Finally, we show in Proposition
2.3.2
that
the symmetries from
[
MM
]
act on the set of generating triples, and hence on the set of pre-staircases.

2.1.
Generating triples

All staircase classes
{\bf{E}}
are quasi-perfect; that is they are given by tuples
(d,m,p,q)
where

{\bf{E}}=dL-mE_{0}-\sum_{i\geq 1}m_{i}E_{i},\qquad(m_{1},m_{2},\dots)=W(p/q),

where
W(p/q)
is the integral weight expansion of
p/q
; see
[
BHM
, Ex.8]
or (
A.4
) below.
It is easy to check that if
{\bf{x}}:=(p,q,t)
satisfies the equation
p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}+8=t^{2}
and
t>0
, then the quantities
(d,m)
defined by

(2.1.1)

\displaystyle d:=\tfrac{1}{8}(3(p+q)+\varepsilon t),\quad m:=\tfrac{1}{8}((p+q)+3\varepsilon t),\quad\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}

satisfy the basic identities
3d=m+p+q
and
d^{2}-m^{2}=pq-1

that correspond to
c_{1}({\bf{E}})=1,{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}=-1
; see
[
BHM
, §2.1]

Moreover, by
[
MM
, Prop.2.2.9]
the class is uniquely determined by
p/q
. In other words, given
p,q,t
satisfying
p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}+8=t^{2}
,
the expressions for
d,m
in (
2.1.1
) are integral for at most one value of
\varepsilon
.
Thus we can think of
{\bf{E}}
as an integral point on the quadratic surface

X_{\mathbb{Z}}:=\bigl\{{\bf{x}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\ |\ {\bf{x}}^{T}A{\bf{x}}=8\bigr\},

where

A
is the symmetric matrix

\displaystyle A:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-1&3&0\\
3&-1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),\quad{\bf{x}}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}p\\
q\\
t\end{array}\right).

Such classes
{\bf{E}}

are center-blocking
12
12

12

that is,
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)>V_{b}(p/q)
when
\mathrm{acc}(b)=p/q
.

if
p/q>3+2\sqrt{2}=a_{\min}
by
[
MM
, Prop.2.2.9]
. Moreover, because the case
\varepsilon=1
corresponds to values of
m/d>1/3
while
\varepsilon=-1
corresponds to
m/d<1/3
, we often omit
\varepsilon
from the notation.
13
13

13

By
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.13]
, there are no perfect classes with
m/d=1/3
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Two quasi-perfect classes
{\bf{E}}:=(d,m,p,q,t,\varepsilon),{\bf{E}}^{\prime}:=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},t^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})

are said to be

adjacent
if
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}
and if after renaming so that
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
(if necessary),
the following relation holds:

(2.1.8)

\displaystyle(p+q)(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})-tt^{\prime}=8pq^{\prime}.

Further, they are called

t^{\prime\prime}
-compatible
if
\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{\prime}
and

(2.1.9)

\displaystyle tt^{\prime}-4t^{\prime\prime}=pp^{\prime}-3(pq^{\prime}+qp^{\prime})+qq^{\prime},\qquad\mbox{ i.e. }\ \ {\bf{x}}^{T}A{\bf{x}}^{\prime}=4t^{\prime\prime}.

We make corresponding definitions for a pair

{\bf{x}},{\bf{x}}^{\prime}\in X_{\mathbb{Z}}
to be
t^{\prime\prime}
-compatible or adjacent.
Further we write
{\bf{x}}<{\bf{x}}^{\prime}
(or
{\bf{E}}<{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
) to denote that
14
14

14

Note that this condition has nothing to do with the relative size of the ratios
p/q,p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
.

p<p^{\prime},q<q^{\prime},t<t^{\prime}
; and
say that
{\bf{x}}\in X_{\mathbb{Z}}
is
positive
if
p,q,t>0
.

Here is a useful result about the relation between adjacency and
t
-compatibility.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

(i)

Suppose that the points
{\bf{x}}_{0},{\bf{x}}_{1}\in X_{\mathbb{Z}}
are
t
-compatible for some
t\geq 3
and have
{\bf{x}}_{0}<{\bf{x}}_{1}
.
Then
{\bf{x}}_{2}:=t{\bf{x}}_{1}-{\bf{x}}_{0}
is positive and in
X_{\mathbb{Z}}
. Also,
{\bf{x}}_{1}<{\bf{x}}_{2}
and the pair
{\bf{x}}_{1},{\bf{x}}_{2}
is
t
-compatible.
Further, if
{\bf{x}}_{0},{\bf{x}}_{1}
are adjacent, so are
{\bf{x}}_{1},{\bf{x}}_{2}
.
Thus, if
{\bf{E}}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{1}
satisfy
p_{0}<p_{1},q_{0}<q_{1},t_{0}<t_{1}
and are adjacent and
t
-compatible, then so are the components of all successive pairs in the sequence obtained from
{\bf{E}}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{1}
by
t
-recursion.

(ii)

The adjacency condition for classes with
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
can also be written in the following equivalent forms

(2.1.10)

\displaystyle(3m^{\prime}-d^{\prime})d

\displaystyle=(m^{\prime}-q^{\prime})p+m^{\prime}q,\quad\mbox{ or}

\displaystyle d^{\prime}d-m^{\prime}m

\displaystyle=pq^{\prime}.

(iii)

If
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
are adjacent, then they are
t^{\prime\prime}
-compatible exactly if

|p^{\prime}q-pq^{\prime}|=t^{\prime\prime}.

Proof.

Since
A
is symmetric,

(t{\bf{x}}_{1}-{\bf{x}}_{0})^{T}A(t{\bf{x}}_{1}-{\bf{x}}_{0})=t^{2}{\bf{x}}_{1}^{T}A{\bf{x}}_{1}-2t{\bf{x}}_{1}^{T}A{\bf{x}}_{0}+{\bf{x}}_{0}^{T}A{\bf{x}}_{0}=8

exactly if
8t^{2}=2t{\bf{x}}_{1}^{T}A{\bf{x}}_{0}
, which holds by (
2.1.9
). Thus
{\bf{x}}_{2}\in X_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Further (
2.1.9
) holds for
{\bf{x}}_{2}:=t{\bf{x}}_{1}-{\bf{x}}_{0},{\bf{x}}_{1}
because

(t{\bf{x}}_{1}-{\bf{x}}_{0})^{T}A{\bf{x}}_{1}=8t-{\bf{x}}_{0}^{T}A{\bf{x}}_{1}=4t,

again by (
2.1.9
). Thus
{\bf{x}}_{1},{\bf{x}}_{2}
are
t
-compatible.

To see that they are adjacent, it is useful to introduce
15
15

15

Here we are following a suggestion of Ana Rita Pires, exhibiting
the adjacency condition as an asymmetric version of the equation
{\bf{x}}^{T}A{\bf{x}}=8
that defines the set of quasi-perfect classes.

the matrix

\displaystyle B:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-1&7&0\\
-1&-1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right).

Then
A=\frac{1}{2}(B+B^{T})
and it is easy to check the following:

-

the class represented by
{\bf{x}}:=(p,q,t)^{T}
is quasi-perfect iff
{\bf{x}}^{T}B{\bf{x}}=8
;

-

the classes represented by
{\bf{x}},{\bf{x}}^{\prime}
are adjacent iff, when ordered so that
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
we have

{\bf{x}}^{T}B{\bf{x}}^{\prime}=0
; and

-

the classes represented by
{\bf{x}},{\bf{x}}^{\prime}
are
t^{\prime\prime}
-compatible iff

{\bf{x}}^{T}(B+B^{T}){\bf{x}}^{\prime}=8t^{\prime\prime}
.

Thus
if
p_{0}/q_{0}<p_{1}/q_{1}
, the points
{\bf{x}}_{0},{\bf{x}}_{1}
are adjacent precisely if

{\bf{x}}_{0}^{T}B{\bf{x}}_{1}=0
. But then
p_{1}/q_{1}<p_{2}/q_{2}
and the adjacency condition for
{\bf{E}}_{1},{\bf{E}}_{2}
is

{\bf{x}}_{1}^{T}B{\bf{x}}_{2}=0
, which holds because

{\bf{x}}_{1}^{T}B{\bf{x}}_{2}={\bf{x}}_{1}^{T}B(t{\bf{x}}_{1}-{\bf{x}}_{0})=8t-{\bf{x}}_{1}^{T}B{\bf{x}}_{0}=8t-{\bf{x}}_{0}^{T}(B^{T}+B){\bf{x}}_{1}=0

since
{\bf{x}}_{0}<{\bf{x}}_{1}
and
{\bf{x}}_{0},{\bf{x}}_{1}
are adjacent and
t
-compatible.
This proves (i).

Now consider (ii). To see that the two conditions in (
2.1.10
) are equivalent use the relation
p+q=3d-m
to obtain

(3m^{\prime}-d^{\prime})d=(m^{\prime}-q^{\prime})p+m^{\prime}q=m^{\prime}(3d-m)-pq^{\prime},

and then simplify. Next use the formulas in (
2.1.1
) to obtain

\displaystyle 64(d^{\prime}d-m^{\prime}m)

\displaystyle=\bigl(3(p+q)+\varepsilon t\bigr)\bigl(3(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})+\varepsilon t^{\prime}\bigr)-\bigl((p+q)+3\varepsilon t\bigr)\bigl((p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})+3\varepsilon t^{\prime}\bigr)

\displaystyle=8(p+q)(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})-8tt^{\prime}.

It follows easily that (
2.1.8
) is equivalent to the conditions in (
2.1.10
).

Finally, to prove (iii), we may assume without loss of generality that
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
. Then
rewrite the LHS
tt^{\prime}-4t^{\prime\prime}
of (
2.1.9
), using the expression for
tt^{\prime}
from (
2.1.8
) and simplify.
∎

The following remarks explain the geometric significance of the
t
-compatibility and adjacency conditions.

Remark 2.1.3
.

(Pre-staircases)
(i)
If
{\bf{E}}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{1}
are
t^{\prime}
-compatible for some
t^{\prime}\geq 3
and
{\bf{E}}_{0}<{\bf{E}}_{1}
,
then by Lemma
2.1.2
the tuples obtained from them by the recursion

(2.1.11)

\displaystyle x_{k+1}=t^{\prime}x_{k}-x_{k-1},\qquad\mbox{ where }\ x\ =d,\ m,\ p,\ q,\ t

are integral, and
also satisfy the Diophantine identities;
16
16

16

The linear identity
3d-m=p+q
is automatic for any such recursively defined sequence, but the quadratic identity
d^{2}-m^{2}=pq-1
holds only under the
t
-compatibility condition; see
[
BHM
, Lem.65]
for a similar result.

moreover, all successive pairs
{\bf{E}}_{k},{\bf{E}}_{k+1}
are
t^{\prime}
-compatible. Thus, this collection of classes forms a
pre-staircase
in the sense of Definition
1.2.5
.
17
17

17

In
[
BHM
, Def.46]
, a pre-staircase is defined to
consist of a recursively defined sequence of quasi-perfect classes that satisfy a linear relation (or equivalently are all adjacent to a fixed class called its blocking class). The definition in
[
MM
, §1.2]
is very similar, though the
requirement for the linear relation is stated separately. Our current use of the word is
more general.

and we say that
{\bf{E}}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{1}
are its
seeds
.
Further, by
[
MM
, Lem.3.1.4]
,
there is
{\lambda}={\lambda}(t^{\prime})>1
such that for each
x\ =d,\ m,\ p,\ q,\ t
there are constants
X\ =D,M,P,Q,T
such that

x_{k}=X{\lambda}^{k}+\overline{X}{\lambda}^{-k}
. Here we write

X:=X^{\prime}+X^{\prime\prime}\sqrt{\sigma}\in\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\sigma})
, where
{\sigma}=(t^{\prime})^{2}-4
,
and define
\overline{X}:=X^{\prime}-X^{\prime\prime}\sqrt{\sigma}
. Moreover
X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
are determined by
x_{0},x_{1}
via the formulas

(2.1.12)

\displaystyle X^{\prime}=\frac{x_{0}}{2},\quad X^{\prime\prime}=\frac{2x_{1}-t^{\prime}x_{0}}{2{\sigma}}.

In particular this implies that the ratios
p_{k}/q_{k},m_{k}/d_{k}
converge
O({\lambda}^{-2k})
to the limits
P/Q,M/D
. Further, in the limit the Diophantine equations satisfied by
(d_{k},m_{k},p_{k},q_{k})
simplify to

(2.1.13)

\displaystyle 3D-M=P+Q,\qquad D^{2}-M^{2}=PQ.

Note also that, if
t^{\prime}\geq 3
then
{\sigma}=(t^{\prime})^{2}-4
is not a perfect square, and it follows from
[
MM
, Cor.3.1.5]

that the limits
z_{\infty}=P/Q,b_{\infty}=M/D
are irrational provided only that the sequences
p_{k}/q_{k},m_{k}/d_{k}
are not constant. This condition holds for all the sequences considered here: indeed
p_{k}/q_{k}
is always assumed to be nonconstant, while the adjacency condition (
2.1.10
) that we impose on the initial terms
{\bf{E}}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{1}
implies that
m_{k}/d_{k}
is nonconstant.

(ii)
Note that for the staircase at
b=1/3
there are three separate strands, which cannot be combined into one recursion. Each of these strands has
t\in\{1,2\}
constant and has alternating values for
\varepsilon
. Thus the definitions of adjacency and
t
-compatibility do not apply, so that this staircase is of rather different nature from the others. Furthermore,
for these strands
d,m
do not satisfy homogeneous recursions, and hence, (
2.1.11
) does not hold. Its properties are discussed in Section
4.3
. See also
[
MM
, Ex.2.3.7]
.

{\Diamond}

Remark 2.1.4
.

(The adjacency condition)
(i)
In the language of
[
MM
]
, the condition

\displaystyle(3m^{\prime}-d^{\prime})d=(m^{\prime}-q^{\prime})p+m^{\prime}q

in (
2.1.10
)
says that
{\bf{E}}
satisfies the lower (i.e. the one for ascending stairs) linear relation given by
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
.
Notice that by
[
MM
, Lem.3.3.5(ii)]
if
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
then
{\bf{E}}
satisfies the lower linear relation given by
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
iff

{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
satisfies the upper linear relation given by
{\bf{E}}
.

(ii) We saw in
[
BHM
, Prop.52]
that if all the classes
{\bf{E}}_{k}=(d_{k},m_{k},p_{k},q_{k},t_{k},\varepsilon)
in an ascending pre-staircase are adjacent to
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},t^{\prime},\varepsilon)
and if
p_{k}/q_{k}<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
for all
k

then the limit point

(b_{\infty},z_{\infty})=(\lim m_{k}/d_{k},\lim p_{k}/q_{k})=(M/D,P/Q)
has the property that
z_{\infty}=\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})
is the lower endpoint
{\partial}^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}})
of the
z
-interval blocked by
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
.
Similarly, if
{\bf{E}}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
is a descending pre-staircase with
p_{k}/q_{k}>p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
for all
k
that
consists of classes that are adjacent to
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
, then its accumulation point
P^{\prime\prime}/Q^{\prime\prime}
is the upper endpoint
{\partial}^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}})
of the
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
-blocked
z
-interval.
In each case, we call
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
the
associated blocking class
of the pre-staircase.

(iii) We will see in §
4.1
that the adjacency condition has quite different consequences as well.
In fact, if we look only at perfect classes whose centers
p/q
are not integral (i.e. if
q>1)
, then every pair of adjacent classes
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
is orthogonal with respect to the intersection pairing, i.e.
{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=0
; see Proposition
4.1.3
. This gives us information about the continued fraction expansions of the centers of the steps.

(iv) Finally, notice that the adjacency relation for the classes
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
was phrased so that the order of the pair is irrelevant, though the condition itself depends on the relation of the centers
p/q,\ p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
. However, although the
t^{\prime\prime}
-compatibility condition does not depend on the order of the inputs,
we are interested in the staircase classes generated by
t^{\prime\prime}
-recursion with the (ordered!) seeds
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
. Note that the ordering here is numerical, depending only on the size of entries in
{\bf{x}},{\bf{x}}^{\prime}
, not on their ratios. Thus the staircase classes themselves may ascend or descend.
{\Diamond}

Remark 2.1.5
.

(i)
Since the basic inequalities involve the
z
-variable, while the function
b\mapsto\mathrm{acc}(b)
can reverse orientation, we will often work below with the
z
-interval
I_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
blocked by
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
rather than the possibly more natural blocked
b
-interval
J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
. Note also that the center
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
of
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
always lies in
I_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
, while
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
never lies in
J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
. In fact,
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.11]
shows that
\mathrm{acc}(m^{\prime}/d^{\prime})>{\partial}^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}})
in all cases.

(ii) If
x_{k},y_{k},k\geq 0,
are recursively defined as in (
2.1.11
) then the difference
x_{k+1}y_{k}-x_{k}y_{k+1}
is constant. Thus one can tell if the ratios
x_{k}/y_{k}
increase or decrease with
k
by looking at the first two terms.
{\Diamond}

Here is our main definition.

Definition 2.1.6
.

The quasi-perfect classes

{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}=(d_{\lambda},m_{\lambda},p_{\lambda},q_{\lambda},t_{\lambda},\varepsilon),{\bf{E}}_{\mu}=(d_{\mu},m_{\mu},p_{\mu},q_{\mu},t_{\mu},\varepsilon),{\bf{E}}_{\rho}=(d_{\rho},m_{\rho},p_{\rho},q_{\rho},t_{\rho},\varepsilon),

with
p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}<p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}<p_{\rho}/q_{\rho}
and
t_{\lambda},t_{\mu},t_{\rho}\geq 3
, are said to form a
generating triple

\mathcal{T}
if

(a)

{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
are adjacent,

(b)

{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
are adjacent and
t_{\rho}
-compatible, i.e.
t_{\rho}=q_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\lambda}q_{\mu}
;

(c)

{\bf{E}}_{\rho},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
are adjacent and
t_{\lambda}
-compatible, i.e.
t_{\lambda}=q_{\mu}p_{\rho}-p_{\mu}q_{\rho}
;

(d)

t_{\lambda}t_{\rho}-t_{\mu}=q_{\lambda}p_{\rho}-p_{\lambda}q_{\rho}
,

(e)

\mathrm{acc}(m_{\rho}/d_{\rho}),\ \mathrm{acc}(m_{\lambda}/d_{\lambda})>\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})
.

Here the letters
{\lambda},\mu,\rho
stand for ‘left’, ‘middle’, and ‘right’.

Example 2.1.7
.

The basic examples of generating triples are the triples

\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}:=({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}^{1}_{n},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1}),\quad n\geq 0,

where

{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}=(n+3,n+2,2n+6,1,2n+3),\quad\varepsilon:=1,

are the blocking classes for the
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
family and, for each
n
,
{\bf{E}}^{1}_{n}
denotes the shared first step of the staircases
18
18

18

The convention is that staircases with label
\ell=
‘lower’ are ascending and converge to the lower endpoint of the corresponing blocked interval, while those
label
u=
‘upper’ are descending. In the current paper the steps are indexed by
k
(while
[
MM
]
used both
k
and
{\kappa}
for reasons explained in
[
MM
, §3.1]
.) The other decorations in the label
of
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n}
for example describe the family (namely
U
) and the blocking class, in this case
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
.

{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n+1}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n}
. As we see from (
2.1.15
),
{\bf{E}}^{1}_{n}
has center
p^{1}_{n}/q^{1}_{n}=[2n+7;2n+4]
with parameters

(2.1.14)

\displaystyle(d_{n}^{1},m_{n}^{1},p^{1}_{n},q^{1}_{n},t^{1}_{n}):

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigl(2n^{2}+11n+14,\ 2n^{2}+9n+9,\ 4n^{2}+22n+29,2n+4,\ 4n^{2}+16n+13\bigr);

we sometimes denote it by
{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7;2n+4]}
.
Further both the ascending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n+1}
and descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n}
have decreasing ratios
m_{k}/d_{k}
, and, by Remark
2.1.4
(ii) these ratios converge to the appropriate endpoint of the blocked
b
-intervals.
In particular, the ratios for the ascending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n+1}
satisfy
m_{n}^{0}/d_{n}^{0}>m_{n}^{1}/d_{n}^{1}>{\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1}})
. Therefore because
b\mapsto\mathrm{acc}(b)
preserves orientation for
b>1/3
the required inequalities

\mathrm{acc}(m_{n+1}^{0}/d_{n+1}^{0}),\mathrm{acc}(m_{n}^{0}/d_{n}^{0})>\mathrm{acc}(m_{n}^{1}/d_{n}^{1})

hold. For later reference, the centers of the steps of staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n}
and their accumulation points are

(2.1.15)

\displaystyle p^{U}_{\ell,n,i}/q^{U}_{\ell,n,i}

\displaystyle=[\{2n+5,2n+1\}^{i},{\rm end}_{n}],\;\ i\geq 0,

\displaystyle\qquad\mbox{ where }\ {\rm end}_{n}=2n+4\;\;\mbox{ or }(2n+5,2n+2),

\displaystyle p^{U}_{u,n,i}/q^{U}_{u,n,i}

\displaystyle=[2n+7;\{2n+5,2n+1\}^{i},{\rm end}_{n}],\;\ i\geq 0,

\displaystyle\mbox{ with limits }z^{U}_{\ell,n,\infty}

\displaystyle=[\{2n+5,2n+1\}^{\infty}],\qquad z^{U}_{u,n,\infty}=[2n+7;\{2n+5,2n+1\}^{\infty}].

Note that here we use
i
as the indexing label for the staircase steps because, for ease of writing, we have written each of the two strands
19
19

19

As remarked earlier, we use the word ‘staircase’ rather loosely to refer to any sequence of classes, infinitely many of which are live for some limiting
b
-value. A subset of these classes whose continued fraction can be described by a single formula is called a
strand
; see
[
MM
, §3.1]
for further discussion.
In the case of the staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n},{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n}
, these strands are distinguished by the ending of the continued fractions of their centers
p/q
.

of the staircase separately. These strands are intertwined; thus the steps in

{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,1}
when written in ascending order have centers at

[6],\ [7;4],\ [7;3,6],\ [7;3,7,4],\ [7;3,7,3,6],\dots.

(For an explanation of how to order continued fractions see Appendix
A
.)
{\Diamond}

Remark 2.1.8
.

(i) An important fact about quasi-perfect classes
(d,m,p,q)
with
m/d>1/3
is that

p/q<\mathrm{acc}(m/d)
; see
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.11]
. This is relevant in a variety of contexts, for example in Lemma
4.2.5
that analyzes the relations between two different perfect classes. Notice also that the definition of a generating triple requires control over the relative positions of the points
\mathrm{acc}(m_{\lambda},d_{\lambda}),\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})
and
\mathrm{acc}(m_{\rho}/d_{\rho})
. One consequence of this hypothesis is that the ratios
m/d
decrease for all our staircases with
b>1/3
, which, as pointed out in Proposition
3.1.7
, is important in the proof that these pre-staircases are live.

We also showed
in
[
MM
, Lem.2.3.5]
that for the
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}

family we have

(2.1.16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{acc}(m_{n}^{0}/d_{n}^{0})<p^{0}_{n+1}/q^{0}_{n+1}=2n+8,\qquad\forall\ n\geq 0.

Because
m_{n}^{1}/d_{1}^{1}<m_{n}^{0}/d_{n}^{0}
we also have
\mathrm{acc}(m_{n}^{1}/d_{1}^{1})<p^{0}_{n+1}/q^{0}_{n+1}
.
In fact, it follows from Lemma
2.1.10
below (applied to the quasi-triples
20
20

20

See Remark
2.1.14
.

({\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1})
and their images under the symmetries) that all the generating triples that we encounter have the property that

\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})<p_{\rho}/q_{\rho}
.
We did not put this property in the definition for the sake of simplicity.

(ii) The triples
\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}
all have
t_{\lambda}<t_{\rho}
and
\varepsilon=1
. However this inequality is reversed when we consider triples with
b<1/3
. For example, the reflection
R
defined in §
2.3
below takes the
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
staircase family (with
b>1/3,\ \varepsilon=1
) to the
{\mathcal{S}}^{L}
family (with
b<1/3,\ \varepsilon=-1
). It fixes
t
but reverses the
z
-orientation. We will prove in Proposition
2.3.2
that the action of
R
preserves generating triples. For example, the three classes

\displaystyle{\bf{B}}^{L}_{2}=R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{2})

\displaystyle=\bigl(10,1,25,4,7,-1\bigr),\quad R^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}^{1}_{2})=\bigl(48,5,120,19,33,-1\bigr),

\displaystyle{\bf{B}}^{L}_{1}=R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{1})=\bigl(5,0,13,2,5,-1\bigr)

also form a generating triple. Note that the image by
R
of the basic triple
({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}^{1}_{1},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1})

whose classes have centers
(6,[7;4],8)
requires special treatment because the point
R(6)
is undefined; see Remark
2.3.6
.

{\Diamond}

We show below that any generating triple
\mathcal{T}
has two associated
principal pre-staircases
, a descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
with seeds (i.e. first two steps)
{\bf{E}}_{\rho},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
, and an ascending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}

with seeds
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
. In order to prove
this we will use our main result, which states that if
\mathcal{T}:=({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
is a generating triple, there are two associated generating triples
x\mathcal{T}:=({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{x\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\mu})
and
y\mathcal{T}:=({\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{y\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
. Here the new class
{\bf{E}}_{x\mu}
in the first triple
x\mathcal{T}

is what will be the third step of the descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
while the middle entry in
y\mathcal{T}
is the third step
{\bf{E}}_{y\mu}
of

{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
.
We sometimes call these the
left (resp. right) derived triples
, with
x
denoting a move to the left and
y
a move to the right. We will also call
x\mathcal{T}
(resp.
y\mathcal{T}
) the left (resp. right)
mutation
of
\mathcal{T}
. See Figure
2.1
to visualize the left versus right move.

Proposition 2.1.9
.

Suppose that
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}:=(d_{\lambda},m_{\lambda},p_{\lambda},q_{\lambda},t_{\lambda},1),\ {\bf{E}}_{\mu}=(d_{\mu},m_{\mu},p_{\mu},q_{\mu},t_{\mu},1)
and
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}:=(d_{\rho},m_{\rho},p_{\rho},q_{\rho},t_{\rho},1)
form a generating triple
\mathcal{T}
.
Then

(i)

If
{\bf{E}}_{x\mu}=\bigl(d_{x\mu},m_{x\mu},p_{x\mu},q_{x\mu},t_{x\mu},1\bigr)
is the class obtained by one
t_{\lambda}
-iteration from
{\bf{E}}_{\rho},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
then
the classes
({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{x\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\mu})
form a generating triple
x\mathcal{T}
.

(ii)

Similarly, if

{\bf{E}}_{y\mu}=\bigl(d_{y\mu},m_{y\mu},p_{y\mu},q_{y\mu},t_{y\mu},1\bigr)
is the class obtained by one
t_{\rho}
-iteration from
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
,
then
({\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{y\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
form a generating triple
y\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

To prove (i), we must check that conditions (a) through (e) hold for
x\mathcal{T}
. Condition (a) states that
E_{\lambda},E_{\mu}
are adjacent, which holds by hypothesis.

The first step in the proof of (b) is to check that the classes
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{x\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
satisfy the order condition
p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}<p_{x\mu}/q_{x\mu}<p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}
. Now
p_{x\mu}/q_{x\mu}<p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}

because
p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}<p_{\rho}/q_{\rho}
; see Remark
2.1.5
(ii).
Further
p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}<p_{x\mu}/q_{x\mu}
if
p_{\lambda}(t_{\lambda}q_{\mu}-q_{\rho})<(t_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\rho})q_{\lambda}
, that is, if

q_{\lambda}p_{\rho}-p_{\lambda}q_{\rho}<t_{\lambda}\bigl(q_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\lambda}q_{\mu}\bigr)=t_{\lambda}t_{\rho},

where the equality holds by condition (b) in Definition
2.1.6
.
But

q_{\lambda}p_{\rho}-p_{\lambda}q_{\rho}=t_{\lambda}t_{\rho}-t_{\mu}

by (d), and
t_{\mu}\geq 3
by hypothesis.
Therefore this order condition holds.

It follows that the new class
{\bf{E}}_{x\mu}
is adjacent to
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
, because it is a linear combination of
the two classes
{\bf{E}}_{\rho},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
which are both adjacent to
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
, and the condition in (
2.1.8
) for classes with centers
>p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}
to be adjacent to
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
is linear in the variables
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
.
Further
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{x\mu}
are
t_{\mu}
-compatible because

\displaystyle q_{\lambda}p_{x\mu}-p_{\lambda}q_{x\mu}

\displaystyle=q_{\lambda}(t_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\rho})-p_{\lambda}(t_{\lambda}q_{\mu}-q_{\rho})

\displaystyle=t_{\lambda}t_{\rho}-(t_{\lambda}t_{\rho}-t_{\mu})=t_{\mu}

by conditions (b) and (d) for
\mathcal{T}
.
Therefore (b) holds for
x\mathcal{T}
.

Condition (c) requires that
{\bf{E}}_{x\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
be adjacent and
t_{\lambda}
-compatible, which holds by
Lemma
2.1.2
(i).

Condition (d) requires that
t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}-t_{x\mu}=q_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\lambda}q_{\mu}
. But

\displaystyle t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}-t_{x\mu}=t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}-(t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}-t_{\rho})=t_{\rho}=q_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\lambda}q_{\mu},

where the last equality holds by condition (c) for the initial triple
\mathcal{T}
.

Finally we must check the inequalities for
\mathrm{acc}(m_{x\mu}/d_{x\mu})
. Lemma
2.1.10
below shows that
\mathrm{acc}(m_{x\mu}/d_{x\mu})<p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}
. (Note that
p_{x\mu}>2p_{\mu}
because
p_{x\mu}=tp_{\mu}-p_{\rho}
for some
t\geq 3
.)
Because
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
is a quasi-perfect class,
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.11]
implies that
p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}<\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})
. Further
\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})<\mathrm{acc}(m_{\lambda}/d_{\lambda})
by assumption (e) for
\mathcal{T}
.
Therefore we have
\mathrm{acc}(m_{x\mu}/d_{x\mu})<\mathrm{acc}(m_{\lambda}/d_{\lambda}),\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})
. This completes the proof of (i).

The proof of (ii) is very similar. Again condition (a) is automatic, while to prove (b) we must first check that the centers of the classes are correctly ordered, i.e. we need

p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}<p_{y\mu}/q_{y\mu}<p_{\rho}/q_{\rho}
. The first inequality holds by construction, while the second
holds if

(t_{\rho}p_{\mu}-p_{\lambda})q_{\rho}<(t_{\rho}q_{\mu}-q_{\lambda})p_{\rho},
that is if

q_{\lambda}p_{\rho}-p_{\lambda}q_{\rho}<t_{\rho}(q_{\mu}p_{\rho}-p_{\mu}q_{\rho})=t_{\rho}t_{\lambda}.

But this holds because
q_{\lambda}p_{\rho}-p_{\lambda}q_{\rho}=t_{\lambda}t_{\rho}-t_{\mu}
by hypothesis.

The rest of the proof follows as before; again we use Lemma
2.1.10
to check (e).
∎

The next lemma applies to any pair of classes
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
that are adjacent and
t^{\prime\prime}
-compatible for some
t^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
with decreasing centers
p/q>p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
and with
p<p^{\prime}
. Hence, in particular, it
applies to any pair of adjacent steps in a descending pre-staircase.

Lemma 2.1.10
.

Let
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q,t,1),{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},t^{\prime},1)
be quasi-perfect classes with
3+2\sqrt{2}<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}<p/q
that are adjacent and
t^{\prime\prime}
-compatible for some
t^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
. Assume further that
\sqrt{2}p^{\prime}>p
. Then

w^{\prime}:=\mathrm{acc}\Bigl(\frac{m^{\prime}}{d^{\prime}}\Bigr)<\frac{p}{q}.

Proof.

To prove this it suffices to show that

w^{\prime}+\frac{1}{w^{\prime}}<\frac{p}{q}+\frac{q}{p}.

Since
(d,m,p,q)
is quasi-perfect,
w^{\prime}:=\mathrm{acc}(m^{\prime}/d^{\prime})
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle w^{\prime}+\frac{1}{w^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(3-m^{\prime}/d^{\prime})^{2}}{1-(m^{\prime}/d^{\prime})^{2}}-2

\displaystyle=\frac{(3d^{\prime}-m^{\prime})^{2}-2((d^{\prime})^{2}-(m^{\prime})^{2})}{(d^{\prime})^{2}-(m^{\prime})^{2}}=\frac{(p^{\prime})^{2}+(q^{\prime})^{2}+2}{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}-1}.

Thus we must show that

(2.1.17)

\frac{(p^{\prime})^{2}+(q^{\prime})^{2}+2}{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}-1}<\frac{p}{q}+\frac{q}{p},

or equivalently

\frac{(p+q)^{2}}{pp^{\prime}qq^{\prime}}<\frac{p^{2}+q^{2}}{pq}-\frac{(p^{\prime})^{2}+(q^{\prime})^{2}}{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}}.

Consider the function
h(z)=z+1/z
. This is an increasing function for
z>1
whose derivative increases to
1
as
z\to\infty
. Therefore, for
z>z^{\prime}
we have

h(z)-h(z^{\prime})>h^{\prime}(z)(z-z^{\prime}),

so that it suffices to show that

h^{\prime}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})(p/q-p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})>\frac{(p+q)^{2}}{pp^{\prime}qq^{\prime}}
, or equivalently

\displaystyle\Bigl(1-\bigl(\frac{q^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}}\bigr)^{2}\Bigr)(pq^{\prime}-p^{\prime}q)>\frac{(p+q)^{2}}{pp^{\prime}}=\Bigl(1+\frac{q}{p}\Bigr)^{2}\ \frac{p}{p^{\prime}}.

But
pq^{\prime}-p^{\prime}q=t^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
, so that because
p/q,p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}>3+2\sqrt{2}
it suffices to check that

2\left(1-\frac{1}{(3+2\sqrt{2})^{2}}\right)>\left(1+\frac{1}{3+2\sqrt{2}}\right)^{2}\frac{p}{p^{\prime}}\;\Leftrightarrow\;\sqrt{2}>\frac{p}{p^{\prime}},

which holds by hypothesis.
∎

Corollary 2.1.11
.

In any generating triple
({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})

that is derived by mutation from one of the basic triples
({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{n}^{1},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1})
in Example
2.1.7
, we
have
\mathrm{acc}(\frac{m_{\mu}}{d_{\mu}})<\frac{p_{\rho}}{q_{\rho}}
.

Proof.

We observed in Remark
2.1.8
(i) that this holds for the triples
({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{n}^{1},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1})
. We can also deduce this from Lemma
2.1.10
because
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1},{\bf{E}}_{n}^{1}
are adjacent steps in the descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n}
. It holds for all derived triples by induction. In the case of

({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{x\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\mu})
this is immediate because
{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{x\mu}
are adjacent steps in a descending pre-staircase. In the case of
({\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{y\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
it holds because
\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})<p_{\rho}/q_{\rho}
by the inductive hypothesis, and
{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{y\mu}
are steps in an ascending pre-staircase with decreasing ratios
m_{k}/d_{k}
so that
\mathrm{acc}(m_{y\mu}/d_{y\mu})<\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})
.
∎

Lemma 2.1.12
.

If
({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
form a generating triple
\mathcal{T}
then:

(i)
