The pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
with recursion parameter
t_{\rho}
and seeds
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
ascends and has blocking class
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
.

(ii)

The pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
with recursion parameter
t_{\lambda}
and seeds
{\bf{E}}_{\rho},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
descends and has blocking class
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
.

(iii)

In both cases the sequence
\mathrm{acc}(m_{k}/d_{k})
decreases and the limits
z_{\infty}=\lim p_{k}/q_{k}
and
b_{\infty}=\lim m_{k}/d_{k}
are irrational.

Proof.

The pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
has first two steps
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
with subsequent steps
{\bf{E}}_{y^{k}\mu},k\geq 1.
It follows from Proposition
2.1.9
(ii) that all these steps lie below
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
,
and are adjacent to
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
. Hence, as explained in Remark
2.1.4
(ii),
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
is the blocking class for this pre-staircase. In particular, this implies that this pre-staircase accumulates at
\bigl(b_{\infty},z_{\infty}=\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})\bigr)
where
b_{\infty}={\partial}^{-}J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}}
.
Similarly, the subsequent steps
{\bf{E}}_{x^{k}\mu},k\geq 1,
of the descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
lie above
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
, and are all adjacent to it. Thus this pre-staircase accumulates at the upper endpoint
{\partial}^{+}J_{{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}}
of the interval blocked by
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
. Finally to check (iii) notice that the first two terms of these sequences decrease because
we assume
\mathrm{acc}(m_{\rho}/d_{\rho}),\mathrm{acc}(m_{\lambda}/d_{\lambda})>\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})
. Therefore the sequences decrease by Remark
2.1.5
(ii). Finally
the irrationality claims follow from Remark
2.1.3
(i).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2.6
part (i).

This follows immediately from Example
2.1.7
, Proposition
2.1.9
, and Lemma
2.1.12
.
∎

Remark 2.1.13
.

(i) In view of Corollary
2.1.11
we could have sharpened the last condition

\mathrm{acc}(m_{\rho}/d_{\rho}),\mathrm{acc}(m_{\lambda}/d_{\lambda})>\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})
in the definition of a generating triple by adding the requirement

\mathrm{acc}(\frac{m_{\mu}}{d_{\mu}})<\frac{p_{\rho}}{q_{\rho}}
. We did not do this to keep the definition as simple as possible.
Note however that, as we shall show later (for example in Lemma
3.2.1
), the value of
m/d
is relevant to the behavior of the corresponding obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}
as
b
varies.

(ii)
It follows from Lemma
2.1.12
that all the pre-staircases in the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
have decreasing ratios
m_{k}/d_{k}
. This is a crucial ingredient in the proof that these pre-staircases are all live; see Proposition
3.1.7
.

(iii) The fact that all the pre-staircases in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
, whether ascending or descending, have ratios
m_{k}/d_{k}
that decrease with
k
is rather paradoxical, since one would naively expect that these ratios would increase for ascending pre-staircases. Indeed, given two classes
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q,t)
and
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},t^{\prime})
we have

\displaystyle\frac{m}{d}<\frac{m^{\prime}}{d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \frac{(p+q)+3t}{3(p+q)+t}<\frac{(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})+3t^{\prime}}{3(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})+t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\ \Longleftrightarrow\ t(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})<t^{\prime}(p+q)\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \frac{t}{p+q}<\frac{t^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}.

However, if we ignore the integer
8
that appears in the definition of
t
and denote
z:=p/q
, we have

\frac{t^{2}}{(p+q)^{2}}\approx\frac{p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}}{(p+q)^{2}}=\frac{z^{2}-6z+1}{(z+1)^{2}},

which is an increasing function of
z
for
z>1
. Given
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
, this rough estimate suggests that we should also have
\frac{t}{p+q}<\frac{t^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}
and hence
m/d<m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
; but this is not the case.
Thus, even though the number
8
is very small compared to the eventual size of
p,q
its influence in the relevant formulas cannot be ignored.

{\Diamond}

Remark 2.1.14
.

(Quasi-triples)
As noted in (
1.2.4
) above, in
[
MM
]
the staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n}
was defined to have blocking class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
and seeds
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed}=(1,1,1,1,2)
and
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n-1}.
Using the recursion parameter for
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},
the next step in the staircase following
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n-1}
is
{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7,2n+4]}
as expected from Example
2.1.7
. For all
n
, this first seed
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed}
is independent of
n.
Similarly, for
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n},
the seed

{\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed}=(-2,0,-5,-1,2)
plays a similar role. In
[
MM
]
, these seeds were especially useful because they behave well under symmetries and also, because their entries are independent of
n,
they made many computations simpler.

Because the two seeds
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed}
and
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed}
together with the blocking class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
generate recursive staircases as described above,
the triads

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\ell,seed}^{n}:=

\displaystyle({\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1}),\qquad\mathcal{T}_{u,seed}^{n}:=({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1},{\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed}),

satisfy the adjacency and
t
-compatibility conditions (a),(b),(c) for a generating triple. It is also easy to check that (d) holds.
Further if we define their
x
- and
y
-mutations as in Proposition
2.1.9
, we have

\displaystyle y\mathcal{T}_{u,seed}^{n}=\mathcal{T}_{u,seed}^{n+1}\quad\text{and}\quad x\mathcal{T}^{n}_{\ell,seed}=\mathcal{T}_{\ell,seed}^{n-1}\quad\mbox{ while}

\displaystyle x\mathcal{T}_{u,seed}^{n}=\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}\quad\text{and}\quad y\mathcal{T}^{n}_{\ell,seed}=\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}.

We will say that triples such as

\mathcal{T}_{\ell,seed}^{n},\mathcal{T}_{u,seed}^{n}
that satisfy conditions (a), (b), (c), and (d) of Definition
2.1.6
are
quasi-triples
. They do not satisfy condition (e). Also neither of the seeds are blocking classes; indeed the upper seed has negative entries, so it is not even a geometric class.

As explained in
[
MM
, §3.3]
, both
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed}
and
-{\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed}=S^{\sharp}{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed}
are steps in the third strand of the staircase at
b=1/3
; indeed this strand has the single seed

{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed}
and has steps given the images of this seed under the shifts
S^{k},k\geq 1.
Further, as we show in §
4.3
, the classes in this strand obstruct the existence of
ascending staircases at the special rational
b
. The other two strands of this staircase
are given by the images by the shift
S^{k},k\geq 1,
of the classes
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{-2}=(1,0,2,1,1,-1)
and
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{-1}=(2,1,4,1,1,1)
.
Moreover, by replacing the
t
entries in
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{-2}
and
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{-3}
by
\varepsilon t
, we can think of
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{-3}=(0,-1,0,1,3,-1)
and
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{-2}
as the first two terms of the sequence
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},n\geq-3
with recursion parameter
t=2
. The results in
[
M1
]
show that this iteration corresponds to the notion of
v
-mutation in the toric model.
Thus the whole configuration of perfect classes found in this paper is generated by the staircase at
b=1/3
together with mutations and symmetries.
{\Diamond}

Remark 2.1.15
.

(Fake pre-staircases and pseudo-triples):
To give perspective on the problem, we now explain some properties of a family of Diophantine classes that share many of the properties of the classes described above but which are ‘fake’, i.e. they do NOT reduce properly under Cremona moves, and are not live.
21
21

21

In fact, the very first pre-staircase that we found when working on
[
BHM
]
actually was fake, and is the image under
S
of one of the pre-staircases described here. See
[
BHM
, Ex.28(iii)]
.
Also see
[
MM
, §4.1]
for an explanation of Cremona reduction.

These classes have the formulas described in Example
2.1.7
but with parameters in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}{\smallsetminus}\mathbb{Z}
, so that only half of the them have integral entries and hence correspond to obstructive classes.
For example, the tuple
{\bf{B}}_{1/2}^{U}=\bigl(\frac{7}{2},\frac{5}{2},7,1,4\bigl)
has center
7
, and of course does not represent an obstructive class. For clarity we will call such a tuple a
pseudo-class
. Similarly none of the tuples
{\bf{B}}_{n-1/2}^{U},n\in{\mathbb{N}},

have integral values for
(d,m)
.
On the other hand, the tuples
{\bf{E}}_{n-1/2}^{1}
are integral for all integers
n
, though
one can check that none of them are exceptional divisors; for example they do not
Cremona reduce correctly and also have negative intersection with
exceptional classes such as
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
.

Note that the triples
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{n}:=\mathcal{T}_{n-1/2}:=\bigl({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n-1/2},{\bf{E}}^{1}_{n-1/2},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1/2}\bigr)
satisfy all the numeric conditions to be a
generating triple (except the requirement to be integral!), and half of the tuples in the ascending ‘staircase’ generated by
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n-1/2},{\bf{E}}^{1}_{n-1/2}
by
t^{0}_{n+1/2}
-recursion are integral.
For example, when
n=0
, the class

{\bf{E}}_{-1/2}^{1}=\bigl(9,5,19,3,6\bigr),\quad p/q=[6;3]

is a step in an ascending fake pre-staircase with centers
[6;\{2,6\}^{k},3]
.
These are the odd-placed terms in the pre-staircase with seeds
{\bf{B}}_{-1/2}^{U},{\bf{E}}_{-1/2}^{1}
and recursion parameter
\rho=t^{0}_{1/2}=4
and hence form one of the strands of this ascending pseudo pre-staircase. As explained in
[
MM
, Lem.3.3.1]
, we can consider this sequence to have recursion parameter
\nu=\rho^{2}-2=14
, and to be generated
22
22

22

In fact we can take the initial seed to be the class
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed}=(1,1,1,1,2)
in Remark
2.1.14
.

by
{\bf{E}}_{-1/2}^{1}
and the third term in this sequence which is

{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=\bigl(125,69,265,41,83\bigr),\quad p/q=[6;2,6,3].

Thus this fake pre-staircase has only one strand. It satisfies the linear relation given by the pseudo-class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{-1/2}
.
By Lemma
2.1.16
below, for sufficiently large
k
the classes
{\bf{E}}_{k}=(d_{k},m_{k},p_{k},q_{k},t_{k})
in this fake pre-staircase are obstructive when
b=\lim m_{k}/d_{k}
. However, they are not live because the obstruction from
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
is larger. Further, one can check that
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}\cdot{\bf{B}}^{U}_{-1/2}=-1
; in fact

{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}\cdot{\bf{E}}<0
for all elements
{\bf{E}}
in this sequence.

Similarly, only one strand of the corresponding descending sequence of tuples is integral, and it also consists of fake classes. For example, when
n=1
this strand of the descending sequence generated by
{\bf{E}}^{0}_{3/2}
and
{\bf{E}}^{1}_{1/2}
with recursion parameter
t^{0}_{1/2}
and pseudo-blocking class
{\bf{E}}^{0}_{1/2}
can be taken to have
seeds

{\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed}=(-2,0,-5,-1,2)
and
{\bf{E}}^{1}_{1/2}=(20,14,41,5,22)
with recursion parameter
(t^{0}_{1/2})^{2}-2=14
and hence next step

{\bf{E}}=\bigl(282,196,579,71,306\bigr),\quad p/q=[8;6,2,5].

Thus, the general step has centers at
[8;\{6,2\}^{k},5],k\geq 0
, and lies in the interval blocked by
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}
, though it satisfies the linear relation given by the pseudo-blocking class
{\bf{E}}^{0}_{1/2}
.
Again
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{1}_{1/2}<0
.

The reader can check that there is an analogous descending pseudo-staircase with centers in the interval blocked by

{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
. This one satisfies the linear relation determined by
{\bf{E}}^{0}_{-3/2}
with
t=2
and is generated numerically
by
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed}
and
{\bf{E}}^{1}_{-1/2}
with recursion parameter
t^{2}-2=2=\nu
.
This sequence consists of the tuples

{\bf{E}}^{\prime\prime}_{\ell,k}:=\bigl(-2+11k,5k,-5+24k,-1+4k,2+4k\bigr),\ k\geq 1,p_{k}/q_{k}=[6;4k-1],

and its continued fraction expansion is ‘degenerate’ in the sense that it does not contain repeated digits.
23
23

23

This happens because this sequence converges too slowly: indeed,
\nu=2
is too small for the argument outlined in Remark
2.1.3
to apply.

Further, one can check that at the limiting
b
-value
b_{\infty}=\mathrm{acc}_{U}^{-1}(6)=5/11
all these classes

{\bf{E}}^{\prime\prime}_{\ell,k}
fail to be obstructive at their centers since the requirement
|d_{k}b_{\infty}-m_{k}|<\sqrt{1-b_{\infty}^{2}}

evaluates to
\frac{10}{11}<\sqrt{96}/{11}
, which is false.

{\Diamond}

Lemma 2.1.16
.

Let
{\bf{E}}_{k}=(d_{k},m_{k},p_{k},q_{k},t_{k}),k\geq 0,
be a sequence of quasi-perfect classes with
{\bf{E}}_{0}<{\bf{E}}_{1}
and
whose entries satisfy a recursion
x_{k+1}=\nu x_{k}-x_{k-1}
with
\nu\geq 3
. Then, if
b_{\infty}:=\lim m_{k}/d_{k}
, there is
k_{0}
such that

\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{k},b_{\infty}}(p_{k}/q_{k})>c_{H_{b_{\infty}}}(p_{k}/q_{k}),\quad k\geq k_{0}.

Hence
\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})=\lim_{k}p_{k}/q_{k}.

Proof.

By
[
BHM
, Lem.15(iii)]
, a quasi-perfect class
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
is obstructive at
p/q
for some
b
(that is, has

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)>c_{H_{b}}(p/q)
) if and only if
|db-m|<\sqrt{1-b^{2}}
.

As we noted in Remark
2.1.3
, when
\nu\geq 3
there is
{\lambda}>1
such that
the ratios
m_{k}/d_{k},p_{k}/q_{k}
converge to
M/D,P/Q
at the rate
O({\lambda}^{-2k})
. Further,

|d_{k}\tfrac{M}{D}-m_{k}|=\tfrac{1}{D}|M\overline{D}-D\overline{M}|{\lambda}^{-2k}<\sqrt{1-\tfrac{M^{2}}{D^{2}}},\quad\mbox{ for suff large }k.

Thus, for sufficiently large
k
the function
\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{k},b_{\infty}}
is obstructive at
p_{k}/q_{k}
, which proves the first claim.

The second claim then follows from
[
CG-HMP
, Thm.1.13]
. Notice that the statement of this theorem assumes the existence of a staircase at
b_{\infty}
, i.e. a sequence of live classes — however the proof given there only uses the fact the classes are obstructive at
b_{\infty}
.
∎

2.2.
The pattern of derived classes

We now describe the set
\mathcal{E}_{[6,8]}
formed by all the blocking classes that are derived from the foundational triple

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}:=\bigl({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}\bigr)

defined in Example
2.1.7
. The main result is Proposition
2.2.6
stating that the union of the corresponding blocked
z
-intervals intersects the interval
[6,8]
in a dense, open subset.
Finally, we assign an infinite ternary decimal label to each point
z\in[6,8]
that is not blocked, and state in Proposition
2.2.8
a more detailed version
of the claims in Theorem
1.1.1
(ii) about the associated pre-staircases.

For simplicity, we will only discuss in detail the case when
z\in[6,8]
. However, the pattern formed by the classes with centers in the intervals
[2n+6,2n+8],n>0
is precisely the same; see Remark
2.2.5
. In particular, the density result holds for all
n
.

We first introduce a ternary decimal label
{\delta}
for the classes
E_{\delta}\in\mathcal{E}_{[6,8]}
.

Figure 2.1.
The edges of the main recursion are in yellow (given by
{\delta}(w)\to{\delta}(xw),\ {\delta}(yw)
), with the other edges of the triples in dotted grey; none of these edges cross the vertical green lines that represent
the blocked intervals
I_{{\bf{E}}}
.

Lemma 2.2.1
.

Each class in
\mathcal{E}_{[6,8]}
can be given a ternary decimal label
{\delta}

such that the following holds:

(i)

We define
{\bf{E}}_{0}:={\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{.1}:={\bf{E}}_{[7;4]},{\bf{E}}_{1}:={\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}
, and call the corresponding triple either
\mathcal{T}_{.1}
or
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{0}
.
All other classes have labels of the form
{\delta}=.a_{1}\cdots a_{k-1}1
where
a_{i}\in\{0,2\}
.

(ii)

If
{\delta}=.a_{1}\cdots a_{k-1}1
then
{\bf{E}}_{\delta}
lies on the
k
th
level
,
i.e. it is the middle class
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
in a generating triple
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
formed from
\mathcal{T}_{.1}
by
k-1
steps;

(iii)

the labelling respects order: if
{\delta}<{\delta}^{\prime}
then the
z
-blocked interval
I_{{\bf{E}}_{\delta}}
lies to the left of
I_{{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

The label is assigned inductively: if the triple

\mathcal{T}_{\delta}:=\bigl({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}={\bf{E}}_{\delta},{\bf{E}}_{\rho}\bigr)
with
{\delta}:=.a_{1}\cdots a_{k-1}1
is already labelled, then the middle classes
{\bf{E}}_{x\mu},{\bf{E}}_{y\mu}
of its two derived triples are labelled

{\bf{E}}_{x\mu}={\bf{E}}_{.a_{1}\cdots a_{k-1}01},\qquad{\bf{E}}_{y\mu}={\bf{E}}_{.a_{1}\cdots a_{k-1}21},

so that the corresponding triples are

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle x\mathcal{T}_{.a_{1}\cdots a_{k-1}1}:=\mathcal{T}_{.a_{1}\cdots a_{k-1}01},\qquad y\mathcal{T}_{.a_{1}\cdots a_{k-1}1}:=\mathcal{T}_{.a_{1}\cdots a_{k-1}21}.

It is evident that this labelling has the properties claimed: see Fig.
2.1
. Note that for
k\geq 1
there are
2^{k-1}
classes at level
k
.
∎

We next consider the properties of the graph whose vertices
{\delta}={\delta}(w)
consist of finite ternary decimals in
(0,1)
with precisely one occurrence of
1
at the end, together with the edges joining
{\delta}(w)
to
{\delta}(xw)
and

{\delta}(yw)
.
The edges of this graph are colored yellow in Figure
2.1
; the initial vertex is
E_{.1}
at level
1
. The
augmented graph
is obtained from this one by adding the remaining edges in the triples; these are dotted grey in the figure.

Proposition 2.2.2
.

This labelling has the following properties.

(i)

Each class
{\bf{E}}_{\delta}
(where
{\delta}\notin\mathbb{Z}
) is the middle entry in a unique triple
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
.

(ii)

The entries in
{\delta}
record in reverse order the sequence of moves
x,y
needed to mutate from
\mathcal{T}_{.1}
to
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
.
For example,
\mathcal{T}_{.0022201}=xyyyxx\mathcal{T}_{.1}
.

(iii)

Except in the initial case
{\delta}=.1
, the other two elements
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w),{\lambda}},{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w),\rho}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
lie on different levels with exactly one of them on the immediately preceding level.
Further,
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w),{\lambda}}
lies on a higher level than
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w),\rho}
if and only if
{\delta}
ends in
01
, so that the last mutation was via
x
.

(iv)

Let
w:=x^{m_{1}}y^{n_{1}}\cdots x^{m_{j}}y^{n_{j}}
be a word in
x,y
with all
m_{i},n_{i}>0
except possibly for
m_{1},n_{j}
which may be
0
, and let
{\delta}(w):=.2^{n_{j}}0^{m_{j}}\cdots 2^{n_{1}}0^{m_{1}}1
be the corresponding ternary decimal. Then, assuming
w
is nonempty,
the other entries in the triple
w\mathcal{T}_{.1}:=\mathcal{T}_{{\delta}(w)}
are
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w),{\lambda}},\ {\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w),\rho}
where

(2.2.2)

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w),{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w^{\prime}),{\lambda}}&\mbox{ if }w=xw^{\prime}\\
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w^{\prime})}=:{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w^{\prime}),\mu}&\mbox{ if }w=yw^{\prime}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w),\rho}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w^{\prime})}=:{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w^{\prime}),\mu}&\mbox{ if }w=xw^{\prime}\\
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w^{\prime}),\rho}&\mbox{ if }w=yw^{\prime}.\end{cases}

(v)

In particular, in the triple
w\mathcal{T}_{.1}
the words in
x,y
that describe the other two elements consist of suitable initial segments of
w
.
More precisely, there is a triple with vertices
{\delta}_{\lambda},{\delta}_{\mu},{\delta}_{\rho}
iff one of the following conditions hold:
24
24

24

Here we assume
w
is not empty, but otherwise allow empty words as necessary.

-

If
{\delta}_{\mu}={\delta}(w)
where
w=x^{m_{1}}\cdots y^{n_{j}}
and
m_{1}>0
, then

{\delta}_{\rho}={\delta}(x^{m_{1}-1}\cdots y^{n_{j}})
and
{\delta}_{\lambda}={\delta}(y^{n_{1}-1}\cdots y^{n_{j}})
.

-

If
{\delta}_{\mu}={\delta}(w)
where
w=y^{n_{1}}\cdots y^{n_{j}}
and
n_{1}>0
, then

{\delta}_{\lambda}={\delta}(y^{n_{1}-1}\cdots y^{n_{j}})
and
{\delta}_{\rho}={\delta}(x^{m_{2}-1}\cdots y^{n_{j}})
.

(vi)

The two pre-staircases generated by the triple
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}=\bigl({\bf{E}}_{{\delta},{\lambda}},{\bf{E}}_{\delta},{\bf{E}}_{{\delta},\rho}\bigr)
, where
{\delta}={\delta}(w)
consist of the classes

{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(x^{j}w)},j\geq 0
(descending) with recursion parameter
t_{{\delta},{\lambda}}
, and
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(y^{j}w)},j\geq 0,
(ascending) with recursion parameter
t_{{\delta},\rho}
.

(vii)

Each
edge

{\epsilon}
in the augmented graph is a lower edge (i.e. one with an endpoint at the middle vertex
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
) in a unique triple
\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon}
, and can be associated to the unique vertex in
\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon}
that is not one of its endpoints; thus the two classes at the endpoints of
{\epsilon}
are part of a recursion whose recursion parameter is the
t
component of the third class in
\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward induction that is left to the reader.∎

Example 2.2.3
.

Here are some examples of the recursive formula in (
2.2.2
).

-

if
w=x^{3}yx
, then

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(x^{3}yx),{\lambda}}={\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(yx),{\lambda}}={\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(x)},\;\;i.e.\ \ \ {\bf{E}}_{.020001,{\lambda}}={\bf{E}}_{.01},\quad\mbox{ while }

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(x^{3}yx),\rho}={\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(x^{2}yx)},\;\;i.e.\ {\bf{E}}_{.020001,\rho}={\bf{E}}_{.02001}.

-

if
w=x^{3}y
, then

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(x^{3}y),{\lambda}}={\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(y),{\lambda}}={\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(\emptyset)},\ \ \ i.e.\ \ \ {\bf{E}}_{.20001,{\lambda}}={\bf{E}}_{.1},\quad\mbox{ while}

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(x^{3}y),\rho}={\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(x^{2}y)},\ \ \ i.e.\ {\bf{E}}_{.20001,\rho}={\bf{E}}_{.2001}.

The above notation allows us to recognize the generating triples and pre-staircases. Notice first that the set of triples
\mathcal{T}
(or equivalently, the set of classes
{\bf{E}}_{\delta}
that form their middle entries) is partially ordered, with order relation generated by the elementary steps
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w)}<{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(xw)}
and
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w)}<{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(yw)}
. This order relation has the property that each element
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w)}
has precisely two successors,
{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(xw)},{\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(yw)}

that are the middle elements in the triples
x\mathcal{T},y\mathcal{T}
respectively (where
\mathcal{T}:=\mathcal{T}_{{\delta}(w)}
), but (when
w\neq\emptyset
) exactly one predecessor
{\rm pre}({\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w)})
that lies in
\mathcal{T}
. Further,

{\rm pre}({\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w)})
is the middle entry of a triple
{\rm pre}(\mathcal{T})=:\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
that
has one other vertex, called
{\rm ppre}({\bf{E}}_{{\delta}(w)})
, in common with
\mathcal{T}
. Indeed, the construction implies that
\mathcal{T}
equals either
x\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
or
y\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
;
and the two triples
\mathcal{T}^{\prime},x\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
share the vertices
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\lambda},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\mu}
, while
\mathcal{T}^{\prime},y\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
share the vertices
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\rho},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\mu}
. Notice that in both cases,
the three classes
{\bf{E}},{\rm pre}({\bf{E}}),{\rm ppre}({\bf{E}})
form a triple (when appropriately ordered) with
{\bf{E}}
as the middle entry, and that these three classes lie on different levels, first
{\rm ppre}({\bf{E}})
, then
{\rm pre}({\bf{E}})
, and then
{\bf{E}}.
Further, the classes
{\rm pre}({\bf{E}})
and
{\bf{E}}
belong to a pre-staircase with recursion parameter
t_{{\rm ppre}({\bf{E}})}
. Note also that the two classes
{\rm ppre}({\bf{E}}),{\rm pre}({\rm pre}({\bf{E}}))
are usually different.

We then inductively assign an integer
\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{\delta})
(called its
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length
) to each vertex
{\bf{E}}_{\delta}
as follows:

•

\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}(E_{0})=\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}(E_{1})=1,\ \ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}(E_{.1})=2;

•

if
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-lengths are already assigned to all the vertices on level
k
, then we assign
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length to those on level
k+1
as follows:

(2.2.3)

\displaystyle\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{{\delta}})=\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\rm pre}({\bf{E}}_{{\delta}}))+\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\rm ppre}({\bf{E}}_{\delta})).

Thus the classes
{\bf{E}}_{.01},{\bf{E}}_{.21}
on level three have
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length
3
, while those on level four divide into two types:
we have

\displaystyle\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{.001})

\displaystyle=\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{.01})+\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{0})=\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{.1})+2\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{0})=4,

\displaystyle\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{.021})

\displaystyle=\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{.01})+\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{.1})=\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{0})+2\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{.1})=5.

Another way to understand the formula (
2.2.3
) is to assign
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-lengths recursively to the edges
{\epsilon}
of the
augmented graph giving them the same
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length as the corresponding vertex (see Proposition
2.2.2
(vii)). In this language, it reads

(2.2.4)

\displaystyle\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{{\delta}})=\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\rm pre}({\bf{E}}_{{\delta}}))+\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\epsilon}),

where
{\epsilon}
is the edge joining the two vertices
{\rm pre}({\bf{E}}_{{\delta}}),\ {\bf{E}}_{{\delta}}
.

Conjecture 2.2.4
.

We conjecture that the following rules hold.
25
25

25

The notion of
CF
-length is defined in (
A.3
).

(i)

The continued fraction expansion of the center
p/q
of the class
{\bf{E}}_{\delta}
has
CF
-length
\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{\delta})
.

(ii)

The steps of the two staircases associated to
{\bf{E}}_{\delta}
have periodic continued fractions with periodic part of length
2(\ell_{{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}}({\bf{E}}_{\delta}))
. Moreover, these periodic parts have reverse cyclic order.

For example, the class
{\bf{E}}_{.1}
has
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length
2
and center
[7;4]
. Its two staircases are periodic of period
4
with steps

\displaystyle[\{7,5,3,1\}^{k},{\rm end}],\ k\geq 0,\quad\mbox{ where }{\rm end}=6,\mbox{ or }(7,5,2),\ \ \mbox{ ascending}

\displaystyle[7,3,\{5,7,1,3\}^{k},{\rm end}],\ k\geq 0,\quad\mbox{ where }{\rm end}=6,\mbox{ or }(5,7,2),\ \ \mbox{ descending}

Further, the reverse of the periodic part
7,5,3,1
of the ascending staircase is
1,3,5,7
, which agrees with
the periodic part
5,7,1,3
of the descending staircase, modulo a cyclic permutation.
See Remark
4.1.10
for a different perspective on these results.

Proposition
4.1.3
explains what we know about the continued fraction expansions of the centers of a pair of adjacent steps
{\bf{E}}_{\delta},\ {\bf{E}}_{x{\delta}}
or
{\bf{E}}_{\delta},\ {\bf{E}}_{y{\delta}}
, while Proposition
4.1.6
establishes what we can prove about the related notion of the weight length of the step centers; see (
A.3
).

Remark 2.2.5
.

(i) To keep things simple, above we only considered the classes with centers in the interval
[6,8]
. However, we saw in Example
2.1.7

that for each
n
the classes
({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7,2n+4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1}\bigr)
form a generating triple.
It follows that, for each
n>0
, the set
\mathcal{E}_{[2n+6,2n+8]}
of all quasi-perfect classes with centers in
[2n+6,2n+8]
has precisely the same structure as
\mathcal{E}_{[6,8]}
.
Indeed,
we may assign to the classes in the initial triple
\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}:=({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7;2n+4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1})
the labels
0^{n},\ .1^{n},\,1^{n}
, and then label the derived classes by

{\delta}^{n}
where
{\delta}
is a ternary decimal as before. Thus the center classes of the first two derived triples

x\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}
and
y\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}
have labels
{\delta}^{n}_{.01},\ {\delta}^{n}_{.21}
, and so on. Conjecture
2.2.4
should hold for all
n
.

(ii)
Finally we observe that the
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length of a vertex can be given the following interpretation. The coefficients of the classes are polynomials in
n
:
by (
2.1.15
), the classes
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
are at level zero and have coefficients
p,t
that are linear in
n
, while
q=1
is constant.
Further the
p,t
coefficients of the classes at level
1
are quadratic in
n
. Thus in these initial cases
the degree of
p,t
as a function of
n
is just the
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length assigned to this class. One can then easily prove by induction that the
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length of a vertex is the degree of
p,t
as a function of
n
. The
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length of an edge (see (
2.2.4
)) can then be understood as the degree of the recursion variable for the pre-staircase with first two steps given by its endpoints.

This interpretation fits in with the above conjecture since in the cases we have calculated the periodic part of the ascending stairs has entries of the form
2n+i
for
i\in\{1,3,5,7\}
, and the degree of the corresponding recursion is half the length of this periodic part. For example, by
(
2.1.14
) the ascending staircase with blocking class
{\bf{E}}^{n}_{.1}=[2n+7;2n+4]
has periodic part
(2n+7,2n+5,2n+3,2n+1)
with recursion variable
t=4n^{2}+16n+13
.

(iii) For further comments on these matters see Remark
4.1.10
. In the table given there, we write the staircase accumulation points in a form that is slightly different (but equivalent) to that conjectured above, in order to emphasize the relation to the blocking class.

{\Diamond}

We are now in a position to prove that the blocked
z
-intervals form a dense subset of
[6,\infty)
.
Recall that for each quasi-perfect class
{\bf{E}}
, the interval
I_{\bf{E}}:=\mathrm{acc}(J_{\bf{E}})
is defined to be the set of
z
such that

\mu_{{\bf{E}},\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(z)}(z)>V_{\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(z)}(z)
, where
\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
is the appropriate inverse to the accumulation function. (In the current situation we take the inverse with values in
(1/3,1)
since all the classes
{\bf{E}}
of relevance here have
m/d>1/3
.) Note that the claim in the following proposition about the length of the blocked interval
I_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}}
was proved in
[
BHM
]
by direct calculation. The current proof is computationally much easier.

Proposition 2.2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{[6,\infty)}
be the set of all quasi-perfect classes with centers in
[6,\infty)
.
Then they block a dense subset of
[6,\infty)
; more precisely

{\it Block}_{[6,\infty)}:=\bigcup_{{\bf{E}}\in\mathcal{E}_{[6,\infty)}}I_{\bf{E}}\cap[6,\infty)\quad\mbox{ is a dense subset of }\ [6,\infty).

Moreover the length of the interval blocked by the class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
converges to
2
as
n\to\infty
.

In fact all the above intervals
I_{\bf{E}}
lie entirely in
[6,\infty)
except for
{\bf{E}}={\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
.

Proof.

Since it suffices to prove the first claim for each
n\geq 0
, we will begin with the case
n=0
. Thus, consider the subset
\mathcal{E}_{[6,8],{\ell}}
of classes in
\mathcal{E}_{[6,8]}
with level
\leq{\ell}
. We will show by induction that the set

{\it Block}_{[6,8],{\ell}}:=\bigcup_{{\bf{E}}\in\mathcal{E}_{[6,8]},{\ell}}I_{\bf{E}}\cap[6,8],\quad{\ell}\geq 0,

is
2^{-({\ell}+1)}
-dense
26
26

26

This constant could definitely be improved; but it is all we need in the current context.

in
[6,8]
. When
{\ell}=0,1
this is clear since
7\frac{1}{4}=[7;4]
is the center of
{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}
and all the points
<7
or
>7\frac{1}{2}
are blocked by either
{\bf{E}}_{6}
or
{{\bf{E}}_{8}}
.
We will prove the following:

\bullet

Any recursively defined parameter at least doubles as one moves from one level to the next. In particular, the
q
coefficient of all classes at level
{\ell}\geq 1
is at least
2^{{\ell}+1}
.

\bullet

if
\mathcal{T}
is any triple with
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
at level
{\ell}
, then in each of the two associated pre-staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}

(see Lemma
2.1.12
)

-

the distance between the centers of the second
27
27

27

This step is
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
itself.
and third steps is at most
1/2^{{\ell}+1}
;

-

the distance from the center of the third step to the pre-staircase limit is
<1/2^{{\ell}+1}
.

Since the pre-staircase limit is in the closure of
{\it Block}_{[6,8],{\ell}}
, it follows that for any point in the interval between the centers of
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
and
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
the distance between that point and

{\it Block}_{[6,8],{\ell}}
is at most
1/2^{\ell+1}
.

We argue by induction. The first point is clear since all recursion parameters are at least
3
(which is the
t
coefficient for
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
). Since
the
q
-coefficient of
{\bf{E}}_{.1}={\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}
at level one is
4
, it must at least double as one goes from level to level.
To prove the second point, notice that if
p/q
and
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
are the centers of successive steps in a pre-staircase with recursion parameter
t^{\prime\prime}
, then because these steps are adjacent and
t^{\prime\prime}
-compatible we have

|p/q-p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}|=t^{\prime\prime}/(qq^{\prime})
by Lemma
2.1.2
(iii). Both pre-staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell},{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
have second step
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
, which lies on level
{\ell}
, and so by hypothesis has
q\geq 2^{{\ell}+1}
.
Further, because the third step lies on a deeper level than the second we can estimate

\frac{t^{\prime\prime}}{qq^{\prime}}=\frac{t^{\prime\prime}}{q(t^{\prime\prime}q-q_{0})}<\frac{1}{q}\leq\frac{1}{2^{{\ell}+1}},

where
q_{0}
comes from the previous step and we have used the obvious fact that
t^{\prime\prime}q-q_{0}>t^{\prime\prime}
, or equivalently

t^{\prime\prime}(q-1)>q_{0}
.

Finally we need to check the distance from the center
p_{3}/q_{3}:=p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
of the third step to the limit
a_{\infty}
. This distance decomposes as a sum that we estimate as follows:

(2.2.5)

\displaystyle|p_{3}/q_{3}-a_{\infty}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 3}|p_{k+1}/q_{k+1}-p_{k}/q_{k}|\leq\sum_{k\geq 3}\frac{t^{\prime\prime}}{q_{k}q_{k+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t^{\prime\prime}}{q_{3}^{2}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2^{2}}+\frac{1}{2^{3}}+\dots\Bigr)\mbox{ since }q_{k+1}>2q_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t^{\prime\prime}}{q_{3}(t^{\prime\prime}q_{2}-q_{1})}\leq\frac{1}{q_{3}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{{\ell}+1}}.

This completes the proof when
n=0
.

The same argument works for general
n
, except that now we can get better estimates since the recursion parameter is at least
n+3
, so that at each stage
each recursively defined parameter increases by a factor of at least
n+2
.
In particular, because the
q
-coefficient of the level one class
{\bf{E}}^{n}_{.1}
is
2n+4
,
the
q
-coefficient of each class at level
\ell
in
\mathcal{E}_{[2n+6,2n+8]}
is at least
2(2+n)^{\ell}
.
Hence the distance
|p_{3}/q_{3}-a_{\infty}|
estimated in (
2.2.5
) has upper bound

\frac{1}{2(2+n)^{{\ell}}}
and hence converges to
0
for each fixed
\ell\geq 1
as
n\to\infty
.
In particular, when
\ell=1
this says that the distance between the centers of the third steps
{\bf{E}}^{n}_{.01},{\bf{E}}^{n}_{.21}
and the limits
{\partial}^{+}I_{[2n+6]},\ {\partial}^{-}I_{[2n+8]}
converges to zero.
But the joint second step has center at
[2n+7;2n+4]
which converges to
2n+7
as
n\to\infty
.
Further the distance between this
joint second and the third step of each pre-staircase also converges to zero. Hence the distances
{\partial}^{+}I_{[2n+6]}-2n+7
and
{\partial}^{-}I_{[2n+8]}-2n+7
both converge to zero, which implies that
the length of each interval
I_{[2n+6]}
must converge to
2
.
∎

We now define the rest of the staircases mentioned in Theorem
1.1.1
, using the notation for symmetries introduced in §
2.3
; see in particular (
2.3
), (
2.3.8
).
For
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
and
{\delta}\in\{0,1\}
, write

(2.2.6)

\displaystyle\widehat{Z}:=\widehat{Z}_{+1}\cup\widehat{Z}_{-1},\qquad\widehat{Z}_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcup_{\{(i,{\delta})\,:\,(-1)^{i+{\delta}}\,=\,\varepsilon\}}\ {\widehat{Z}}_{i,{\delta}},

\displaystyle{\widehat{Z}}_{i,{\delta}}:=\bigl\{S^{i}R^{\delta}(z)\ \big|\ z\in[6,\infty),\mathrm{acc}_{+1}^{-1}(z)\notin{\it Block}\bigr\},

and define
Z_{\varepsilon}\subset\widehat{Z}_{\varepsilon}
to consist of all points that are not endpoints of blocked intervals. Thus
\widehat{Z}
is a countable union of Cantor sets, while
Z
is the complement of the set of endpoints.
It is convenient to assign ternary decimal labels to the points in
{\widehat{Z}}
in the following way, where as before we simplify notation by describing it for the steps in
[6,8]
, with the understanding that
n
is added if
z\in[2n+6,2n+8]
, and extra decorations
(i,{\delta})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}\times\{0,1\}
are added for the points in
{\widehat{Z}}\cap\bigl(S^{i}R^{\delta}([6,\infty))\bigr)
.

Consider the triple
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
with
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}={\bf{E}}_{\delta}
, where

{\delta}=.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{i}1
.
The ascending principal pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
has steps
\bigl({\bf{E}}_{{\delta}^{+}_{k}}\bigr)_{k\geq 0},
where

{\delta}_{\ell,k}=0.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{i}2^{\times k}1
and hence has limit with infinite decimal label

{\delta}_{\ell,\infty}=0.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{i}2^{\times\infty}
. Similarly, the limit of the descending
principal pre-staircase can be given the decimal label
{\delta}_{u,\infty}=0.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{i}0^{\times\infty}
.
More generally if
{\alpha}_{\infty}=0.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{i}\cdots
is any infinite decimal with entries from
\{0,2\}

then we claim that there is a corresponding sequence of classes
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}=\bigl({\bf{E}}_{{\alpha}_{k}}\bigr)_{k\geq 1},
where
{\alpha}_{k}=0.a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{k}1
. Note that, unless
{\alpha}_{\infty}
ends in repeated
0
s or
2
s, the corresponding sequence of step centers contains both increasing and decreasing subsequences because
{\alpha}_{k}<{\alpha}_{\infty}
(resp.
{\alpha}_{k}>{\alpha}_{\infty}
) for all
k
such that
a_{k+1}=2

(resp.
a_{k+1}=0
). Further, there is a bijection between these decimals and sequences of
vertices in the graph illustrated in Fig.
2.1
that go down one level at each step.

The next definition defines the monotonic pre-staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm 1}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
by picking out particular subsequences of steps. Note that there are many ways of doing this, all of which would work equally well.

Definition 2.2.7
.

For each
{\alpha}_{\infty}\in Z
we define
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
to be the limit of the ratios
m_{{\alpha}_{k}}/d_{{\alpha}_{k}}
of the degree coordinates of
{\bf{E}}_{{\alpha}_{k}}
. Further we define the
ascending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
to have steps at
{\alpha}_{n_{k}}
where
({\alpha}_{n_{k}})_{k\geq 0}
is the maximal subsequence such that
a_{n_{k}}=0,a_{n_{k}+1}=2
. Similarly, the

descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
has steps at
{\alpha}_{n_{k}}
where
({\alpha}_{n_{k}})_{k\geq 0}
is the maximal subsequence such that
a_{n_{k}}=2,a_{n_{k}+1}=0
.

In this definition (as in Definition
1.2.5
) we use the word
pre-staircase
to refer to this sequence of perfect classes whose center points
p_{k}/q_{k}
converge to a limit
z_{\infty}
, since
the word staircase should be reserved for a sequence of classes that are live at the limiting
b
-value.
Thus this notion of pre-staircase does not assume that when
b=\mathrm{acc}^{-1}(z_{\infty})
the corresponding functions
\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{k},b}
are even obstructive at the center points
p_{k}/q_{k}
, let alone live.
Note also that it is considerably more general than that in
[
MM
, §1.2]
, since the steps are no longer required to be recursively defined.

Here is our main result about these pre-staircases.

Proposition 2.2.8
.

(i)

For each
{{\alpha}_{\infty}}\in Z
, all the steps in the ascending pre-staircase

{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
are live at
b=b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
.

(ii)

For each staircase family
(S^{i}R^{{\delta}})^{\#}\bigl({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}\cap[2n+6,2n+8]\bigr)
, there is a constant
D_{0}
such that each step in each
descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
of degree
\geq D_{0}
is live at
b=b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
.

Here is an immediate consequence.

Corollary 2.2.9
.

Every
b
that is not in the special rational sequence is either blocked or has a staircase.
Moreover, unless
b
is in the closure of a blocked interval, it admits both an ascending and a descending staircase.

The special rational
b
are described in (
2.3.9
).
The proofs are given in §
3.2
.

2.3.
Effect of symmetries

We explain how two symmetry operations from
[
MM
]
act on perfect classes and hence on triples, and prove several key facts about this action, including Theorem
1.2.6
(ii) in Corollary
2.3.4
.
The action of these symmetry operations
S^{\sharp},R^{\sharp}

is illustrated in Fig.
2.2
. Since this is rather complicated, we begin with a brief recap of some results in
[
MM
]
.

The symmetries act on the
(p,q,t)
coordinates by
{\bf{x}}:=(p,q,t)^{T}\mapsto{\bf{x}}^{\prime}:=(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},t^{\prime})^{T}
via the matrices
S,R
given by

\displaystyle S:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}6&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right),\quad R:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}6&-35&0\\
1&-6&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right).

Thus
t
is fixed, while
S,R
induce the following action on the
z
-coordinate:

\displaystyle S:(a_{\min},\infty)\to(a_{\min},6),\quad p/q\mapsto(6p-q)/p,

\displaystyle R:(6,\infty)\to(6,\infty),\quad p/q\mapsto(6p-35q)/(p-6q).

Thus
S
is a shift to the left, while
R
is a partially defined reflection that fixes the point
7
.
If we define the
y_{i}
and
v_{i}
recursively by

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle v_{i}=y_{i}/y_{i-1},\quad y_{0}=0,\ y_{1}=1,\ y_{i+1}=6y_{i}-y_{i-1},\ i\geq 1

we have
S(v_{i})=v_{i+1}
; note that
v_{i}\to a_{\min}=3+2\sqrt{2}
.
It is also useful to consider the points
w_{i}:=\frac{y_{i+1}+y_{i}}{y_{i}+y_{i-1}}=S(w_{i-1}),i\geq 1,
given by the sequence
7,41/7,\dots
.

Figure 2.2.
The complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
consists of classes with centers in
(w_{1},\infty)
; the reflection
R
takes this to a complete family with centers in
(v_{2},w_{1})
and
b<1/3
, and
S^{i}
shifts every such family
i
units to the left, changing the sign of
\varepsilon
by
(-1)^{i}
. Note that
R
reverses
z
-order, while
S
preserves it. The blue intervals
[w_{i+2},w_{i+1}]
are blocked by the blocking classes
(S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})
for the appropriate
b
-value.

By
[
MM
, Lem.2.1.1]
we have

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle b_{\varepsilon,i+1}:=\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left(\frac{y_{i+1}}{y_{i}}\right)=\frac{y_{i+1}+y_{i}+3\varepsilon}{3y_{i+1}+3y_{i}+\varepsilon},

where
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, and we write
\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
for the appropriate branch of the inverse; thus

\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
has image in
(1/3,1)
if
\varepsilon=1
and in
(0,1/3)
if
\varepsilon=-1
.
It is shown in
[
MM
, Rmk. 1.2.5]
that for all
i\geq 0
, when
b>1/3
, the class
(S^{2i})^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})
blocks
v_{2i+2}
, while when
b<1/3
,

(S^{2i+1})^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})
blocks
v_{2i+3}
(we define the action of a symmetry on a quasi-perfect class below). Thus
b_{\varepsilon,i+1}
is blocked when either
\varepsilon=1
and
i
is odd or when
\varepsilon=-1
and
i
is even. However, the following
b
-values are not blocked, because they are in the closure of
Stair
by
[
MM
, Thm.1.1.1]
:

(2.3.9)

\displaystyle 1/5,\ 59/179,\dots,b_{-1,2i},\dots\qquad 11/31,\ 349/1045,\dots,b_{1,2i+1},\dots

These values of
b
with
\varepsilon=(-1)^{i}
will be called the
special rational
b
, and denoted by

(2.3.10)

b_{i}:=b_{(-1)^{i-1},i}\mbox{ so that }b_{2},b_{3},b_{4},\dots=1/5,11/31,59/179,\dots

We will see that each such
b
is the limit point of both ascending and descending sequences of
b
-values with staircases and hence is unobstructed; see Remark
2.3.6
(iii). However, none of the steps of these staircases remain live at one of these special
b
-values; indeed, according to Conjecture 1.2 in
[
CG-HMP
]
, these special
b
-values should not admit any staircases at all.

We extend the action of
S,R
to quasi-perfect classes
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q,t,\varepsilon)
via the formulas in (
2.1.1
) that define
d,m
as functions of
p,q,t,\varepsilon
, noting that both
S
and
R
preserve
t
and act on
\varepsilon
by
\varepsilon\mapsto-\varepsilon
. We denote this image by
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\bf{E}})
.
The main result of
[
MM
]
is that for each
i\geq 0,{\delta}\in\{0,1\}
the transformation
S^{i}R^{\delta}
takes the seeds and blocking classes in the staircase family
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
to the seeds and blocking classes of a staircase family that we denote

(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{S}}^{U})
. (See Remark
2.3.6
for a discussion of the slightly anomalous case
R^{\sharp}({\mathcal{S}}^{U})=:{\mathcal{S}}^{L}
.) Moreover, for all such
i,{\delta}
the image of a perfect tuple by
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}
is perfect.

We now extend this result by showing that these symmetries take each generating triple with centers in
(7,\infty)
to another generating triple.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
T=S^{i}R^{\delta}
for some
i\geq 0,{\delta}\in\{0,1\}
, and let

{\bf{E}}_{0}:=(d_{0},m_{0},p_{0},q_{0},t_{0},\varepsilon),\quad{\bf{E}}_{1}:=(d_{1},m_{1},p_{1},q_{1},t_{1},\varepsilon).

(i)

If
{\bf{E}}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{1}
are adjacent, then so are
T^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{0}),\ T^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{1})
.

(ii)

If
{\bf{E}}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{1}
are
t
-compatible, then so are
T^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{0}),\ T^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{1})
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
T=S
and that
p_{0}/q_{0}<p_{1}/q_{1}
.
Write
S^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{i}):=(d_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime},p_{i}^{\prime},q_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime})
,
i=0,1
. Since
S
preserves order, to prove (i) we must show that

(p_{0}+q_{0})(p_{1}+q_{1})-t_{0}t_{1}=8p_{0}q_{1}\Longrightarrow(p_{0}^{\prime}+q_{0}^{\prime})(p_{1}^{\prime}+q_{1}^{\prime})-t_{0}^{\prime}t_{1}^{\prime}=8p_{0}^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}.

But
t^{\prime}_{0}t^{\prime}_{1}=t_{0}t_{1}
, and

\displaystyle(p_{0}^{\prime}+q_{0}^{\prime})(p_{1}^{\prime}+q_{1}^{\prime})-8p_{0}^{\prime}q_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(7p_{0}-q_{0})(7p_{1}-q_{1})-8(6p_{0}-q_{0})p_{1}

\displaystyle=p_{0}p_{1}+q_{0}p_{1}-7p_{0}q_{1}+q_{0}q_{1}

\displaystyle=(p_{0}+q_{0})(p_{1}+q_{1})-8p_{0}q_{1}

as required. Thus the action of
S
, and hence of
S^{i}
, preserves adjacency.

Since
R
reverses order, we must show that, with
R^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{i}):=(d_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime},p_{i}^{\prime},q_{i}^{\prime},t_{i}^{\prime})
, we have

(p_{0}+q_{0})(p_{1}+q_{1})-t_{0}t_{1}=8p_{0}q_{1}\Longrightarrow(p_{0}^{\prime}+q_{0}^{\prime})(p_{1}^{\prime}+q_{1}^{\prime})-t_{0}^{\prime}t_{1}^{\prime}=8p_{1}^{\prime}q_{0}^{\prime}.

This holds because

\displaystyle(p_{0}^{\prime}+q_{0}^{\prime})(p_{1}^{\prime}+q_{1}^{\prime})-8p_{1}^{\prime}q_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(7p_{0}-41q_{0})(7p_{1}-41q_{1})-8(6p_{1}-35q_{1})(p_{0}-6q_{0})

\displaystyle=(p_{0}+q_{0})(p_{1}+q_{1})-8p_{0}q_{1}.

Now consider (ii). This holds because
S^{T}AS=R^{T}AR=A
for
A
defined in (
2.1
). In other words, this holds because
S,R
preserve
t^{2}
.
∎

Proposition 2.3.2
.

Let
\bigl({\bf{E}}_{{\lambda}},{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho}\bigr)
be a generating triple whose classes have centers in
(7,\infty)
.
Then for any
i\geq 1
, the classes

\bigl((S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{{\lambda}}),(S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{\mu}),(S^{i})^{\sharp}(({\bf{E}}_{\rho})\bigr),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bigl((S^{i}R)^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{\rho}),(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{\mu}),(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{{\lambda}})\bigr)

also form generating triples.

Notice here that because the action of
R
on the
z
-axis is orientation reversing, the order of the three entries in the second triple above is reversed.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.3.1
the action of
T=S^{i}R^{\delta}
preserves
t
and hence the required adjacency and
t
-compatibility conditions. Since the
t
values are unchanged by these symmetries, the equalities among them are preserved. Next observe that
\det S=1=-\det R
. Hence, expressions such as
q_{\mu}p_{\rho}-p_{\mu}q_{\rho}
are unchanged by
S
. Thus
(S^{i})^{\sharp}
preserves the three identities expressing
t
as a function of the relevant
p,q
.
The reader can check that
R^{\sharp}
also preserves these identities since the minus sign in the determinant is compensated for by the fact that
R^{\sharp}
interchanges the first and last elements of the triple.

It remains to check that the image triple
\bigl(\underline{{\bf{E}}}_{{\lambda}},\underline{{\bf{E}}}_{\mu},\underline{{\bf{E}}}_{\rho}\bigr)
satisfies the last condition, namely

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{acc}({\underline{m}}_{\rho}/{\underline{d}}_{\rho}),\mathrm{acc}({\underline{m}}_{{\lambda}}/{\underline{d}}_{{\lambda}})>\mathrm{acc}({\underline{m}}_{\mu}/{\underline{d}}_{\mu}).

Notice that

\displaystyle\frac{m}{d}=\frac{p+q+3\varepsilon t}{3(p+q)+\varepsilon t}>\frac{m^{\prime}}{d^{\prime}}=\frac{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}+3\varepsilon t^{\prime}}{3(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})+\varepsilon t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\varepsilon t(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})>\varepsilon t^{\prime}(p+q).

Therefore, because the function
b\mapsto\mathrm{acc}(b)
reverses orientation precisely if
\varepsilon=-1
, we may conclude that

(2.3.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{acc}\Bigl(\frac{m}{d}\Bigr)>\mathrm{acc}\Bigl(\frac{m^{\prime}}{d^{\prime}}\Bigr)\Longleftrightarrow t(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})>t^{\prime}(p+q).

In particular, in the initial triple
({\bf{E}}_{{\lambda}},{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
we have
\mathrm{acc}(m_{{\lambda}}/d_{{\lambda}}),\mathrm{acc}(m_{\rho}/d_{\rho})>\mathrm{acc}(m_{\mu}/d_{\mu})
so that

\frac{t_{{\lambda}}}{t_{\mu}}>\frac{p_{{\lambda}}+q_{{\lambda}}}{p_{\mu}+q_{\mu}},\quad\frac{t_{\rho}}{t_{\mu}}>\frac{p_{\rho}+q_{\rho}}{p_{\mu}+q_{\mu}},\quad p_{{\lambda}}/q_{{\lambda}}<p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}<p_{\rho}/q_{\rho}.

It follows from the formulas in
[
MM
]
that if
({\underline{p}},{\underline{q}})^{T}=S^{i}R^{\delta}(p,q)^{T}
, then
{\underline{p}}+{\underline{q}}=rp-sq
for some integers
r,s>0
. Therefore, because the symmetries preserve the
t
-coordinate,

\frac{p_{{\lambda}}+q_{{\lambda}}}{p_{\mu}+q_{\mu}}>\frac{{\underline{p}}_{{\lambda}}+{\underline{q}}_{{\lambda}}}{{\underline{p}}_{\mu}+{\underline{q}}_{\mu}}\Longrightarrow\ \mathrm{acc}(\frac{{\underline{m}}_{{\lambda}}}{{\underline{d}}_{{\lambda}}})>\mathrm{acc}\bigl(\frac{{\underline{m}}_{\mu}}{{\underline{d}}_{\mu}}\bigr),

But

\frac{{\underline{p}}_{{\lambda}}+{\underline{q}}_{{\lambda}}}{{\underline{p}}_{\mu}+{\underline{q}}_{\mu}}=\frac{rp_{{\lambda}}-sq_{{\lambda}}}{rp_{\mu}-sq_{\mu}}\ <\ \frac{p_{{\lambda}}+q_{{\lambda}}}{p_{\mu}+q_{\mu}}

if
s(p_{{\lambda}}q_{\mu}-q_{{\lambda}}p_{\mu})<r(q_{{\lambda}}p_{\mu}-p_{{\lambda}}q_{\mu})
which always holds because

q_{{\lambda}}p_{\mu}-p_{{\lambda}}q_{\mu}>0
by hypothesis.
A similar argument proves that
\mathrm{acc}({\underline{m}}_{\rho}/{\underline{d}}_{\rho})>\mathrm{acc}({\underline{m}}_{\mu}/{\underline{d}}_{\mu})
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 2.3.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a pre-staircase for
b=b_{\infty}
that is the image by some symmetry
S^{i}R^{\delta}
of a pre-staircase in the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
. If
b_{\infty}>1/3
then the ratios
m_{k}/d_{k}
decrease, while if

b_{\infty}<1/3
then the ratios
m_{k}/d_{k}
increase. In all cases,
\mathrm{acc}(m_{k}/d_{k})
decreases.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{S}}
is a principal pre-staircase in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
, then this holds by Lemma
2.1.12
(iii).
It therefore holds for all principal pre-staircases by (
2.3.11
) and the fact that
the function
\mathrm{acc}:[0,1)\to[a_{\min},\infty)
preserves order on the interval
(1/3,1)
and reverses it on
(0,1/3)
. We can interpret these order relations as saying that if the levels (in the sense of Lemma
2.2.1
) of the classes
{\bf{E}}_{k}
strictly increase, then the corresponding ratios
m_{k}/d_{k}
decrease when
m_{k}/d_{k}>1/3
(resp. increase when
m_{k}/d_{k}<1/3
). This suffices to prove the lemma, since the levels of the classes in any of the new pre-staircases in
Definition
2.2.7
strictly increase.
∎

Corollary 2.3.4
.

Theorem
1.2.6
(ii) holds.
Moreover, for every class
{\bf{E}}\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
, and every symmetry
T=S^{i}R^{\delta}
we have
I_{T^{\sharp}({\bf{E}})}=T(I_{{\bf{E}}})
.

Proof.

In light of Proposition
2.3.2
, the first claim will follow once we prove that
S
commutes with mutation and
R
switches
x
-mutation to
y
-mutation and vice versa. This is straightforward, e.g. to show
S^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{x\mu})
is the middle entry of
xS^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T})
, we compute

\displaystyle S^{\sharp}(t_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\rho},t_{\lambda}q_{\mu}-q_{\rho})

\displaystyle=(6(t_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\rho})-(t_{\lambda}q_{\mu}-q_{\rho}),t_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\rho})

\displaystyle=(t_{\lambda}(6p_{\mu}-q_{\mu})-(6p_{\rho}-q_{\rho}),t_{\lambda}p_{\mu}-p_{\rho}).

The remaining cases, including the analogous facts for the left and right entries, are left to the reader.

To prove the second claim, suppose that
{\bf{E}}
is the middle entry
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
in the triple
\mathcal{T}
. Then the end points of the blocked
z
-interval
I_{{\bf{E}}}
are the accumulation points of the principal pre-staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{x\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{y\mathcal{T}}_{u}
. The symmetry
T
takes the steps, and hence accumulation points, of these pre-staircases to the steps and accumulation points of the pre-staircases with blocking class
T^{\sharp}({\bf{E}})
.
Hence it takes the end points of
I_{{\bf{E}}}
to those of
I_{T^{\sharp}({\bf{E}})}
.
28
28

28

For further discussion of the action of the symmetries on
{\it Block}
see the end of
[
MM
, §1.2]
.

∎

Corollary 2.3.5
.

The image of each quasi-triple
\mathcal{T}^{n}_{\ell,seed},T^{n}_{u,seed}
under a symmetry
S^{i}R^{\delta}
is another quasi-triple.

Proof.

By the definition in Remark
2.1.14
, a quasi-triple satisfies all the condition of a triple except for (e). Therefore, this holds by the first paragraph of the proof of Proposition
2.3.2
.
∎

Remark 2.3.6
.

(i) The complete family
R^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
is somewhat anomalous because the class
R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})
is not geometric, since the reflection
R:z\mapsto(6z-35)/(z-6)
is not defined at
z=6
. On the other hand,
R(6/1)=1/0
, and if we define
d,m
via (
2.1.1
) we obtain the tuple
(d,m,p,q,t,\varepsilon)=(0,-1,1,0,3,-1)
.
As pointed out in
[
MM
, Rmk 2.3.4(ii)]
,
this is a numerically meaningful replacement for the class
R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})
. For example,
there is a descending staircase with blocking class

R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{1})=R^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{[8]})=(5,0,13,2,5,-1)

(and hence recursion parameter
5
) that is generated by the tuples

R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})=R^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{[6]})=(0,-1,1,0,3,-1),\qquad R^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{[7;4]})=(13,0,34,5,13,-1).

Its third step has center
\frac{169}{25}
and degree variables
(d,m)=(65,1)
.
This is the descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{L}_{u,1}
associated to the triple
R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
.
The corresponding ascending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{L}_{\ell,0}
with first two steps
R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}),R^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{[7;4]})
is the Fibonacci staircase. All the steps of the latter staircase have
m=0
, and there is no geometric blocking class. However, because by
Proposition
2.3.2
the three classes in
R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
satisfy all the numeric conditions to be a generating triple, we may define the
complete family
R^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
as before. Note that all its elements except for the steps in the
Fibonacci staircase have

m>0
. Further, with the sole exception of

R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})
, they all have have nonnegative entries with
p/q>a_{\min}
and so are blocking classes.

However, it is important to note that
R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})
is not geometrically meaningful, and so certain results do not apply to it. For example, the analysis of the steepness of a staircase following Proposition
3.1.7
(used to rule out potentially overshadowing classes) cannot apply to this class because it does not form an actual step for any
c_{H_{b}}
. In particular, any technique relying on
m/d
cannot be used with this class, because
m/d=-1/0
.

(ii) Fig.
2.2
illustrates the fact that the intervals
[w_{2i+2},w_{2i+1}],i\geq 0,
are blocked when
b>1/3
while their complements
[w_{2i+1},w_{2i}],i\geq 1,

are blocked when
b<1/3
.
To see that
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
blocks
[w_{2},w_{1}]
one only needs to observe that the accumulation point
z_{\infty}
of the descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0}
is
[7;\{1,5\}^{\infty}]>7=w_{1}
, and that the reflection
SR^{\sharp}
fixes the class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
. It follows that
w_{2}=SR(w_{1})
is greater than the limit point of the ascending staircase
(SR)^{\sharp}({\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0})
, so that
[w_{2},w_{1}]
is blocked by
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
. Then the rest of this claim follows by applying the symmetries.

(iii) The fact that the centers of the blocking classes
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
converge to
v_{1}:=\infty
as
n\to\infty
implies that the images of these classes under
R
and
S
have centers converging to
v_{2}:=6
.
These classes have ratios
m/d<1/3
that converge to
1/5
as
n\to\infty
.
However, they do not form staircases at
b=1/5
because (as is easy to check),
29
29

29

Formulas for the
(p,q,t)
entries for the blocking classes
{\bf{B}}^{L}_{n}=R^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n})
are given in (
3.1.11
); they have
(d,m)=(5n,n-1)
. Using (
1.2.3
) allows us to similarly compute the
d,m
coordinates of
S^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n})
.
the step corners are not visible at
b=1/5
since they fail the test
|bd-m|<\sqrt{1-b^{2}}
at
b=1/5
; see Lemma
1.2.1
. Similarly,
none of the classes in the complete families

S^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}),R^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
are live at
b=1/5
, since for each such class both
d
and the quantity
|b-m/d|

are larger than they are for the appropriate blocking class.
However, these classes do organize into
staircases whose accumulation points
(b_{\infty},z_{\infty})
converge to
(1/5,v_{2})
, both from the left (in the family

S^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
) and from the right (in the family

R^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
). Because the shifts
S^{i}
act on these staircases, a similar claim holds for the points
v_{2i}
with
b<1/3
and for
v_{2i+1}
for
b>1/3
. These are the only rational points on the
z
-axis that might be accumulation points of staircases, since all the others are blocked by Corollary
4.1.9
. In Section
4.3
, we show that these rational points
v_{i},i\geq 3,
do not have ascending staircases, but it remains unknown if they have descending staircases.
Similarly, we do not know if there is a descending staircase at
b=1/3
.

{\Diamond}

We will show in Proposition
4.1.3
that for any pair of adjacent classes
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
we have

{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=0
. Since the symmetries preserve adjacency, this implies that any pair of successive steps in any principal pre-staircase have zero intersection. In fact, as seen in the following lemma, regardless of adjacency, the symmetries
T:=S^{i}R^{\delta}
preserve intersection number.

Lemma 2.3.7
.

(i)

For quasi-perfect classes
{\bf{E}}_{1}
and
{\bf{E}}_{2}
with center greater than
5
, we have
S^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{1})\cdot S^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{2})={\bf{E}}_{1}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{2}
.

(ii)

For classes
{\bf{E}}_{1}
and
{\bf{E}}_{2}
with centers greater than
7
,
R^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{1})\cdot R^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{2})={\bf{E}}_{1}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{2}.

Proof.

For (i), consider

\displaystyle S^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{1})

\displaystyle=S^{\sharp}(d_{1},m_{1},p_{1},q_{1})=(D_{1},M_{1},P_{1},Q_{1})

\displaystyle S^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{2})

\displaystyle=S^{\sharp}(d_{2},m_{2},p_{2},q_{2})=(D_{2},M_{2},P_{2},Q_{2}).

Then, by
[
MM
, Lemma 2.1.5]
, for
i=1,2,

we have
30
30

30

See (
A.4
) for the definition of the
weight decomposition
W(p,q):=W(p/q)
.

W(P_{i},Q_{i})=W(6p_{i}-q_{i},p_{i})=(p_{i}^{\times 5},p_{i}-q_{i})\sqcup W(p_{i}-q_{i},q_{i}),

where by definition
W(p_{i}-q_{i},q_{i})
is
W(p_{i},q_{i})
with the first entry
q_{i}
removed.
As

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{1}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{2}=d_{1}d_{2}-m_{1}m_{2}-q_{1}q_{2}-W(p_{1}-q_{1},q_{1})\cdot W(p_{2}-q_{2},q_{2}),

we want to show

d_{1}d_{2}-m_{1}m_{2}-q_{1}q_{2}=D_{1}D_{2}-M_{1}M_{2}-5p_{1}p_{2}-(p_{1}-q_{1})(p_{2}-q_{2}).

To check this, we use
the formulas for
d_{i},m_{i},D_{i},M_{i}
in terms of
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
, except that
rather than the usual notation of including
\varepsilon
explicitly, we account for
\varepsilon
by letting
t_{1},t_{2}
be either positive or negative.
Thus, we have
S:t_{1}\mapsto-t_{1}
and
S:t_{2}\mapsto-t_{2}
.
We then obtain:

\displaystyle d_{1}d_{2}-m_{1}m_{2}-q_{1}q_{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{8}\bigl((p_{1}+q_{1})(p_{2}+q_{2})-t_{1}t_{2}\bigr)-q_{1}q_{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{8}(p_{1}p_{2}-7q_{1}q_{2}+q_{1}p_{2}+q_{2}p_{1}-t_{1}t_{2})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{8}\bigl((7p_{1}-q_{1})(7p_{2}-q_{2})-t_{1}t_{2}\bigr)-5p_{1}p_{2}-(p_{1}-q_{1})(p_{2}-q_{2})

\displaystyle=D_{1}D_{2}-M_{1}M_{2}-5p_{1}p_{2}-(p_{1}-q_{1})(p_{2}-q_{2}).

This completes (i). The proof of (ii) is similar and left to the reader.
∎

Remark 2.3.8
.

(i) Let
X_{\mathbb{Z}}=\{(p,q,t)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\ |\ p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}+8=t^{2}\}
.
It is not known whether there is an element
A\in{\rm GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
that induces a map
X_{\mathbb{Z}}\to X_{\mathbb{Z}}
that does not fix
t
.
Since there are integral triples
(p,q,t)\in X_{\mathbb{Z}}
for which neither pair of numbers
(d,m)
defined by (
1.2.3
) are integral, one
might also require that
A
preserve the integrality of the appropriate pair
(d,m)
.
The symmetries considered above are the only elements of this group that fix
t
(or equivalently fix the quadratic form
p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}
); see
[
MM
, Lem.2.1.3]
. Further, by
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.4]
they also preserve integrality.

(ii) We now discuss the properties of a ‘symmetry’
A
that is not in
{\rm GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
but has some interesting features.

Denote by
{\mathcal{C}}
the set of integral tuples
(d,m,p,q,t)
with
t>0
(but possibly with some negative entries) that satisfy the numeric conditions to be a quasi-perfect class with
\varepsilon=1
, i.e.

p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}+8=t^{2}
and
d,m
satisfy (
2.1.1
).
The subset with all entries nonnegative is denoted
{\mathcal{C}}^{+}
.
31
31

31

Although
{\mathcal{C}}^{+}\subset X_{\mathbb{Z}}
, the two sets are different since
X_{\mathbb{Z}}
contains elements for which
d,m
are not integers.

Consider the
transformation

\displaystyle A^{\sharp}:

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{C}}^{+}\to{\mathcal{C}},\quad(d,m,p,q,t)\to\bigl(m+3Q,d+Q,p-q+5Q,Q,p+q\bigr),

\displaystyle\hskip 130.08621pt\quad\mbox{ where }\ Q:=\tfrac{1}{2}(p-q-t).

It is not hard to check that
p+q+t
is even for all
{\bf{E}}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
so that
A({\bf{E}})
is always integral. One can check that it also satisfies the requirements to be in
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Notice that

\displaystyle A^{\sharp}(1,1,1,1,2)=(-2,0,-5,-1,2),\quad\mbox{ i.e. }\ A^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed})={\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed},

\displaystyle A^{\sharp}(n+3,n+2,2n+6,1,2n+3)=(n+5,n+4,2n+10,1,2n+7),\;

\displaystyle\hskip 144.54pt\mbox{ i.e. }A^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n})={\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+2}.

Thus
A^{\sharp}
acts on the seeds and blocking classes of the staircase family
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
. Because it is linear it therefore takes all steps in the ascending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n}
(that has blocking class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
and seeds

{\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n-1}
) to the corresponding descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n}
with blocking class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
and seeds
{\bf{E}}^{U}_{u,seed},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1}
. In particular, for all
n
it takes the step

{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7,2n+4]}
that

{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n}
shares with
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n+1}
to the corresponding shared step

{\bf{E}}_{[2n+9,2n+6]}
of the staircases

{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{\ell,n+1}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,n+2}
.
However, although
A^{\sharp}
takes each of the three classes in the generating triple
({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7,2n+4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1})
to another perfect class, the image classes

{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+2},{\bf{E}}_{[2n+9,2n+6]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+3}
do not form a generating triple. Correspondingly there are many classes descended from
({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7,2n+4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1})
whose image under
A^{\sharp}
is not perfect.
For example, consider the step
{\bf{E}}_{[7;5,2]}
after
{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}
in the descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0}
.
Since
{\bf{E}}_{[7;5,2]}=\bigl(38,24,79,11,34\bigr)
we have

A^{\sharp}\bigl(38,24,79,11,34\bigr)=\bigl(75,55,153,17,90\bigr),

with center
153/17=9
and hence corresponding quasi-perfect class

{\bf{E}}=75L-55E_{0}-17E_{1\dots 9}
. This class is not perfect because
{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}={\bf{E}}\cdot(4L-3E_{0}-E_{1\dots 8})=-1.
See also Remark
2.1.15
.

{\Diamond}

3.
The pre-staircases are live

In §
3.1
we develop a criterion for a quasi-perfect class to be perfect and then apply it, together with the results from
[
M1
]
to prove in Corollary
3.1.5
that all the classes in the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
, as well as their images under the symmetries are perfect.
Proposition
3.1.7
then explains why the principal pre-staircases are live. The proof here needs a new slope estimate that is proved in Lemmas
3.1.8
and
3.1.10
. We extend these arguments in §
3.2
to show that there are both ascending and descending staircases at every unblocked
b
, except possibly for the special rational
b
.

3.1.
The principal pre-staircases are live

We first establish some useful sufficient conditions for a quasi-perfect class to be an exceptional class and hence perfect.
The following result is extracted from the proof of
[
BHM
, Prop.42]
.

Lemma 3.1.1
.

Let
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
be a quasi-perfect class such that
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)
is live for all
b
in an open set
J\subset[0,1)
. Then
{\bf{E}}
is perfect.

Proof.

Because, by
[
BHM
, Lem.15(ii)]
,
c_{H_{b}}(p/q)
is the maximum
of the obstructions given by exceptional classes, for each
b\in J
there must be a exceptional class
{\bf{E}}_{b}^{\prime}
such that
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)=\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}_{b},b}(p/q)
.
We saw in

[
BHM
, Lem.15(i)]
that if the degree coordinates of
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}_{b}
are
(d^{\prime},m^{\prime})

where
|bd^{\prime}-m^{\prime}|<\sqrt{1-b^{2}}
,
then

V_{b}(z)<\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}_{b},b}(z)\leq V_{b}(z)\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{(d^{\prime})^{2}-(m^{\prime})^{2}}},

One can get from this an upper bound for
d^{\prime}
in terms of the ratio
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)/V_{b}(p/q).

Therefore, there can only be finitely many such classes
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}_{b}
, which implies that there is an exceptional class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime};{\bf{m}}^{\prime})

and an open subset
J_{b}
of
J_{\bf{E}}
such that

\displaystyle\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p/q)=\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q),\quad\forall\ b\in J_{b}.

Then we may write

{\bf{m}}^{\prime}={\lambda}{\bf{m}}+{\bf{n}}
where
{\bf{m}}=W(p/q)
is the weight expansion of
p/q
(i.e. the coordinates of
{\bf{E}}
)
and
{\bf{m}}\cdot{\bf{n}}=0
.
Since

\frac{{\bf{m}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf{w}}(p/q)}{d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b}=\frac{{\lambda}p}{d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b}=\frac{p}{d-mb}

for all
b\in J_{b}
, we must have
d^{\prime}={\lambda}d,\ m^{\prime}={\lambda}m
for some
{\lambda}>0
.
The identities

\displaystyle{\bf{m}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf{m}}^{\prime}-1=(d^{\prime})^{2}-(m^{\prime})^{2}={\lambda}^{2}(d^{2}-m^{2})={\lambda}^{2}(pq-1),

\displaystyle{\bf{m}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf{m}}^{\prime}-1={\lambda}^{2}{\bf{m}}\cdot{\bf{m}}+\|{\bf{n}}\|^{2}-1={\lambda}^{2}pq+\|{\bf{n}}\|^{2}-1

then imply that
\|{\bf{n}}\|^{2}=1-{\lambda}^{2}
. Therefore, unless
{\bf{n}}^{\prime}=0
so that
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}={\bf{E}}
we must have
0<{\lambda}<1
.
Further

{\bf{E}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf{E}}=d^{\prime}d-m^{\prime}m-{\bf{m}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf{m}}={\lambda}(d^{2}-m^{2}-{\bf{m}}\cdot{\bf{m}})=-{\lambda}.

But
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf{E}}
is an integer. It follows that
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}={\bf{E}}
, so that
{\bf{E}}
is perfect as claimed.
∎

We next show that every quasi-perfect class that intersects nonnegatively with every exceptional class is perfect. Although this follows from the general theory of exceptional curves in blowups of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, the proof below is self-contained, using only the positivity of intersections of distinct exceptional classes.
It is based on the following version of
[
BHM
, Prop.21(i)]
.
It shows that, for
b
less than and sufficiently close to
m/d
, the obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)
from a quasi-perfect class
{\bf{E}}
at its center is larger than
any other that is defined by an exceptional class with which it intersects nonnegatively.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

Suppose that the quasi-perfect class
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
has nonnegative intersection with the exceptional class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
. Then
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)>\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p/q)
for all
b\in\bigl(\frac{m^{2}-1}{md},\frac{m}{d}\bigr]
.

Proof.

Because
{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}\geq 0
we have

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}-{\bf{m}}\cdot{\bf{m}}^{\prime}\geq 0.

Therefore if
b\leq\frac{m}{d}
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bf{m}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf{w}}(p/q)}{d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b}\;\;\leq\;\;\frac{{\bf{m}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bf{m}}}{q(d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}\frac{m}{d})}\;\;\mbox{ since }b\leq\frac{m}{d}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d(dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime})}{q(dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime})}=\frac{d}{q}.

On the other hand, because
{\bf{w}}(a)\cdot{\bf{w}}(a)=\frac{p}{q}

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)=\frac{{\bf{m}}\cdot{\bf{w}}(a)}{d-mb}=\frac{p}{d-mb}>\frac{d}{q}\;\;\mbox{ if }\;\;pq>d^{2}-dmb.

Since
pq=d^{2}-m^{2}+1
this will hold if also

dmb>d^{2}-pq=m^{2}-1,

i.e.
b>\frac{m^{2}-1}{dm}.
Hence when
\frac{m^{2}-1}{dm}\leq b<\frac{m}{d}

we have
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)>\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p/q)
.
∎

Corollary 3.1.3
.

A quasi-perfect class
{\bf{E}}
such that
{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}\geq 0
for all
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}{\smallsetminus}{\bf{E}}
is perfect.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1.2

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}
is live at
p/q
for all
b
in an open set. Hence this follows from Lemma
3.1.1
.∎

The next result is key to the proof that all the classes considered here are perfect.

Lemma 3.1.4
.

Let
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q,t)
be a quasi-perfect class such that one of the endpoints of the associated blocked
b
-interval
J_{\bf{E}}
is unobstructed and irrational. Then
{\bf{E}}
is perfect.

Proof.

We suppose that
m/d>1/3
, leaving the case
m/d<1/3
to the reader.

Suppose first that the lower endpoint
b_{\infty}\in{\partial}J_{\bf{E}}
is unobstructed and irrational, and let
z_{\infty}:=\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})
.
Thus
c_{H_{b_{\infty}}}(z_{\infty})=V_{b_{\infty}}(z_{\infty})
because
z_{\infty}
is unobstructed, while

V_{b_{\infty}}(z_{\infty})=\mu_{b_{\infty}}(z_{\infty})=\frac{qz_{\infty}}{d-mb_{\infty}}

where the second equality holds by (
1.2.2
), and the first holds by continuity since

z_{\infty}
is the lower endpoint of
I_{{\bf{E}}}=\mathrm{acc}(J_{{\bf{E}}})
.
Therefore, by the scaling property
32
32

32

This says that for any target
X
and
{\lambda}>1
,
c_{X}({\lambda}z)\leq{\lambda}c_{X}(z)
; see
[
BHM
, eq;(1.1.1)]
.

we must have

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle c_{H_{b_{\infty}}}(z)=\frac{qz}{d-mb_{\infty}}=\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(z),\qquad z\in[z_{\infty},p/q].

Thus
{\bf{E}}
is live on
(z_{\infty},p/q]
for
b=b_{\infty}
. By Lemma
3.1.1
, it suffices to show that

{\bf{E}}
is live at
a=p/q
on some interval
[b_{\infty},b_{\infty}+\varepsilon)
.

If this is not true, there is an exceptional class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime},{\bf{m}}^{\prime})
such that
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}(p/q)=\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(p/q)
, while
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p/q)>\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)
for
b\in(b_{\infty},b_{\infty}+\varepsilon)
. By
[
BHM
, eq.(2.1.6)]
and
[
McS
, Prop.2.3.2]
a general obstruction function has the form

\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(z)=\frac{A+Cz}{d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b},

on the closure of any interval consisting of points
z
such that
\ell_{wt}(z)>\ell_{wt}({\bf{m}}^{\prime})
.
33
33

33

Because in this paper we use two notions of the length of a continued fraction, we here write
\ell_{wt}(p/q)
(rather than
\ell(p/q)
) to denote

\sum_{i=0}^{n}\ell_{i}
, where
p/q=[\ell_{0};\dots,\ell_{n}]
, and call it the
weight length
of
p/q
.
Further,
\ell_{wt}({\bf{m}}^{\prime})
is simply the number of elements in the tuple
{\bf{m}}^{\prime}
; see Appendix
A
.

Since
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
is obstructive at
p/q
, we know that
\ell_{wt}({\bf{m}}^{\prime})\leq\ell_{wt}(p/q)
by

[
BHM
, Lem.14.]
, while the fact that
{\bf{E}}
is obstructive on
[z_{\infty},p/q]
implies that

\ell_{wt}(p/q)\leq\ell_{wt}(z)
for all
z\in[z_{\infty},p/q]
. Therefore the above formula describes
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(z)
on the whole interval
[z_{\infty},p/q]
.

Next notice
that the slope of the function
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}(z)
for
z\in(p/q-\varepsilon,p/q)
must agree with that of
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(p/q)
: if it were smaller
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}(z)
would overwhelm

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(z)
for
z<p/q
, while if it were larger the scaling property would be violated when
z>b_{\infty}
. Therefore, the constant
A
above must vanish.

Thus if we now fix
z=p/q
and
b=b_{\infty}
we have

\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}(p/q)=\frac{Cp}{q(d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b_{\infty})}=\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(p/q)=\frac{p}{d-mb_{\infty}},

which implies that

C(d-mb_{\infty})=q(d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b_{\infty}).

Since
b_{\infty}
is irrational by Remark
2.1.3
(i), this is impossible unless
Cm=qm^{\prime},\ Cd=qd^{\prime}
.
Hence

\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p/q)=\frac{Cp}{q(d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b)}=\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)=\frac{p}{d-mb},\quad\mbox{ for all }\ b,

and in particular for
b\approx b_{\infty}
.
Therefore
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)
is live at
p/q
for
b\in[b_{\infty},b_{\infty}+\varepsilon)
, so that
{\bf{E}}
is perfect by Lemma
3.1.1
.

This proves the lemma when the lower endpoint is unobstructed. The proof when
(b_{\infty},z_{\infty})
is the higher endpoint is essentially the same. Again, we first argue that
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(z)
is live on
[p/q,z_{\infty})
and then show that if
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p/q)
is live for
b\in(b_{\infty}-\varepsilon,b_{\infty}]
the function
z\mapsto\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}(z)
must be constant on
[p/q,z_{\infty})
, and hence (by the irrationality of
b_{\infty}
) be given by the same formula as
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(z)
. It follows that the two obstructions must be equal as
b
varies.
∎

Corollary 3.1.5
.

Let
{\bf{E}}
be a quasi-perfect class in the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
or in one of its images under a symmetry
S^{i}R^{\delta}
. Then
{\bf{E}}
is perfect.

Proof.

The results of
[
M1
]
show that
for every class
{\bf{E}}
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}

the lower endpoint
b_{\bf{E}}
of the corrsponding blocked
b
-interval
J_{\bf{E}}
is unobstructed. Therefore
{\bf{E}}
is perfect by Lemma
3.1.1
. The
proof of the first claim is completed by
Lemmas 4.1.2 and 4.1.3 in
[
MM
]
that show that the image by the shift
S
(resp.
R
) of a perfect class whose coefficients
(d,m,p,q)
satisfy (
1.2.3
) is another perfect class whose coefficients
(d,m,p,q)
satisfy (
1.2.3
). Note that in the case of
R
we restrict consideration to classes with
p/q>7
; see the discussion in Remark
2.3.6
(i).
∎

Corollary 3.1.6
.

Let
{\bf{E}}
be a perfect class that occurs as a step in a principal pre-staircase. Then both endpoints of the corresponding blocked
b
-interval
J_{\bf{E}}
are unobstructed.

Proof.

Our assumptions imply that
for each endpoint
b_{\infty}
of
J_{{\bf{E}}}
there is a sequence of perfect classes
{\bf{E}}_{k}
with
p_{k}/q_{k}\to z_{\infty},\ m_{k}/d_{k}\to b_{\infty}
, where

z_{\infty}=\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})
. Moreover, because these classes form a staircase rather than a pre-staircase, the corresponding obstructions are live at the limiting
b
-value
b_{\infty}
. Then

[
BHM
, Lem.27]
implies
that
c_{H_{b_{\infty}}}(z_{\infty})
is the limit of the obstructions
\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{k},m_{k}/d_{k}}(p_{k}/q_{k})
. Moreover, because
d_{k}\to\infty
this limit is
V_{b_{\infty}}(z_{\infty})
.
∎

We next turn to the proof that the principal pre-staircases are live.
By
[
BHM
, Thm.51]
, there are three reasons why a sequence of perfect classes whose steps
p_{k}/q_{k}
converge to
z_{\infty}
may not form a staircase at
b_{\infty}=\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(z_{\infty})
.

(i)

The convergence
m_{k}/d_{k}\to b_{\infty}
may be so slow that there is no
k_{0}
such that the classes
{\bf{E}}_{k},k\geq k_{0},
are obstructive at their centers when
b=b_{\infty}
.

(ii)

There may be a sequence of obstructive classes each of which obscures
a finite number of steps.

(iii)

There may be an
overshadowing class
, i.e. a class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
whose obstruction function
z\mapsto\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}
goes through the accumulation point
\bigl(z_{\infty},V_{b_{\infty}}(z_{\infty})\bigr)
with sufficiently steep slope to obscure the step corners at
\bigl(p_{k}/q_{k},p_{k}/(d_{k}-m_{k}b_{\infty})\bigr)
for all
k\geq k_{0}
.

Here we say that the class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}

obscures
the step at
p_{k}/q_{k}
given by
{\bf{E}}_{k}
if there is
\varepsilon>0
such that
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}(z)>\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{k},b_{\infty}}(z)
either for
z\in(p_{k}/q_{k}-\varepsilon,p_{k}/q_{k})
or for
z\in(p_{k}/q_{k},p_{k}/q_{k}+\varepsilon)
. Thus if a prestaircase in
H_{b_{\infty}}
is live, infinitely many of its step classes
{\bf{E}}_{k}
are live for
b=b_{\infty}
and
z
in some neighborhood of the step center
p_{k}/q_{k}
.

By
[
MM
, Cor.4.2.3]
, problems (i), (ii) never happen for a recursively defined pre-staircase
because there is an upper bound on the degree of a class that could
obscure a step corner. On the other hand there could be an overshadowing class. In particular,
recall the following
identity (from
[
BHM
, (2.2.5)]
or
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.7]
)

(3.1.3)

\displaystyle V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))=\frac{1+\mathrm{acc}(b)}{3-b}.

When
b\in(1/5,5/11)
so that
\mathrm{acc}(b)<6
, the function
z\mapsto\frac{1+z}{3-b}
is the obstruction
from the special exceptional class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime\prime}=3L-E_{0}-2E_{0}-E_{1\dots 6}
when
z<6
. Therefore this obstruction always goes through the accumulation point
(\mathrm{acc}(b),V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))
.
If
{\mathcal{S}}
ascends, then this class causes no difficulties because
\frac{1+z}{3-b}<V_{b}(z)
when
z<\mathrm{acc}(b)
. However, we do need to check that this slope of this function is not steep enough to overshadow the steps of a descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}
with limit
z_{\infty}<6
.
Now, the slope
s_{k}({\mathcal{S}})

of the line segment from the accumulation point
\bigl(\mathrm{acc}(b),V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))\bigr)
to the outer corner
\bigl(\frac{p_{k}}{q_{k}},\frac{p_{k}}{d_{k}-m_{k}b}\bigr)
of the
k
th step is

s_{k}({\mathcal{S}}):=\frac{\frac{p_{k}}{d_{k}-m_{k}b_{\infty}}-\frac{1+\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})}{3-b_{\infty}}}{\frac{p_{k}}{q_{k}}-\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})}.

Therefore
\lim_{k}s_{k}({\mathcal{S}})>\frac{1}{3-b_{\infty}}
exactly if

(3.1.4)

\displaystyle b_{\infty}\bigl(m_{k}(p_{k}+q_{k})-p_{k}q_{k}\bigr)>d_{k}(p_{k}+q_{k})-3p_{k}q_{k},\quad\mbox{ for }k\geq k_{0}.

If this holds and if the pre-staircase has a blocking class, then as explained in the next result, we can use an arithmetic argument from
[
MM
]
to rule out the existence of an overshadowing class. The
case of nonrecursive
{\mathcal{S}}
is more complicated and is treated in §
3.2
.

Proposition 3.1.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}:=\bigl({\bf{E}}_{k}=(d_{k},m_{k},p_{k},q_{k},t_{k})\bigr)_{k\geq 0}
be a sequence of
recursively defined perfect classes such that
m_{k}/d_{k}
decreases with irrational limit
b_{\infty}>\tfrac{1}{3}
. Assume
{\mathcal{S}}
is associated to a blocking class
{\bf{B}}
with
t_{{\bf{B}}}\geq 3
,
and that, if
{\mathcal{S}}
