descends, the inequality (
3.1.4
) holds for some
k_{0}
.
Then

{\mathcal{S}}
is live, i.e.
H_{b_{\infty}}
has a staircase with steps
\bigl({\bf{E}}_{k}\bigr)_{k\geq k_{0}}
.
The same result holds if the
m_{k}/d_{k}
increase with irrational limit
b_{\infty}<1/3
.

Proof.

Since
{\bf{E}}_{k}
is perfect, it is live at its center
p_{k}/q_{k}
for
b=m_{k}/d_{k}
; see Lemma
1.2.1
. To prove the claim about
{\mathcal{S}}
, we must show that for large
k,

{\bf{E}}_{k}
remains live at the limiting value
b_{\infty}.
As explained above, by
[
MM
, Thm.4.2.1, Cor.4.2.3]

it suffices to rule out the existence of an overshadowing class.
This obstruction must be different from
z\mapsto\frac{1+z}{3-b}
; this holds for descending pre-staircases by assumption, and holds for ascending pre-staircases because the line
z\mapsto\frac{1+z}{3-b}
cuts through the volume curve from below and so is too steep. Therefore,
the three lines given by the obstructions from the overshadowing class, the blocking class, as well as the graph of
z\mapsto\frac{1+z}{3-b_{\infty}}
are distinct and
all go through the accumulation point
(b,\mathrm{acc}(b))=(b_{\infty},z_{\infty})
. But

b_{\infty},z_{\infty}
are both irrational by Remark
2.1.3
(i). In
[
MM
, Prop.4.3.7]
an arithmetic argument is used to show that such an overshadowing class
cannot exist.
This argument also crucially uses the fact that the
m_{k}/d_{k}
decrease when
b>1/3
and increase when
b<1/3
, which holds by Corollary
2.3.3
.
∎

It remains to prove that the principal pre-staircases satisfy (
3.1.4
). Lemma 4.2.7 in
[
MM
]

shows that this estimate holds for all descending pre-staircases
associated to the base triples
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\#}(\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*})
, except
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}
.
Rather than extending that asymptotic argument to cover more cases, we will prove that in most (but not all cases) the inequality in (
3.1.4
) holds with
k_{0}=0
, since that will be useful in §
3.2
where we prove Proposition
2.2.8
.
Although its main steps are given below, the proof also relies on some formulas and estimates that are established in Appendix B. To simplify our formulas we will denote the sum
p_{\bullet}+q_{\bullet}
by
r_{\bullet}
.

Lemma 3.1.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a descending principal pre-staircase with steps

(d_{k},m_{k},p_{k},q_{k},t_{k},\varepsilon),

k\geq 0
, and write
r_{k}:=p_{k}+q_{k}
.
Then, the inequality
(
3.1.4
) holds for a given
k_{0}\geq 0
if, for all
k\geq k_{0}
, one of the following equivalent conditions holds:

(3.1.5)

\displaystyle\frac{t_{k+1}^{2}-8}{t_{k+1}r_{k+1}}

\displaystyle>\frac{t_{k}^{2}-8}{t_{k}r_{k}},\quad\mbox{ or }

(3.1.6)

\displaystyle\frac{r_{k+1}}{t_{k}}-\frac{r_{k}}{t_{k+1}}

\displaystyle>\tfrac{1}{8}\bigl(t_{k}r_{k+1}-t_{k+1}r_{k}\bigr).

Proof.

Notice first that (
3.1.4
) holds exactly if

(3.1.7)

\displaystyle\bigl(m_{k}(p_{k}+q_{k})-p_{k}q_{k}\bigr)b_{\infty}>d_{k}(p_{k}+q_{k})-3p_{k}q_{k}.

If we substitute for
m_{k},d_{k}
in terms of
p_{k},q_{k}
using (
1.2.3
) we obtain

8d_{k}(p_{k}+q_{k})-24p_{k}q_{k}=3(t_{k}^{2}-8)+\varepsilon t_{k}r_{k},\quad 8m_{k}(p_{k}+q_{k})-8p_{k}q_{k}=(t_{k}^{2}-8)+3\varepsilon t_{k}r_{k}.

Since, by Lemma
B.1
(iii),
r_{k}\geq 3t_{k}\geq 3
when
\varepsilon=-1
,
these expressions are negative exactly when
\varepsilon=-1
.
Thus the condition in (
3.1.7
) is equivalent to

(3.1.8)

\displaystyle{b_{\infty}}

\displaystyle>\frac{3(t_{k}^{2}-8)+t_{k}r_{k}}{(t_{k}^{2}-8)+3t_{k}r_{k}}=3-\frac{8t_{k}r_{k}}{(t_{k}^{2}-8)+3t_{k}r_{k}}\quad\mbox{ if }\varepsilon=1,

\displaystyle{b_{\infty}}

\displaystyle<\frac{t_{k}r_{k}-3(t_{k}^{2}-8)}{3t_{k}r_{k}-(t_{k}^{2}-8)}=3-\frac{8t_{k}r_{k}}{3t_{k}r_{k}-(t_{k}^{2}-8)}\quad\mbox{ if }\varepsilon=-1.

As
k\to\infty
, the ratio
\frac{d_{k}(p_{k}+q_{k})-3p_{k}q_{k}}{m_{k}(p_{k}+q_{k})-p_{k}q_{k}}
converges to

\frac{D(P+Q)-3PQ}{M(P+Q)-PQ}=\frac{D(3D-M)-3(D^{2}-M^{2})}{M(3D-M)-(D^{2}-M^{2})}=\frac{M}{D}=b_{\infty},

where we use the identities in (
2.1.13
).
Therefore, the result will hold if we prove that for
k\geq k_{0}
the sequence on RHS of (
3.1.8
) is increasing when
\varepsilon=1
and decreasing when
\varepsilon=-1
.

Thus, when
\varepsilon=1
, we need
the sequence
\frac{t_{k}r_{k}}{(t_{k}^{2}-8)+3t_{k}r_{k}}
to decrease with
k
, or equivalently
the sequence
\frac{t_{k}^{2}-8}{t_{k}r_{k}}
to increase with
k
.
Similarly, when
\varepsilon=-1
the condition still is that

\frac{t_{k}^{2}-8}{t_{k}r_{k}}
should increase with
k
.
This proves the claim about condition (
3.1.5
). Finally, the inequality (
3.1.6
)
is just a rearrangement of (
3.1.5
).
∎

Lemma 3.1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
be any triple.
Then

(i)

If (
3.1.6
) holds for the first two terms
{\bf{E}}_{\rho},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
in
the associated descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
, then it also holds for these terms
in
{\mathcal{S}}^{x\mathcal{T}}_{u}
.

(ii)

If (
3.1.5
) holds for the first two terms
{\bf{E}}_{\rho},{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
in
the associated descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
, then it also holds for these terms
in
{\mathcal{S}}^{y\mathcal{T}}_{u}
.

Proof.

We must show that if the inequality

(3.1.9)

\displaystyle\frac{r_{\mu}}{t_{\rho}}-\frac{r_{\rho}}{t_{\mu}}>\tfrac{1}{8}(t_{\rho}r_{\mu}-t_{\mu}r_{\rho})

holds for
\mathcal{T}
then it also holds for
x\mathcal{T}
. Under this mutation
the RHS remains the same. Hence it suffices to check that

\frac{t_{\lambda}r_{\mu}-r_{\rho}}{t_{\mu}}-\frac{r_{\mu}}{t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}-t_{\rho}}>\frac{r_{\mu}}{t_{\rho}}-\frac{r_{\rho}}{t_{\mu}}.

When we multiply throughout by
t_{\mu}(t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}-t_{\rho})t_{\rho}
, the terms that are products of three factors cancel, and after dividing the remaining terms by
t_{\lambda}
, we obtain the inequality

t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}t_{\rho}r_{\mu}-t_{\mu}t_{\rho}r_{\rho}-t_{\rho}^{2}r_{\mu}>t_{\mu}^{2}r_{\mu}-t_{\mu}t_{\rho}r_{\rho}.

Now cancel the term
t_{\mu}t_{\rho}r_{\rho}
from both sides and divide by
r_{\mu}
to obtain the inequality in Lemma
B.1
(v). This proves (i).

Now consider (ii). We must show that
the inequality

\frac{t_{\mu}^{2}-8}{t_{\mu}r_{\mu}}>\frac{t_{\rho}^{2}-8}{t_{\rho}r_{\rho}}

persists under a
y
-mutation.
Since the RHS remains unchanged, it suffices to show that the LHS increases, i.e.

\frac{(t_{\rho}t_{\mu}-t_{\lambda})^{2}-8}{(t_{\rho}t_{\mu}-t_{\lambda})(t_{\rho}r_{\mu}-r_{\lambda})}>\frac{t_{\mu}^{2}-8}{t_{\mu}r_{\mu}}.

After simplifying, this reduces to the inequality

t_{\mu}(t_{\rho}t_{\mu}-t_{\lambda})(t_{\lambda}r_{\mu}-t_{\mu}r_{\lambda})<8\Bigl(t_{\rho}^{2}t_{\mu}r_{\mu}-(t_{\lambda}r_{\mu}+t_{\mu}r_{\lambda})t_{\rho}+t_{\lambda}r_{\lambda}-t_{\mu}r_{\mu}\Bigr).

Simplify and increase the LHS of this inequality by using the fact that

t_{\lambda}r_{\mu}-t_{\mu}r_{\lambda}=8q_{\rho}<\tfrac{4}{3}r_{\rho}.

Here the equality holds by Lemma
B.1
(iv), while
the inequality holds because

p_{\rho}/q_{\rho}>a_{\min}>5
so that
q_{\rho}<r_{\rho}/6

Next simplify and decrease the RHS by ignoring the term
+t_{\lambda}r_{\lambda}
and replacing

-(t_{\lambda}r_{\mu}+t_{\mu}r_{\lambda})t_{\rho}
by
-2t_{\mu}r_{\mu}t_{\rho}
, which is smaller because
r_{\lambda}<r_{\mu},t_{\lambda}<t_{\mu}
(see Lemma
B.1
(ii)).
These maneuvers show that, after cancelling the common factor of
t_{\mu}
,
it suffices to prove

\tfrac{1}{6}r_{\rho}(t_{\rho}t_{\mu}-t_{\lambda})<(t_{\rho}^{2}-2t_{\rho}-1)r_{\mu}.

We now show that
this inequality holds even without the term
-\tfrac{1}{6}r_{\rho}t_{\lambda}
on LHS. Indeed, after omitting this term and then rearranging,
we find that it suffices to prove

\tfrac{1}{6}\frac{r_{\rho}}{t_{\rho}}\Bigl(1-\frac{2}{t_{\rho}}-\frac{1}{t_{\rho}^{2}}\Bigr)^{-1}<\frac{r_{\mu}}{t_{\mu}}.

But because
t_{\rho}\geq 3
,
1-\frac{2}{t_{\rho}}-\frac{1}{t_{\rho}^{2}}>\frac{2}{9}
so that it suffices to check that

\frac{3}{4}\frac{r_{\rho}}{t_{\rho}}\leq\frac{r_{\mu}}{t_{\mu}}
. But this holds by Corollary
B.2
.
∎

Finally, we claim in Lemma
3.1.10
that the inequality (
3.1.5
) holds for the base cases. Its statement is
complicated by the fact that (
3.1.5
) does
not
hold for
\mathcal{T}_{*}^{0}
or its image by
R
. We give the proof now in the ‘easy’ cases that do not involve these triples; the proof is completed in Lemma
B.3
.

Recall from
(
2.1.14
) that the entries
(p,q,t)
in each of the classes in the base triple
\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*},n\geq 0,
are

(3.1.10)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}:\ (p,q,t):=(2n+6,1,2n+3),

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{\mu}:\ (4n^{2}+22n+29,2n+4,4n^{2}+16n+13),\quad{\bf{E}}_{\rho}:\ (2n+8,1,2n+5).

Since
R(p/q)=(6p-35q)/(p-6q)
and
R
fixes
t
while interchanging
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
, the corresponding entries for the triples
R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}),n\geq 0,
are

(3.1.11)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}:\ (p,q,t):(12n+13,2n+2,2n+5),

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{\mu}:\ (24n^{2}+62n+34,4n^{2}+10n+5,4n^{2}+16n+13),\ {\bf{E}}_{\rho}:\ (12n+1,2n,2n+3).

Lemma 3.1.10
.

(i)

Let
\mathcal{T}
be any triple of the form
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}),i\geq 0,{\delta}\in\{0,1\},
or one of the form
yx^{k}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}
for all
k\geq 0
. Then,
provided that
\mathcal{T}\neq\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*},R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
,
the inequality (
3.1.5
) holds for the first two terms in the associated descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
.

(ii)

The inequality (
3.1.5
) holds for the second and third terms in the descending pre-staircases associated to
y^{k}R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
,
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

Consider (i).
When
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}
or
R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}),n>0
, we can check that inequality (
3.1.5
) holds directly from the formulas in
(
3.1.10
), (
3.1.11
) above.
Next note that because

S(p/q)=(6p-q)/p
, the entries in
S^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
and
(SR)^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
are

(3.1.12)

\displaystyle\mbox{ for }S^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}):\;\;

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}=(35,6,3),\quad{\bf{E}}_{\mu}=(170,29,13),\quad{\bf{E}}_{\rho}=(47,8,5);

\displaystyle\mbox{ for }(SR)^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}):\;\;

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}=(76,13,5),\quad{\bf{E}}_{\mu}=(165,34,13),\quad{\bf{E}}_{\rho}=(6,1,3).

It is again easy to check that
(
3.1.5
) holds for
S^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
and
(SR)^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
.

We next claim that
if (
3.1.5
) holds for a triple
\mathcal{T}
then it also holds for
S^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T})
.
Since
S
fixes the parameter
t
, by rearranging (
3.1.5
) so that
the
t
terms are on one side and the
r
terms on the other, one finds that
S
preserves (
3.1.5
) provided that it decrease the ratio
r_{\mu}/r_{\rho}
.
Thus, we need

\frac{7p_{\mu}-q_{\mu}}{7p_{\rho}-q_{\rho}}<\frac{p_{\mu}+q_{\mu}}{p_{\rho}+q_{\rho}}
, which holds because

\frac{p_{\mu}}{q_{\mu}}<\frac{p_{\rho}}{q_{\rho}}
.

This completes the proof of (i) except for the claims about the
yx^{k}
-mutations of the exceptional triple

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}
. For these details and the proof of (ii), see Lemma
B.3
.
∎

Corollary 3.1.11
.

Every descending principal pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}
except
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{y^{k}R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})}_{u}
satisfies
the inequality (
3.1.4
) with
k_{0}=0
, and the pre-staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{y^{k}R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})}_{u}
satisfy (
3.1.4
) with
k_{0}=1
. Hence, every principal pre-staircase is live, and hence is a staircase.

Proof.

We proved that
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{u,0}
is live in
[
BHM
, Ex.70]
. All other descending pre-staircases are associated to some triple which is a mutation of one of the basic triples listed in Lemma
3.1.10
. Therefore, it follows from that result together with Lemmas
3.1.8
and
3.1.9

that the inequality (
3.1.4
) holds (with
k_{0}=0
for all triples except for
y^{k}R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
, where
k_{0}=1
). This proves the first claim. It now follows from Proposition
3.1.7
that every principal pre-staircase (both ascending and descending) is live.
∎

3.2.
Uncountably many staircases

We now prove Proposition
2.2.8
. We first discuss the ascending pre-staircases, which turn out to be relatively easy to deal with. As explained in Definition
2.2.7
, we denote by
{\mathcal{S}}^{\pm}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
any (ascending or descending) pre-staircase with limit point at
{\alpha}_{\infty}\in Z
, for simplicity omitting the decorations
n,i,{\delta}
that specify more precisely where it is.

In this case, the key to our argument is the following lemma, that explains the influence of the ratio
m/d
on the behavior of the corresponding obstruction.
This result applies to any pair of obstructive classes
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
. These have the form
{\bf{E}}:=dL-mE_{0}-\sum m_{i}E_{i}
(abbreviated as
(d,m,{\bf{m}})
) where

3d-m-\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}=1,\qquad d^{2}-m^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}^{2}=-1.

The corresponding obstruction function
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}
is piecewise linear, with the form
z\mapsto\frac{A+Cz}{d-mb}
in any interval consisting of points
z
with
\ell_{wt}(z)>\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}
; see (
A.3
) and
[
BHM
, §2.1]
. Moreover, if
z_{0}
is fixed, there is a constant
A_{0}=A(z_{0})
such that as a function of
b
the obstruction

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z_{0})
has the form
b\mapsto A_{0}/(d-mb)
.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
{\bf{E}}:=(d,m,{\bf{m}}),\ {\bf{E}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime},{\bf{m}}^{\prime})
be obstructive classes as above.
Then:

(i)

If
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{0}}(z_{0})=\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{0}}(z_{0})\geq V_{b_{0}}(z_{0})
for some
b_{0},z_{0}
, then

(3.2.1)

\displaystyle m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}<m/d\;\Longleftrightarrow\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}b}\Big|_{b=b_{0}}\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(z_{0})<\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}b}\Big|_{b=b_{0}}\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z_{0}).

(ii)

If
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}<m/d
and
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{0}}(z_{0})<\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{0}}(z_{0})
for some
b_{0}
, then

\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(z_{0})<\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z_{0})
for all
b>b_{0}
.

Proof.

As explained above, we may write

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z_{0})=\frac{A_{0}}{d-mb},\qquad\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(z_{0})=\frac{A^{\prime}_{0}}{d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b}.

But

\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}b}\Big|_{b=b_{0}}\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z_{0})=\frac{A_{0}}{d-mb_{0}}\ \frac{m}{d-mb_{0}}=\frac{1}{d/m-b_{0}}\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{0}}(z_{0}).

Note here that
d/m>1
while
b_{0}<1
. Therefore
\frac{1}{d/m-b_{0}}>\frac{1}{d^{\prime}/m^{\prime}-b_{0}}
iff
d/m<d^{\prime}/m^{\prime}
, which happens iff
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}<m/d
. This proves (i).

The calculation above also implies that if
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{0}}(z_{0})>\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{0}}(z_{0})
and
m/d>m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
then

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z_{0})
increases faster than
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(z_{0})
as
b
increases. Hence (ii) also holds.
∎

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be any triple
with center class
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
. Then:

(i)

If
b>1/3
the obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu},b}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
is live for all
b\in[{\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}}),m_{\mu}/d_{\mu}]
, and hence for all
b\in[{\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}}),{\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}})]
.

(ii)

If
b<1/3
the obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu},b}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
is live for all
b\in[m_{\mu}/d_{\mu},{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}})]
, and hence for all
b\in[{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}}),{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}})]
.

Proof.

First suppose that
b>1/3
.
Because
c_{H_{b}}
is unobstructed for
b\in{\partial}J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}}
by Corollary
3.1.5
, we can apply
[
BHM
, Prop. 42]
to show that the obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu},b}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
is live
for
b
in the blocked interval
J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}}
. It is also live at the lower endpoint
{\partial}^{-}J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}}
by (
3.1.2
). Next note that
\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu},b}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
is live
for
b=m_{\mu}/d_{\mu}
by
[
BHM
, Prop.21]
, where
m_{\mu}/d_{\mu}>{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}})
by
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.11]
.
If it were not live at some
b_{0}\in[{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}}),m_{\mu}/d_{\mu}\bigr)
, there would some exceptional class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
with degree coordinates
(d^{\prime},m^{\prime})
such that
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{0}}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})>\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu},b_{0}}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
. Therefore there would have to be
b_{1}<b_{2}
with
{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}})<b_{1}<b_{0}<b_{2}<m_{\mu}/d_{\mu}
at which the two obstructions are equal, with
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
growing faster than
\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu},b}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
at
b=b_{0}
and slower at
b=b_{2}
. But this contradicts
Lemma
3.2.1
. Now note that
m_{\mu}/d_{\mu}>{\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}})
because
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
is a step in a pre-staircase for
b={\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}})
with decreasing
m/d
values. This completes the proof.

A similar argument follows for
b<1/3
, where the order of
b
is reversed. In particular, the interval
J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}}
lies to the left of
J_{{\bf{E}}_{\mu}}
, and the sequence
m_{k}/d_{k}
increases for all principal pre-staircases by Lemma
2.1.12
(iii) with limit
{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}})
. Therefore we always have

m_{\mu}/d_{\mu}<{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}})
. Further details are left to the reader.
∎

Corollary 3.2.3
.

All the steps in each ascending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
are live at their centers when
b
equals
the limiting value

b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
.

Proof.

Let
{\bf{E}}
be a step in some ascending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
, and denote by
\mathcal{T}
the unique triple with middle step
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}={\bf{E}}
.
By construction the
z
-limit point of
{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
is at most
{\partial}^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}})
. Therefore if
b>1/3
the corresponding
b
-value is
\leq{\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}})
, and
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
is live at
b
by Lemma
3.2.2
(i). On the other hand if
b<1/3
then the
b
-value corresponding to the

z
-limit point of
{\mathcal{S}}^{+}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
is
\geq{\partial}^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}})
, and the conclusion now follows from
Lemma
3.2.2
(ii).
∎

The descending pre-staircases
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
present a more complicated problem.
According to the discussion before Proposition
3.1.7
,
the first step in the proof is to show that the steps are obstructive at the limiting
b
-value

b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
.
This is a consequence of the next lemma that shows that each class
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
is obstructive on the whole of the
b
-interval

(3.2.2)

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{T}}:=\mathrm{acc}^{-1}_{\varepsilon}(I_{\mathcal{T}}),\quad\mbox{ where }\ I_{\mathcal{T}}=\bigl({\partial}^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}}),\ {\partial}^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}})\bigr).

that lies between
J_{{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}}
and

J_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}}
.

Lemma 3.2.4
.

Each step
{\bf{E}}
of each descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
for
{{\alpha}_{\infty}}\in Z
is obstructive at its center at the limiting
b
-value
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
. Moreover, the obstruction
z\mapsto\frac{1+z}{3-b}
does not overshadow any step in
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
.

Proof.

Since
\frac{1+z}{3-b}>V_{b}(z)
for
z>\mathrm{acc}(b)
, the
first claim follows immediately from the second. To show the second, first suppose that
b>1/3
. We know from Lemma
3.1.10
that the inequality (
3.1.4
) holds for all steps (except possibly the first) of all descending principal staircases. Each step
{\bf{E}}
in
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
is the center step
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
of a unique triple
\mathcal{T}
. By definition, the next step in
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
may be written
{\bf{E}}_{y^{k}x^{i}\mu}
, where
i,k\geq 1
.
Thus its center lies strictly to the right of the lower endpoint
{\partial}^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}})
of
I_{\mathcal{T}}
, as do the centers of all subsequent steps. Hence the inequality (
3.1.4
), which holds for
b_{\infty}={\partial}^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}})
, continues to hold for
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}>b_{\infty}
.

When
b<1/3
both sides of (
3.1.4
) are negative, and a corresponding argument applies.
∎

The second step in the proof is to find, for each descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
, a uniform bound for the degree of a class that could obstruct any one of its steps
{\bf{E}}_{k},k\geq 0
.
We will treat the case
b>1/3
in detail; the changes needed for the case
b<1/3
are discussed in Remark
3.2.6
. In the following we use the notion of the
level
of a step that was defined in Lemma
2.2.1
.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

(i)

Let

{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
be a perfect class such that for some
0<x<b_{\infty}<m/d
we have

\frac{m}{d}>x(1+\frac{1}{d^{2}})
and

(3.2.3)

\displaystyle A(m,d,x):=\frac{m(m-xd)-1}{d(m-xd)-x}<b_{\infty}<m/d.

Then if
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
is any other perfect class with degree
d^{\prime}>1/(b_{\infty}-x)
, we have

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(p/q)>\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}(p/q)
.

(ii)

If
x_{0}<b_{\infty}<y^{\prime}:=m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}<y:=m/d<x_{1}
then

(3.2.4)

\displaystyle A(m,d,x_{0})<A(m^{\prime},d^{\prime},x_{0})

provided that, with
f(y)=\frac{1-x_{0}y}{y-x_{0}},y>x_{0}
, we have

(3.2.5)

\displaystyle md^{\prime}-m^{\prime}d<\Bigl(\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}-\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\Bigr)f(x_{1}),\quad\mbox{ and }

(3.2.6)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}\bigl(f(y^{\prime})-f(y)\bigr)<\Bigl(\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}-\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\Bigr)\bigl(f(y^{\prime})-f(x_{1})\bigr).

(iii)

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be any descending principal staircase in
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}\bigl({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}\cap[2n+6,2n+8]\bigr)
,
n\geq 0,
with recursion parameter
t_{\lambda}>3
and with
i+{\delta}
even so that
b>1/3
. Denote by

b_{\min},b_{\max}
the infimum (resp. supremum) of the
b
-values for these staircases. Then there are constants
x_{0}<b_{\min}<b_{\max}<x_{1}
and a level
\ell
such that
conditions (
3.2.5
), (
3.2.6
) hold whenever

(d,m),(d^{\prime},m^{\prime})
are the degree coordinates of a pair of adjacent steps in
{\mathcal{S}}
at level
\geq\ell
.

Proof.

It is shown in
[
BHM
, Prop.21(iii)]
if this inequality for
x=r/s
is satisfied any class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
such that

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(p/q)<\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}(p/q)
must have
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}<r/s
. Since in this case

\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}
is
obstructive we must have
|b_{\infty}d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}|<1
by
[
BHM
, Lem.15]
, which readily gives the bound
on
d^{\prime}
. This proves (i).

The inequality (
3.2.4
) states that even though
m/d
decreases to
b_{\infty}
along the staircase, the quantity
A(m,d,x_{0})

(which also has limit
b_{\infty}
) increases, a fact that is key to the argument in Corollary
3.2.7
.
Now, it is straightforward to check that
(
3.2.4
) is equivalent to the inequality

md^{\prime}-m^{\prime}d<\frac{d^{\prime}-x_{0}m^{\prime}}{m-x_{0}d}-\frac{d-x_{0}m}{m^{\prime}-x_{0}d^{\prime}},\quad\mbox{ when }\ 0<md^{\prime}-m^{\prime}d.

Since
x_{0}<b_{\infty}<y^{\prime}:=m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}<y:=m/d<x_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d^{\prime}-x_{0}m^{\prime}}{m-x_{0}d}-\frac{d-x_{0}m}{m^{\prime}-x_{0}d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}\frac{1-x_{0}y^{\prime}}{y-x_{0}}-\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\frac{1-x_{0}y}{y^{\prime}-x_{0}}

\displaystyle>\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}\frac{1-x_{0}y}{y-x_{0}}-\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\frac{1-x_{0}y^{\prime}}{y^{\prime}-x_{0}}.

Therefore, since
f(y)
is decreasing, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d^{\prime}-x_{0}m^{\prime}}{m-x_{0}d}-\frac{d-x_{0}m}{m^{\prime}-x_{0}d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle>\Bigl(\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}-\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\Bigr)f(y^{\prime})-\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}\bigl(f(y^{\prime})-f(y)\bigr)

\displaystyle>\Bigl(\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}-\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\Bigr)f(x_{1})\quad\mbox{by }\eqref{eq:rsb2}.

Therefore,
(
3.2.4
) holds if, in addition, (
3.2.5
) holds.
This proves (ii).

Now consider (iii). For simplicity, we begin by considering the family

{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}\cap[6,8]
.
Since each principal staircase is recursively defined, as was observed in
[
MM
, Cor.4.2.3]
there always is a constant
x<b_{\min}
such that (
3.2.3
) holds for all classes in that particular staircase.
As we shall see in Corollary
3.2.7
, the existence of such a constant
x
is enough to show that the pre-staircase is live unless it is overshadowed by a class of low degree.
34
34

34

Our earlier proof that the principal pre-staircases are live used a different argument.

The difficulty is that we want to find a single constant that applies to all staircases in this family.
It turns out that the descending staircase with blocking class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}

(and recursion parameter
3
) is exceptional and that we get better estimates if we exclude it.
Thus we will find constants
x^{\prime}_{0},x_{1},\ell
such that
conditions (
3.2.5
), (
3.2.6
) hold for any pair of adjacent steps at level
\geq\ell
in

{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
and where
\mathcal{T}
has

p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}>7
, and then take
x_{0}=\max(x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime\prime}_{0})
where
x^{\prime\prime}_{0}
is the lower constant for
the exceptional staircase.
Note that the value of
p_{\lambda}
is relevant to the question at hand because, by Lemma
B.1
(iii), the
ratio
md^{\prime}-m^{\prime}d=m_{\rho}d_{\mu}-m_{\mu}d_{\rho}>0
is fixed for all adjacent pairs of steps and equals
p_{\lambda}
.

We first claim that for any
x_{1}>b_{\max}
there is
\ell=\ell(x_{1})
such that (
3.2.6
) holds for all adjacent steps at levels
\geq\ell
. This holds because

-

the ratio
d^{\prime}/d
is
\geq t_{\lambda}-1
, where
t_{\lambda}\geq 13
is the recursion parameter of the staircase and
f
decreases, so that it suffices to show

f(y^{\prime})-f(y)<(1-(d/d^{\prime})^{2})\bigl(f(y^{\prime})-f(x_{1})\bigr)\leq 143/144\bigl(f(b_{\infty})-f(x_{1})\bigr):=C

whenever
y,y^{\prime}
are
m/d
-values for two successive steps in the staircase;

-

for some
c_{1},c_{2}>0
we have
-c_{1}\leq f^{\prime}(y)\leq-c_{2}<0
when
y\in[b_{\infty},x_{1}]
so that

|f(y^{\prime})-f(y)|<c_{1}|y^{\prime}-y|
;

-

we saw in (
2.2.5
) that adjacent steps
p/q
at level
\geq\ell
are less than a distance
\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}
apart; a similar argument applies to the ratios
m/d
, where we use the formula in Lemma
B.1
(iii) instead of the adjacency relation
|p/q-p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}|=t^{\prime\prime}/qq^{\prime}
.

Further details are left to the reader.

Next observe that by Lemma
B.4

\frac{d_{\mu}}{d_{\rho}}-\frac{d_{\rho}}{d_{\mu}}>t_{\lambda}-1.

Therefore, by (ii),
(
3.2.4
) will hold for a given
x_{0}<b_{\infty}
if we also choose
x_{1}
so that

(3.2.7)

\displaystyle p_{\lambda}/(t_{\lambda}-1)<\frac{1-x_{0}x_{1}}{x_{1}-x_{0}}.

Now, for every class
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
under present consideration we have
7<p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}<8
, so that

t_{\lambda}^{2}/p_{\lambda}^{2}=1-6q_{\lambda}/p_{\lambda}+(q_{\lambda}/p_{\lambda})^{2}+8/p_{\lambda}^{2}\geq 1/7.

Therefore, because we also have
t_{\lambda}\geq 5
,

p_{\lambda}/(t_{\lambda}-1)=\frac{p_{\lambda}}{t_{\lambda}}(1-1/t_{\lambda})^{-1}<\sqrt{7}\ \frac{5}{4}<4.

On the other hand we know by (
2.1.16
) that
1/2<{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}})\leq b_{\infty}<2/3
, where
2/3=m/d
for
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
.
Moreover, all step classes except
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}
have
m/d<2/3
by
Lemma
2.1.10
. But if
x_{0}=1/2,x_{1}=2/3
we have

\frac{1-x_{0}x_{1}}{x_{1}-x_{0}}=4
. Therefore in this case (
3.2.5
) holds with
x_{0}=1/2,x_{1}=2/3
.

To establish (iii) in the general case (still with
b>1/3
),
we first need to choose suitable upper and lower bounds
x_{0},x_{1}

for
b_{\infty}
,
which is done in Corollary
B.6
. Next notice that for any such family we always have
d^{\prime}/d>t_{\lambda}
, and again we can assume
t_{\lambda}\geq 13
by omitting the staircase with smallest
z
-accumulation point.
Hence
for given
x_{0},x_{1}
there always is a level
\ell
such that (
3.2.6
) holds for all staircases in the family. We then show in Lemma
B.8

that
(
3.2.7
) holds for
x_{0},x_{1}
as chosen above.
The argument given above then extends to complete the proof of (iii).
∎

Remark 3.2.6
.

Lemma
3.2.5
extends to the case
b<1/3
as follows.
If a staircase has
b<1/3
then the ratios
m/d
increase to
b_{\infty}
and we have the following analogs to the claims in this lemma.

(i
′
)

If

{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
is a perfect class such that for some
0<m/d<b_{\infty}<x
we have

\displaystyle m/d<b_{\infty}<\frac{m(xd-m)+1}{d(xd-m)+x}=:A^{\prime}(m,d,x),

then for any other perfect class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
with degree
d^{\prime}>1/(x-b_{\infty})
, we have

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b_{\infty}}(p/q)>\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{\infty}}(p/q)
.

(ii
′
)

If

x_{0}<y:=m/d<y^{\prime}:=m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}<b_{\infty}<x_{1}
then
A(m^{\prime},d^{\prime},x_{0})<A(m,d,x_{0})

provided that, with
f(y)=\frac{1-x_{0}y}{y-x_{0}}
where
y>x_{0}
as before,

(3.2.8)

\displaystyle m^{\prime}d-md^{\prime}

\displaystyle<\Bigl(\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}-\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\Bigr)f(x_{1}),

\displaystyle\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}\bigl(f(y)-f(y^{\prime})\bigr)

\displaystyle<\Bigl(\frac{d^{\prime}}{d}-\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\Bigr)\bigl(f(y^{\prime})-f(x_{1})\bigr).

(iii
′
)

There are constants
x_{0}<b_{\min}<b_{\max}<x_{1}<1/3
(where
b_{\min},b_{\max}
are the minimum, resp. maximum, of the
b
-values for these staircases) and a level
\ell

such that, if
{\mathcal{S}}
is any descending principal staircase in
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}\bigl({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}\cap[2n+6,2n+8]\bigr)
,
n\geq 0,
where
t_{\lambda}>5
and
i+{\delta}
is odd so that
b<1/3
,
the above
inequalities hold when

(d,m),(d^{\prime},m^{\prime})
are the degree coordinates of any two adjacent steps in
{\mathcal{S}}
at level
\geq k
.

The proofs of (i
′
) and (ii
′
) are analogous to those in the case
b>1/3
and are left to the reader.
As for (iii
′
), for fixed
x_{0},x_{1}
one can always choose
\ell
so that
the second inequality above holds. Also, just as before, the inequality (
3.2.8
) follows from
(
3.2.7
).
Therefore, to complete the proof it remains to establish (
3.2.7
), which is accomplished in Lemma
B.8
.
The fact that the values of
x_{0},x_{1}
in those lemmas are bounds for
b_{\infty}
again follows from Corollary
B.6
.

{\Diamond}

Corollary 3.2.7
.

A descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
is live unless it is overshadowed.

Proof.

First suppose that
m/d>1/3
, and consider a pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
in the family
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})\cap[2n+6,2n+8]
with steps
{\bf{E}}_{k}
and
i+{\delta}
even, and choose
x_{0},x_{1},\ell
as in Lemma
3.2.5
(iii). Then
x_{0}<b_{\min}
, where
b_{\min}
is the minimum of the
b
-values for the staircases in
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})\cap[2n+6,2n+8]
. Therefore, because
m_{k}/d_{k}>b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}\geq b_{\min}>x_{0}
there is a constant
d_{x_{0}}

that depends only on
x_{0}
such that

\frac{m_{k}}{d_{k}}>x(1+\frac{1}{d_{k}^{2}})
whenever
d_{k}\geq d_{\min}
.
Further, because there are only finitely many classes in this whole family that have level less than any fixed number
\ell
, we may suppose that
{\bf{E}}_{k}
has level
\geq\ell
.
Since the sequence
m_{k}/d_{k}
decreases with limit
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
, it then follows from
Lemma
3.2.5
(ii), (iii) and our choice of
x_{0}
that the sequence

A(m_{k},d_{k},x_{0})

increases, and it is easy to check that its limit is also

b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
. Therefore the inequality (
3.2.3
) with
b_{\infty}=b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
holds for all steps with
d_{k}\geq d_{x}
and level
\geq\ell
. Hence Lemma
3.2.5
(i) implies that the degree
d^{\prime}
of any class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
with
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}}(p_{k}/q_{k})\geq\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{k},b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}}(p_{k}/q_{k})
must be bounded above by
1/(b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}-x)\leq 1/(b_{\min}-x)
. But there are only finitely many exceptional classes of any given degree. Therefore
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
is live unless there is one class (whose obstruction would have to go through the accumulation point) that obsures infinitely many of its steps. Such a class is an overshadowing class.

The proof when
b<1/3
is very similar, with the statements in Remark
3.2.6
replacing those of Lemma
3.2.5
. Further details are left to the reader.
∎

Proposition 3.2.8
.

Every descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}:={\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
is live. Moreover, if
{\mathcal{S}}
belongs to the family
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})\cap[2n+6,2n+8]
there is a constant
D_{0}
that depends only on
n,i,{\delta}
such that any step in
{\mathcal{S}}
of degree
>D_{0}
is live.

Proof.

By
Lemma
3.2.5
(iii),
{\mathcal{S}}
is live unless it is overshadowed.
We will show that any class that overshadows
{\mathcal{S}}
must be a blocking class, and hence cannot exist since there are unblocked values of
b
on both sides of
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
.
For clarity we will consider the cases
b>1/3,b<1/3
separately. Hence
let us first suppose that
{{\alpha}_{\infty}}\in Z_{+1}
so that
{\mathcal{S}}
is a pre-staircase for some
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}>1/3
.

Suppose that at
b=b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
the obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(z)=\frac{A+Cz}{d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b}
from some class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
goes through the accumulation point
\bigl({\alpha}_{\infty},V_{b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}}({\alpha}_{\infty})\bigr)
. If
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
overshadows

{\mathcal{S}}
,
then we must have
C>A
since, by Lemma
3.2.4
, the obstruction is steeper than the function
z\mapsto\frac{1+z}{3-b}
mentioned in (
3.1.3
) above.
Let
w=w(b)
be the
z
-coordinate of the point of intersection of the line
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(z)=\frac{A+Cz}{d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b}
with the line
\frac{1+z}{3-b}
.
Then
w=\frac{(d^{\prime}-3A)-b(m^{\prime}-A)}{(3C-d^{\prime})-b(C-m^{\prime})}
so that

\frac{{\partial}w}{{\partial}b}=\frac{-(C-A)(3m^{\prime}-d^{\prime})}{\bigl((d^{\prime}-3C)-b(m^{\prime}-C)\bigr)^{2}}.

If
3m^{\prime}-d^{\prime}\geq 0
, then
\frac{{\partial}w}{{\partial}b}\leq 0
for all
b
, and so is
<\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))
which is
>0
when
b>1/3
.
Therefore, because
w(b)=\mathrm{acc}(b)
when
b=b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
, we must have
w(b)<\mathrm{acc}(b)
when

b>b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
. But then
the class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
blocks all
b
in some nonempty interval
(b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}},b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}+{\delta})
,
since the graph of
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}
crosses the line
z\mapsto\frac{1+z}{3-b}
before this line crosses the volume curve, so that
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))>V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))
.
But this is impossible, since by hypothesis there are unobstructed points arbitrarily close to
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
and on both sides of it.
Similarly, if
\frac{{\partial}w}{{\partial}b}>\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))
, the class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
will block
b
in
an interval of the form
(b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}-{\delta},b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}})
, which is again impossible.

Therefore, it remains to consider the case when

\frac{{\partial}w}{{\partial}b}=\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))
, which can happen only if
3m^{\prime}-d^{\prime}<0
.
In this case
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
will block some
b
near
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
unless

w(b)\geq\mathrm{acc}(b)
for all
b
near

b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
. We show below that in fact when the first derivatives agree we always have

\frac{{\partial}^{2}w}{{\partial}b^{2}}\Big|_{b=b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}}<\frac{{\partial}^{2}\mathrm{acc}(b)}{{\partial}b^{2}}\Big|_{b=b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}}.

But this implies that
w(b)<\mathrm{acc}(b)
for
b\in(b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}},b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}+{\delta})
,
so that as above such
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
would have to be a blocking class, and hence cannot exist.

To begin the argument, notice that we can assume that
1/3<b<5/11=\mathrm{acc}_{+}^{-1}(6)
.
Indeed, otherwise
b>0.61

so that the condition
|d^{\prime}b-m^{\prime}|<1
implies that
d^{\prime}\leq 3
, and there are no potential overshadowing classes of such low degree. Next observe that, by
Lemma
1.2.1
, because
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
is obstructive at
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}

we have

0<3(d^{\prime}b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}-m^{\prime})=d^{\prime}(3b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}-1)+d^{\prime}-3m^{\prime}<3.

Therefore, because
d^{\prime}-3m^{\prime}>0
by assumption, we must have
d^{\prime}-3m^{\prime}=1
or
2
, and if we write
\varepsilon:=d^{\prime}-3m^{\prime}
we have

w(b)=\frac{(3-b)(m^{\prime}-A)+\varepsilon}{(3-b)(C-m^{\prime})-\varepsilon}.

Since
m^{\prime}-A,C-m^{\prime}
are integers and
w(b)>5
the terms
(3-b)(m^{\prime}-A),(3-b)(C-m^{\prime})
, if nonzero, dominate
\varepsilon
and hence must have the same sign. Further,
because
0\leq A<C
and
w(b)>a_{\min}>5
,
we cannot have
m^{\prime}=A
. Indeed,
if
m^{\prime}=A
, then because
b\leq 5/11
and
\varepsilon=1,2
, we have

w(b)=\frac{\varepsilon}{(3-b)(C-A)-\varepsilon}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3-b-\varepsilon}<5
.

Next observe that, since
\varepsilon/(C-m^{\prime})\leq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}w}{{\partial}b}

\displaystyle=\frac{(C-A)\varepsilon}{\bigl((3-b)(C-m^{\prime})-\varepsilon\bigr)^{2}},\quad\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}^{2}w}{{\partial}b^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\partial}w}{{\partial}b}\ \frac{2(C-m^{\prime})}{(3-b)(C-m^{\prime})-\varepsilon}\leq\frac{{\partial}w}{{\partial}b}\ \frac{2}{(3-b)-\varepsilon/(C-m^{\prime})}<\frac{{\partial}w}{{\partial}b}\ \frac{2}{1-b}.

On the other hand, if
z(b):=\mathrm{acc}(b)
, then differentiating the equation
z+1/z=\frac{(3-b)^{2}}{1-b^{2}}-2
we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\bigr)\frac{{\partial}z}{{\partial}b}=\frac{2(3b-1)(3-b)}{(1-b^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=:F(b),\quad\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle\frac{2}{z^{3}}\Bigl(\frac{{\partial}z}{{\partial}b}\Bigr)^{2}+\bigl(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\bigr)\frac{{\partial}^{2}z}{{\partial}b^{2}}

\displaystyle=F^{\prime}(b).

Now solve the second equation for
\frac{{\partial}^{2}z}{{\partial}b^{2}}
, and simplify the term
(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}})^{-1}F^{\prime}(b)
by using the identity
\frac{1}{(1-b^{2})(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}})}=\frac{1}{2(3b-1)(3-b)}\frac{{\partial}z}{{\partial}b}
, to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}^{2}z}{{\partial}b^{2}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{2(1-b^{2})(5-3b)+4b(3-b)(3b-1)}{(1-b^{2})^{2}(3-b)(3b-1)}-\frac{{\partial}z}{{\partial}b}\frac{2}{z(z^{2}-1)}\Bigr)\ \frac{{\partial}z}{{\partial}b}.

Now suppose that
\frac{{\partial}z}{{\partial}b}=\frac{{\partial}w}{{\partial}b}
for some value of
b<5/11
. Then, because
z>a_{\min}
one can check that
\frac{{\partial}^{2}z}{{\partial}^{2}b}>\frac{{\partial}^{2}w}{{\partial}b^{2}}
because

\displaystyle\frac{2(5-3b)}{(1-b^{2})(3-b)(3b-1)}>\frac{20}{(3-b)(1-b^{2})}>\frac{2}{1-b},\quad\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle\frac{4b}{(1-b^{2})^{2}}>\frac{{\partial}z}{{\partial}b}\frac{2}{z(z^{2}-1)}=\frac{4z(3b-1)(3-b)}{(z^{2}-1)^{2}(1-b^{2})^{2}}.

This completes the proof in the case
b>1/3
.

The argument when
b<1/3
is similar, except that now
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
is smaller than
the recursively defined
b_{\infty}
for the staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}_{k}}_{u}
that contains
{\bf{E}}_{k}
, while both sides of (
3.1.4
) are negative. Therefore, as before, this inequality continues to hold at
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
. Further details are left to the reader.
∎

Corollary 3.2.9
.

Proposition
2.2.8
(i), (ii) holds.

Proof.

Claim (i) is proved in Corollary
3.2.3
, while (ii) follows immediately from Lemma
3.2.8
.
∎

4.
Proof of main theorems

We first develop some arithmetic properties of continued fractions, as preparation for the proofs of Theorems
1.1.1
parts (i), (ii), (iii) and
1.2.6
in §
4.2
. After a short discussion
of stabilization in §
4.4
, Corollary
4.3.5
gives the proof of
Theorem
1.1.1
part (iv).

4.1.
Arithmetic properties of perfect classes

Recall that from Lemma
2.1.2
(ii) that two quasi-perfect classes
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q,t,\varepsilon),\ {\bf{E}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},t^{\prime},\varepsilon)
(with the same
\varepsilon
-value)
are said to be adjacent iff
dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}=\min(pq^{\prime},p^{\prime}q)
. Our first aim in this section is to translate this condition into information on the continued fraction expansions of the centers
p/q,p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
.
We will use the notations and results from Appendix
A
; in particular
W(p/q)
denotes the
weight decomposition (
A.4
) of
p/q
.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

Let
p/q,p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}>1
, and write

CF(p/q)=[s_{0};\dots,s_{n}],\ CF(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})=[s^{\prime}_{0};\dots,s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}].

(i)

If the inequality

(4.1.1)

\displaystyle W(p/q)\cdot W(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})\geq\min(pq^{\prime},p^{\prime}q),

holds for
p/q=[s_{0};\dots,s_{n}]
and
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}=[s^{\prime}_{0};\dots,s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}]
then it also holds for

P/Q:=(kp+q)/p=[k;s_{0},\dots,s_{n}],\;\;\mbox{and }P^{\prime}/Q^{\prime}:=(kp^{\prime}+q^{\prime})/p^{\prime}=[k;s^{\prime}_{0},\dots,s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}].

Moreover, there is equality in (
4.1.1
) for
p/q,p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
if and only if there is equality for
P/Q
and
P^{\prime}/Q^{\prime}
.

(ii)

The inequality
4.1.1
holds for all pairs
p/q
and
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
.

(iii)

There is equality in (
4.1.1
) only if

s_{\alpha}=s_{\alpha}^{\prime}
for
0\leq\alpha\leq\min(n,n^{\prime})-1
.

Proof.

If we write

W([s_{0},\dots,s_{n}])=\bigl(q_{0}^{\times s_{0}},\dots,q_{n}^{\times s_{n}}\bigr),\quad W([s^{\prime}_{0},\dots,s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}])=\bigl((q_{0}^{\prime})^{\times s_{0}^{\prime}},\dots,(q^{\prime}_{n})^{\times s^{\prime}_{n}}\bigr)

then we have

\displaystyle W([k,s_{0},\dots,s_{n}])

\displaystyle=\bigl(p^{\times k},q_{0}^{\times s_{0}},\dots,q_{n}^{\times s_{n}}\bigr),

\displaystyle W([k,s^{\prime}_{0},\dots,s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}])

\displaystyle=\bigl((p^{\prime})^{\times k},(q_{0}^{\prime})^{\times s_{0}^{\prime}},\dots,(q^{\prime}_{n})^{\times s^{\prime}_{n}}\bigr).

Let us suppose that
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
so that

P/Q=(kp+q)/p>P^{\prime}/Q^{\prime}=(kp^{\prime}+q^{\prime})/(p^{\prime}).

Then, assuming that (
4.1.1
) holds for
p/q,p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle W([k,s_{0},\dots,s_{n}])\cdot W([k,s^{\prime}_{0},\dots,s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}])

\displaystyle=kpp^{\prime}+W([s_{0},\dots,s_{n}])\cdot W([s^{\prime}_{0},\dots,s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}])

\displaystyle\geq kpp^{\prime}+pq^{\prime}=p(kp^{\prime}+q^{\prime})=QP^{\prime}=\min(QP^{\prime},PQ^{\prime}).

Thus (
4.1.1
) holds for
P/Q,P^{\prime}/Q^{\prime}
and we either have equality in both cases or neither.
This proves (i).

Now consider (ii). By (i) it suffices to consider the case when
s_{0}\neq s_{0}^{\prime}
,
and we may assume that
s_{0}<s_{0}^{\prime}
so that

p_{0}/q_{0}<p^{\prime}_{0}/q^{\prime}_{0}
.
Then by (
A.6
)

\displaystyle W([s_{0},\dots,s_{n}])\cdot W([s^{\prime}_{0},\dots,s^{\prime}_{n}])

\displaystyle=\bigl(q_{0}^{\times s_{0}},\dots,q_{n}^{\times s_{n}}\bigr)\cdot\bigl((q_{0}^{\prime})^{\times s_{0}^{\prime}},\dots,(q^{\prime}_{n})^{\times s^{\prime}_{n}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq s_{0}q_{0}q_{0}^{\prime}+q_{1}q_{0}^{\prime}=(s_{0}q_{0}+q_{1})q_{0}^{\prime}=p_{0}q_{0}^{\prime}.

This proves (ii).

Now consider (iii). Again, by (i) it suffices to consider the case when
s_{0}\neq s_{0}^{\prime}
and, by renaming if necessary, we may assume

s_{0}<s_{0}^{\prime}
. We must show that equality holds only if either
n=0
or
n^{\prime}=0
.
If
q_{1}=0
then
p=s_{0},q=1
and

W([s_{0}])\cdot W([s^{\prime}_{0},\dots,])=s_{0}s_{0}^{\prime}=pq^{\prime}=\min(pq^{\prime},qp^{\prime}).

Otherwise
the weight decomposition
W([s_{0},\dots,s_{n}])
must have at least
s_{0}+2
terms, which means that there is equality only if
s_{0}^{\prime}=s_{0}+1
and
s_{1}^{\prime}=0
. Thus
n^{\prime}=0
, as required.
∎

Corollary 4.1.2
.

Suppose that there is equality in (
4.1.1
) and that

p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
. Then
s_{i}=s_{i}^{\prime}
for
0\leq i\leq\min(n,n^{\prime})-1
and

(i)

if
\min(n,n^{\prime})
is even, one of the following three possibilities occurs:

-

n=n^{\prime}
and
s_{n}<s_{n}^{\prime}
, or

-

n<n^{\prime}
and
s_{n}\leq s_{n}^{\prime}
, or

-

n>n^{\prime}
and
s_{n^{\prime}}=s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-1
.

(ii)

if
\min(n,n^{\prime})
is odd, one of the following three possibilities occurs:

-

n=n^{\prime}
and
s_{n}>s^{\prime}_{n}
, or

-

n<n^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}_{n}=s_{n}-1
,
or

-

n>n^{\prime}
and
s_{n^{\prime}}\geq s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Consider (i). As in the proof of Lemma
4.1.1
, we may suppose that
\min(n,n^{\prime})=0
.
Notice because we are forgetting an even number of terms, we still have
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
.
Therefore, if
n=0\leq n^{\prime}
, then
q=1,p=s_{0}
and

W([s_{0}])\cdot W([s_{0}^{\prime},\dots])=s_{0}q_{0}^{\prime}=pq^{\prime}.

Thus
the first two cases are clear. If
n>n^{\prime}=0
, then
s_{0}<s_{0}^{\prime}
,
q^{\prime}=1
and

\displaystyle W([s_{0},s_{1},\dots])\cdot W([s_{0}^{\prime}])

\displaystyle=(q_{0}^{\times s_{0}},q_{1}^{\times s_{1}},\dots])\cdot(1^{\times s_{0}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\geq s_{0}q_{0}+q_{1}=p=pq^{\prime}.

Moreover, by our conventions about continued fractions
W(p/q)
must have at least two terms after the initial block
q_{0}^{\times s_{0}}
, there is
equality only if
s_{0}^{\prime}=s_{0}+1
. This proves (i). The proof of (ii) is similar, and is left to the reader.
∎

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Let
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q,t,\varepsilon),\ {\bf{E}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},t^{\prime},\varepsilon)
be perfect classes.
If
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
are adjacent, then
W(p/q)\cdot W(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})=\min(pq^{\prime},p^{\prime}q)
and the conditions in
Corollary
4.1.2
hold. Further
{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=0
.

Proof.

A quasi-perfect class cannot be adjacent to itself because each such class satisfies
d^{2}-m^{2}=pq-1\neq pq
. Therefore we have
{\bf{E}}\neq{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
and hence
{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}\geq 0
because the classes are assumed to be perfect. Thus

\displaystyle 0\leq{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}-W(p/q)\cdot W(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})=\min(pq^{\prime},p^{\prime}q)-W(p/q)\cdot W(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}),

where the last equality holds by Lemma
2.1.2
(ii). By (
4.1.1
) this is possible only if

\min(pq^{\prime},p^{\prime}q)=W(p/q)\cdot W(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
so that
the conditions in Corollary
4.1.2
hold.
Further
{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=0
.∎

Lemma 4.1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triple in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
, and let

p_{k}/q_{k}=[s_{k,0};\dots,s_{k,n_{k}}],k\geq 0,
be the step centers in one of the associated staircases
{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell},
or
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
. Write

W(p_{k}/q_{k})=[q_{k,0}^{\times s_{k,0}},\dots,q_{k,n_{k}}^{\times s_{k,n_{k}}}],\ \ k\geq 0.

Then

(i)

The numbers
n_{k}:=\ell_{CF}(p_{k}/q_{k})
strictly increase.
35
35

35

Here,
\ell_{CF}
is the length of the continued fraction defined in (
A.3
)

(ii)

For all
k\geq 0
and
i<n_{k}
we have
s_{k+j,i}=s_{k,i}
for all
j\geq 1
.
Moreover for each
\ell
and
i\leq n_{\ell}
the weights
q_{k,i}
satisfy the recursion

q_{k+2,i}=tq_{k+1,i}-q_{k,i},\quad k\geq\ell,

where
t
is the recursion parameter of
{\mathcal{S}}
.

(iii)

The accumulation point of
{\mathcal{S}}
has infinite continued fraction
[s^{\mathcal{S}}_{0};\dots,s^{\mathcal{S}}_{i},\dots]
. Further each step satisfies

\displaystyle CF(p_{k}/q_{k})=[s^{\mathcal{S}}_{0};\dots,s^{\mathcal{S}}_{n_{k}-1},s^{\mathcal{S}}_{n_{k}}+{\delta}]

for some
{\delta}\neq 0
,
where
{\delta}=+1
if either
{\mathcal{S}}
ascends and
n_{k}
is odd, or
{\mathcal{S}}
descends and
n_{k}
is even.

Proof.

Consider a pair of steps
p_{k}/q_{k},p_{k+1}/q_{k+1}
. Since they are adjacent, Lemma
4.1.1
(iii) shows that
their continued fractions of lengths
n_{k},n_{k+1}
agree until the term in the
n
th place, where
n=\min(n_{k},n_{k+1})
. We want to rule out the possibility that
n:=n_{k+1}\leq n_{k}
.
Because the steps are adjacent and
t
-compatible, the recursion parameter
t
is given by
t=|p_{k+1}q_{k}-q_{k+1}p_{k}|
; and we may assume
t\geq 5
, since the only staircase in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
with
t=3
is
the descending stair with blocking class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
, which has the property in question here.
We will carry out the argument assuming that
that the staircase ascends, so that
p_{k}/q_{k}<p_{k+1}/q_{k+1}
, leaving the descending case to the reader.

We argue by contradiction. Thus, suppose that
n:=n_{k+1}\leq n_{k}
, and
write

\displaystyle W(p_{k}/q_{k})

\displaystyle=(q_{k,0}^{\times s_{0}},\dots,q_{k,n-1}^{\times s_{n-1}},q_{k,n}^{\times s_{n}},\dots),

\displaystyle W(p_{k+1}/q_{k+1})

\displaystyle=\bigl((q^{\prime}_{k+1,0})^{\times s_{0}},\dots,(q^{\prime}_{k+1,n-1}=m)^{\times s_{n-1}},1^{\times m}\bigr).

If
n
is odd, then Corollary
4.1.2
(ii) implies that
s_{n}\geq m
. But then
q_{k,n-1}\geq m
which implies that
q_{k,0}\geq q^{\prime}_{k,0}
, contrary to our hypothesis.
Hence
n
is even.

Next observe that there is a
2\times 2
matrix
A
with
\det A=1
such that

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}p_{k+1}&p_{k}\\
q_{k+1}&q_{k}\end{pmatrix}=A\begin{pmatrix}m&q_{k,n-1}\\
1&q_{k,n}\end{pmatrix},\quad A=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\begin{pmatrix}s_{i}&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&w\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore, because
p_{k+1}=tp_{k}-p_{k-1}>(t-1)p_{k}
, we have

p_{k+1}=xm+y>(t-1)(xq_{k,n-1}+yq_{k,n}).

Since the entries of
A
are nonnegative and
y<(t-1)yq_{k,n}
, we must have

xm>(t-1)xq_{k,n-1},\quad\mbox{ i.e. }\quad m>(t-1)q_{k,n-1}.

But also because
\det A=1
and
t=p_{k+1}q_{k}-q_{k+1}p_{k}
, we know that

t=mq_{k,n}-q_{k,n-1}
. Thus, writing
q_{k,n-1}=a,q_{k,n}=b
for simplicity, we have

t=mb-a
and

m>(t-1)a
so that
a+t=mb>(t-1)ab
. But
b\geq 1
, so that we need

t>(t-2)a
. Since
t\geq 5
and
a\geq 2
this cannot occur.

This completes the proof when the staircase ascends. The case of a descending staircase is essentially the same, except that
n
is now odd and
\det A=-1
. This proves (i).

The first claim in (ii) holds by (i) and Lemma
4.1.1
(iii).
Then the
second claim holds because, by (
A.4
), if
p/q=[s_{0},\dots,s_{n}]
the
i
th weight
q_{i}
of
p/q
depends only on

p,q=q_{0}(p/q)
and
s_{j}
for
j<i
.
Thus, because the relevant
s_{j}
do not depend on
k\geq k_{0}
, the weights
q_{k,i},i\leq n_{k_{0}},
satisfy the defining recursion of
{\mathcal{S}}
. Thus (ii) holds.

Towards (iii), note first that as explained in Remark
2.1.3
(i),
the accumulation point of
{\mathcal{S}}
is irrational and hence has infinite continued fraction. Next,
consider two adjacent terms

p_{k}/q_{k}=[s_{k,0};\dots,s_{k,n_{k}}],\quad p_{k+1}/q_{k+1}=[s_{k+1,0};\dots,s_{k+1,n_{k}},s_{k+1,n_{k}+1},\dots]

Since
{\bf{E}}_{k},{\bf{E}}_{k+1}
are adjacent, there is equality in (
4.1.1
) so that we may apply Corollary
4.1.2
. Therefore
s_{k+1,i}=s_{k,i}
for
i<n_{k}
.
Similarly, we have

s_{k+1,n_{k}}=s_{k+2,n_{k}}=s_{k+\ell,n_{k}}=s^{\infty}_{n_{k}},\quad\ell\geq 1.

Thus the general formula in (iii) holds, and we just have to check that
{\delta}=1
in the two given circumstances.
Suppose first that
{\mathcal{S}}
ascends, and that
n_{k}
is odd.
Then, in the language of Corollary
4.1.2
, we have
p/q=p_{k}/q_{k}<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}=p_{k+1}/q_{k+1}
so that
n=n_{k}<n^{\prime}=n_{k+1}
.
Then by part (ii) we have
s_{n}=s^{\prime}_{n}+1
which gives

s_{k,n_{k}}=s_{k+1,n_{k+1}}+1=s^{\infty}_{n}+1

as claimed. Similarly, if
{\mathcal{S}}
descends, and
n_{k}
is even, we have

p/q=p_{k+1}/q_{k+1}<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}=p_{k}/q_{k}
and
n>n^{\prime}=n_{k}
. Hence by (i) we have

s^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}=s_{n,k}=s_{n^{\prime}}+1=s_{n,k+1}+1=s^{\infty}_{n}+1,

as claimed.
∎

Corollary 4.1.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=({\bf{E}}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be any principal
36
36

36

Technically every staircase in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
is principal by Definition
1.2.7
, but we include “principal” here to highlight that these are not the staircases
{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha_{\infty}}
of Definitionn
2.2.7
, which for
\alpha_{\infty}\in[6,8]
consist of perfect classes in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
.
staircase in the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
or one its images under a symmetry. If
{\mathcal{S}}
has recursion parameter
t\geq 3
, then

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+1}=0,\qquad{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+2}=1,

\displaystyle{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+j+1}=t{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+j}-{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+j-1},\quad\forall j\geq 1,\ k\geq 0.

Proof.

Since the symmetries preserve adjacency by Lemma
2.3.1
, we know that every pair of classes

{\bf{E}}_{k},{\bf{E}}_{k+1}
are adjacent by Proposition
2.1.9
.
Therefore by Lemma
4.1.4
(ii), when
j\geq 1

we have

q_{k+j+1,{\alpha}}=tq_{k+j,{\alpha}}-q_{k+j-1,{\alpha}},\quad{\alpha}\leq\ell_{CF}(p_{k}/q_{k}).

Since these are the only terms in
W(p_{k+j}/q_{k+j})
involved in computing
{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+j}
, and
d_{k+j},m_{k+j}
satisfy the same recursion, we always have

{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+j+1}=t{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+j}-{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+j-1},\quad j\geq 1.

In particular, when
j=1
,
Proposition
4.1.3
implies that

{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+2}=t\ {\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k+1}-{\bf{E}}_{k}\cdot{\bf{E}}_{k}=0-(-1)=1.

This completes the proof.∎

Given a blocking class
{\bf{E}}_{\nu},\nu={\lambda},\rho
we denote the corresponding blocked
z
-interval by

({\partial}^{-}_{\nu},{\partial}^{+}_{\nu}):=I_{{\bf{E}}_{\nu}}
. We now show that in any derived triple
\mathcal{T}
the center point of the middle class
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
has the minimal weight length among all points in
(p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda},p_{\rho}/q_{\rho})
that are not blocked by
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
or
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
. (We can expand the interval
I_{\mathcal{T}}
in the conclusion of Proposition
4.1.6
to
(p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda},p_{\rho}/q_{\rho})
by the fact that the center of a perfect class
{\bf{E}}
has shortest weight length amongst points in
I_{\bf{E}}
by the fact that
I_{\bf{E}}
is contained in the interval on which
{\bf{E}}
is nontrivial, and on this interval the center has shortest weight length by
[
CG-HMP
, Lem. 2.28 (1)]
.)

Proposition 4.1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{T}:=({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}_{\mu},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
be any triple derived from one of the basic triples
({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7;2n+4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1})
. Then the weight length of the center
p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}
is strictly less than the weight length of any other point
p/q\in I_{\mathcal{T}}
.
37
37

37

The interval
I_{\mathcal{T}}=({\partial}^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}}),{\partial}^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}}))
is defined in (
3.2.2
).

Proof.

Let
p/q
be any rational number lying between the centers of
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
and
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
.
Write

CF(p/q)=[a_{0},\dots,a_{\ell}],\quad CF(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})=[s_{0},\dots,s_{n}].

and assume that

\ell_{wt}(p/q)=\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{\ell}a_{\alpha}\;<\;\ell_{wt}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})=\sum_{{\alpha}=1}^{n}s_{\alpha}.

We aim to show that
p/q
must either be
<{\partial}^{+}_{\lambda}
or
>{\partial}^{-}_{\rho}
.
Note that, if we define
{\alpha}_{0}:=\min\{{\alpha}\ |\ a_{\alpha}\neq s_{\alpha}\}
,
then we must have
{\alpha}_{0}\leq n
.

Suppose first that
{\alpha}_{0}<n
. Then we claim that
p/q
is either
<{\partial}^{+}_{\lambda}
or
>{\partial}^{-}_{\rho}
. This holds because, by Lemma
4.1.4
(iii) these limit points have infinite continued fractions with first
n_{k}-1
terms equal to those of
p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}
. Thus, for example, if
{\alpha}_{0}
is odd
and
a_{{\alpha}_{0}}<s_{{\alpha}_{0}}
then
p/q>{\partial}^{-}_{\rho}
, while if

{\alpha}_{0}
is odd
and
a_{{\alpha}_{0}}>s_{{\alpha}_{0}}
then
p/q<{\partial}^{+}_{\lambda}
. The other cases are similar.

Next suppose that
{\alpha}_{0}=n
. Then, because
\ell_{wt}(p/q)<\ell_{wt}(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
we must have

a_{n}<s_{n}
. We consider the cases
n
even or odd separately.
Suppose that
n
is even. Then
p/q<p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}
, and so we need to check that
p/q
is smaller than the accumulation point
{\partial}^{+}_{\lambda}
of the descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
. We saw in
Lemma
4.1.4
(iii) that in this case the last entry
s_{n}
in
CF(p_{\mu}/q_{\mu})
satisfies

s_{n}=s^{\mathcal{S}}_{n}+1
. Therefore,
a_{n}\leq s^{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
. But
a_{n}
is the last element in
CF(p/q)
while

CF({\partial}^{+}_{\lambda})
is infinite. Therefore
p/q<{\partial}^{+}_{\lambda}
by (
A.2
).

The argument when
n
is odd is similar, except that now one compares with the ascending staircase. Therefore such
p/q
cannot exist.
∎

Example 4.1.7
.

Here are some simple examples to illustrate the argument in Proposition
4.1.6
.
Consider the basic triple

\mathcal{T}_{*}^{0}:=({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1})
, and take
p/q=[7;3]
.
Then
{\partial}^{+}_{\lambda}=[7;\{5,1\}^{\infty}],{\partial}^{-}_{\rho}=[\{7,3\}^{\infty}]
by (
2.1.15
).
Since in this case
n=1
is odd we should have
p/q>[\{7,3\}^{\infty}]
, which can be readily checked.
Similarly, in the triple
x\mathcal{T}_{*}^{0}=({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},{\bf{E}}_{[7;5,2]},{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]})
one can calculate that
the limit of the ascending staircase is
[\{7,5,3,1\}^{\infty}]
so that,
for example,
[7;5]>[\{7,5,3,1\}^{\infty}]
is blocked by
{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}
.

{\Diamond}

Lemma 4.1.8
.

Every rational point in
[6,\infty)
lies in some
I_{\bf{E}}
for
{\bf{E}}\in{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
. In particular, no staircase with
b>1/3

accumulates to a rational point in
[6,\infty)
.

Proof.

Fix
n\geq 0
and consider the classes
{\bf{E}}_{\delta}
that belong to a triple with centers in
[2n+6,2n+8]
. Define

\displaystyle{\omega}_{k}:=\min\{\ell_{wt}(p/q)\ \big|\ p/q\mbox{ is the center of some }{\bf{E}}_{\delta}\mbox{ of level }k\}.

Thus

{\omega}_{1}=\ell_{wt}([2n+7;2n+4])=4n+11\geq 4n+1.

Since each center
p/q
of a class
{\bf{E}}_{\delta}
at level
k+1
is adjacent to a center at level
k
, it follows from Lemma
4.1.4
and Corollary
4.1.2
that
{\omega}_{k+1}\geq{\omega}_{k}+1
. Indeed,
Lemma
4.1.4
(i) shows that the CF-length of
p/q
is at least one more than that of the centers at level
k
and, even if
{\delta}=-1
, the last entry of
CF(p/q)
is
\geq 2
; thus the weight length increases by at least
1
. Hence
{\omega}_{k}\geq 4n+k
.
In other words the weight lengths of the centers at level
k
(which are minimal among the unblocked weight lengths) are all at least
4n+k
, so that all rational numbers of weight length
<4n+k
are blocked by classes at level
<k
. In particular, if
p/q
is any rational number with
\ell_{wt}(p/q)=K
then
p/q
is blocked by some class in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
at level
<K
.
∎

Recall the sequence
y_{0}=0,y_{1}=1,y_{2}=6,\dots,y_{i+1}=6y_{i}-y_{i-1}
and the associated points

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle v_{1}=\infty,\ v_{2}=6,\ v_{3}=35/6,\ \dots,\ v_{k}=y_{k}/y_{k-1}=S^{k-2}(v_{2}),\dots

from (
2.3.7
). The points

w_{k}=(y_{k}+y_{k-1})/y_{k-1}=7,\ 41/7,\ \dots

are also relevant; see Figure
2.2
.

Corollary 4.1.9
.

(i)

Every rational point in
(3+2\sqrt{2},\infty)
is blocked by a class with
m/d>1/3
,
except for the points
v_{2i+1},i\geq 1
which are unobstructed.

(ii)

Every rational point in
(3+2\sqrt{2},\tau^{4})
is blocked by a class with
m/d<1/3
,
except for the points
v_{2i},i\geq 1
which are unobstructed.

Proof.

The symmetry
R:p/q\mapsto(6p-35q)/(p-6)
maps the interval
[7,\infty)=[w_{1},v_{1})
to the interval
(6,7]=(v_{2},w_{1}]
and hence takes the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
to a complete family
R^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
whose blocking classes have
m/d<1/3
and block all rational points in
(6,7]\cap\mathrm{acc}([0,1/3))
.
Similarly, the symmetry
SR:p/q\mapsto(6p-35q)/(p-6)
maps the interval
[7,\infty)=[w_{1},v_{1})
to the interval
(v_{3},w_{2}]=(35/6,41/7]
and hence takes the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
to a complete family
(SR)^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
whose blocking classes have
m/d>1/3
and block all rational points in
(v_{3},w_{2}]
.
We proved in
[
MM
, Lem.3.4.6]
that the class
(S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})
blocks the interval
[w_{i+1},w_{i}]
for
b<1/3
when
i
is odd and for
b>1/3
when
i
is even. Hence the families

(S^{2i})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
and
(S^{2i+1}R)^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
for
i\geq 0
together block all rational points
for
b>1/3
except for the points
v_{3},v_{5},
and so on; see Figure
2.2
. Notice that the points
v_{2i+1}
are unobstructed since they are limits of accumulation points of staircases, which necessarily are unobstructed.

Similarly, the families

(S^{2i+1})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
and
(S^{2i}R)^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
for
i\geq 0
together block all rational points
for
b<1/3
except for the points
v_{2},v_{4},
and so on. Again, these points are unobstructed.
∎

Figure 4.1.
This diagram depicts the blocking classes between
6
and
8
through level five. The horizontal black lines represent the interval
[6,8]
. Each vertical line through level four is labeled with the continued fraction of a number between
6
and
8
which is the center of a blocking class.
To obtain the vertical line representing
E_{\mu}
from that of
E_{\lambda}
and
E_{\rho}
, draw a diagonal (gray) line from the top of the line representing
E_{\mu}
to the bottom of the line representing
E_{\rho}
, and vice versa. The vertical line dropped from the intersection of these two diagonals represents
E_{\mu}
.
Any unbroken gray “V” shape represents a generating triple. Each gray diagonal line represents a principal staircase associated to the blocking class at its lower endpoint; the steps are the classes whose upper endpoints are on the given diagonal.
Classes on the same horizontal line have the same
CF
-length, while classes whose upper endpoints are on the same level are depicted in the same color.

We end this subsection with an extended remark on the combinatorics and arithmetic properties of the family
\mathcal{CS}^{U}
on the interval
[6,8]
, since they are so similar to those of Farey sequences.

Remark 4.1.10
.

(Farey description of the blocking classes)

Suppose that
p/q\in[p_{1}/q_{1},p_{2}/q_{2}]
has
q>q_{1},q_{2}
.
The first Farey sequence
38
38

38

A subsequence of this sequence is relevant to the construction of the weight decomposition of
p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}
; see the Appendix to
[
McS
]
.
containing
p/q

consists of all rational numbers
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
in the interval
[p_{1}/q_{1},p_{2}/q_{2}]
with
q^{\prime}\leq q
, arranged in increasing order.
For example, in the case of
[7;4]\in[7,8]
the sequence is
is

[7],\ [7;4],\ [7;3],\ [7;2],\ [7;1,2],\ [7;1,3],\ [8];

similarly, the first Farey sequence between
[7]
and

[7;4]
containing
[7;5,2]
is

[7],[7;11],[7;10],[7;9],[7;8],[7;7],[7;6],[7;5,2],[7;5],[7;4,2],[7;4].

This sequence has the property that any three adjacent terms
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime},p/q,p^{\prime\prime}/q^{\prime\prime}
satisfy the identity
39
39

39

This expression is called the Farey sum of
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}/q^{\prime\prime}
.

\frac{p}{q}=\frac{p^{\prime}+p^{\prime\prime}}{q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}}=:\frac{p^{\prime}}{q^{\prime}}\oplus\frac{p^{\prime\prime}}{q^{\prime\prime}}.

Moreover, it turns out that the sequence can be constructed by repeatedly taking the Farey sum of two adjacent elements, discarding any with too large a denominator.

Proposition
4.1.6
implies that given any triple in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
the nearest neighbors to

p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}
in the first Farey sequence between
p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}
and
p_{\rho}/q_{\rho}
that contains
\rho_{\mu}/q_{\mu}
lie in the blocked intervals
I_{{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}},I_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}}
. Thus
p_{\mu}/q_{\mu}
is the Farey sum of these numbers.
For example,

[7;4]=\frac{29}{4}=\frac{7+22}{1+3}=:[7]\oplus[7;3],\quad[7;5,2]=[7;6]\oplus[7;5].

and
[7],[7;6]\in I_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}}

while
[7;3]\in I_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}}
, and
[7;5]\in I_{{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}}
.

{\Diamond}

Thus the class
E_{\mu}
can be viewed as a type of Farey sum of
E_{\lambda}
and
E_{\rho}
: given the blocked intervals
I_{{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}}
and
I_{{\bf{E}}_{\rho}}
, there is a unique rational number between them with shortest weight decomposition, which will also be the center of a blocking class.
In this language, the first part of Conjecture
2.2.4
claims that the
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length of the class
{\bf{E}}_{\delta}
is precisely the continued fraction length
\ell_{CF}
of its center. In particular, this would imply that

\ell_{CF}({\bf{E}}_{\lambda})+\ell_{CF}({\bf{E}}_{\rho})=\ell_{CF}({\bf{E}}_{\mu}),

which experimentally seems to be true. In fact there seems to be more internal structure here that comes from the relation of the adjacency condition to weight expansions explained in Lemma
4.1.1
.
For example, if a principal staircase has blocking class
{\bf{E}}_{\mu}
with center
[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n}]
then
in (almost) all cases we have calculated
40
40

40

The descending stairs associated to the blocking classes
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
are the only exceptions we have found.

the limit has the form

[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n-1},P_{\mu}^{\infty}]
where the periodic part
P_{\mu}
has length
2(n+1)
and is a combination in some order of the periodic parts
P_{\lambda},P_{\rho}
associated to
{\bf{E}}_{\lambda}
and
{\bf{E}}_{\rho}
.
We give examples in the following table.

center

{\partial}^{-}(I_{\bf{E}})

{\partial}^{+}(I_{\bf{E}})

{\bf{E}}_{0}=[6]

[\{5,1\}^{\infty}]

[7,\{5,1\}^{\infty}]

{\bf{E}}_{1}=[8]

[\{7,3\}^{\infty}]

[9;\{7,3\}^{\infty}]

{\bf{E}}_{.1}=[7;4]

[7;\{5,3,1,7\}^{\infty}]

[7;\{3,5,7,1\}^{\infty}]

{\bf{E}}_{.01}=[7;5,2]

[7;5,\{1,3,5,1,7,5\}^{\infty}]

[7;5,\{3,1,5,7,1,5\}^{\infty}]
