This pattern can be depicted just as the Farey diagram is recorded, and the two ways of depicting the Farey diagram (as in
[
H
, §1.2]
) emphasize two different features of the set of centers of our blocking classes. In the first diagram in Figure
2.1
, classes lying on the same horizontal line have equal level (see §
2.2
). In Figure
4.1
, however, classes lying on the same diagonal line lie in the same principal staircase, and classes lying on the same horizontal line have equal
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}
-length, thus equal
{CF}
-length (by Conjecture
2.2.4
).

Remark 4.1.11
.

If
b
is rational, then the accumulation point formula forces
\mathrm{acc}(b)
to be at worst a quadratic irrational, and hence to have periodic continued fraction. By Corollary
4.1.9
no rational number greater than six can be an accumulation point of a staircase.
If we knew enough about the numerics of the principal staircases, then by extending the arguments in §
3.2
one might be able to conclude that the only unblocked points
z
with periodic continued fraction are the endpoints of the blocked intervals. Since none of these correspond to a rational
b
by Remark
2.1.3
, it would follow that
the only possible rational
b
with staircases are
b=0,1/3
or the special rational
b
corresponding to the
z
in (
1.1.3
).

{\Diamond}

4.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.1.1

Our arguments are based on the following result.

Proposition 4.2.1
.

Let
{\bf{E}}
be a perfect class with
p/q>a_{\min}
such that the endpoints
{\partial}J_{\bf{E}}
are unobstructed, and let

{\bf{E}}^{\prime}\neq{\bf{E}}
be another perfect class with
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}>a_{\min}
. Then
J_{\bf{E}}\cap J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.

Remark 4.2.2
.

As noted in Remark
1.2.9
(i), the class
B=6L-3E_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{7}2E_{i}
is an exceptional class since it Cremona reduces to
(0,-1,0,\ldots)
, but not a perfect class because the coefficients of the
E_{i}
are twice those of the integer weight expansion of its center. This class illustrates the importance of the assumptions in Proposition
4.2.1
since, as we now show,
B
is a blocking class such that
\emptyset\neq J_{B}\subset J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}}.

Note first that by
[
BHM
, Thm.1]
we have

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}}=\left(\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2},\frac{3(7+\sqrt{5})}{44}\right)\approx(0.382,0.63),\qquad I_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}}=\bigl([\{5,1\}^{\infty}]],[7;\{5,1\}^{\infty}]\bigr).

Further, because the coefficients of
E_{1},\dots,E_{7}
in
B
are a constant multiple of the weight expansion of
7/1
, the proof of
[
BHM
, Prop. 21(i)]
(see also Lemma
3.1.2
) adapts to show that
B
is live at
z=7
for an interval of
b
that includes
(4/9,10/19)\approx(0.44,0.526)
; in other words
c_{H_{b}}(7)=\mu_{B,b}(7)=\frac{14}{6-3b}
for these
b
. We may also directly compute that
\mu_{B,\mathrm{acc}^{-1}_{+1}(7)}(7)\approx 3.368>3.354\approx V_{H_{\mathrm{acc}^{-1}_{+1}(7)}}(7)
. Thus
B
blocks
\mathrm{acc}^{-1}_{+1}(7)\approx 0.614
, which is contained in
J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}}
by (
4.2.1
).
On the other hand, the class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
has
\mu_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},b}(7)=\frac{6}{3-2b}
, so that

\mu_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},b}(7)=\mu_{B,b}(7)
for
b=3/5
. By comparing the ratios of
m/d
, it follows from
Lemma
3.2.1
(i) above that
the obstruction function for
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
grows faster at
z=7
than that of
B
as
b
increases from
3/5
to
{\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}})
. Since both these obstruction functions are constant for
z\geq 7
and
\mu_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},b}(z)=V_{H_{b}}(z)
at

z={\partial}^{+}(I_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}})>7
and
b={\partial}^{+}(J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}})>3/5
, it follows that
the class
B
is no longer obstructive
at these values of
z,b
.

The argument that
{\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}})<{\partial}^{-}(J_{B})
and that for
z<7
we have
\mu_{B,b}(z)<\mu_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},b}(z)
is easier, since for
z<7
the obstruction
\mu_{B,b}
lies on the line through the origin and
(7,\frac{14}{6-3b})
, while

\mu_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0},b}
is constant for
z\in[6,7]
and for
z<6
lies on the line from the origin to
(6,\frac{6}{3-2b})
.

Another exceptional class which is not perfect yet is live and blocking is
73L-46E_{0}-22E_{1}-21E_{2,\dots,7}-4E_{8,\dots,12}-1E_{13,\dots,16}
, with center
151/21
and whose blocked interval is nested inside that of
{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}
. Again because the coefficients of
E_{1},\dots,E_{16}
are closely related to the entries in the weight expansion of its center, we may use
[
BHM
, Prop. 21(i)]
to find an interval on which this class is live and an argument similar to the one above to show that
J_{{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}}
contains its blocked interval. We expect there are many such classes. This is in contrast to, for example, the class
5L-E_{0}-2E_{1,\dots,6}-E_{7}
, which is live for
b\approx 1/5
as explained in
[
BHM
, Ex. 34]
but is not a blocking class by
[
BHM
, (2.2.6)]
.

{\Diamond}

Since in Proposition
4.2.1
we assume that the endpoints of
J_{{\bf{E}}}
are unobstructed, the two intervals
J_{{\bf{E}}}
and
J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
cannot overlap, and the key point of the proof is to show that they are not nested: in other words we cannot have
J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}\subset J_{{\bf{E}}}
. The arguments to prove this are somewhat delicate. Hence we will begin the discussion
by using Proposition
4.2.1
to deduce
the main results stated in §1. We begin with a simple corollary of
Proposition
4.2.1
.

Corollary 4.2.3
.

(i)

Every perfect class
{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
with center in
(7,\infty)
is derived by mutation from one of the basic generating triples

\bigl({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n},{\bf{E}}_{[2n+7;2n+4]},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1}\bigr),\ n\geq 0
and so is a member of the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
.

(ii)

Every other perfect class with center
>a_{\min}
is the image of a perfect class in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
by a symmetry
S^{i}R^{\delta}
, for some
i\geq 0,\ {\delta}\in\{0,1\}
with
i+{\delta}>0
.

Proof.

Consider the union
{\mathcal{J}}
of the intervals
J_{\bf{E}}
that are blocked by some class
{\bf{E}}
in the complete staircase family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
.
Since all these classes are perfect by Corollary
3.1.5
, Proposition
3.1.7
implies that all the associated pre-staircases are live. Hence, by Corollary
3.1.6
, both
endpoints of the corresponding blocked
b
-interval
J_{\mathcal{E}}
are unobstructed.
41
41

41

In the proof that the classes are perfect we used the fact that the lower endpoint of
J_{\bf{E}}
is unobstructed; now we have a similar result for the upper endpoint.

We proved in Proposition
2.2.6
that
{\mathcal{J}}
is an open dense set of
\mathrm{acc}_{U}^{-1}([6,\infty))=[5/11,1)
. Therefore, the interval
J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
must have nonempty intersection with
{\mathcal{J}}
. But this is possible only if

J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
is contained in a component
J_{\bf{E}}
of
{\mathcal{J}}
. Proposition
4.2.1
then shows that
J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}=J_{{\bf{E}}}
, which implies that
I_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}=I_{\bf{E}}
. But then the two classes have the same breakpoint (since this is the point of shortest length in
I_{{\bf{E}}}
, see Lemma
4.2.4
), and hence coincide by the uniqueness result in
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.1]
. This proves (i).

To prove (ii), notice that, as illustrated in Figure
2.2
, the set of
z
-values blocked by
perfect classes with
m/d>1/3
and in some family
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
with
i+{\delta}
even is dense in the interval
(a_{\min},\infty)
. Hence the set of blocked
b
-values is also dense, and it follows as above that there are no other perfect classes with
m/d>1/3
. A similar argument applies to perfect classes with
m/d<1/3
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2.6
(iii).

By Corollary
4.2.3
(ii), it suffices to consider perfect classes in the complete family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
. Besides the classes
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
, each perfect class in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
is the middle entry in a unique triple and so a step in both the ascending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}
(live by Proposition
3.1.7
) and the descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}}_{u}
(live by Corollary
3.1.11
). For
n\geq 1
,
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}
is a member of both the descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}^{n-1}_{*}}_{u}
and the ascending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*}}_{\ell}
. The class
{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}
is a member of the ascending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}}_{\ell}
and the descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{(SR)^{\sharp}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}}_{u}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.2.2
.

We must show that
{\it Block}\subset[0,1)
is the disjoint union of the sets
J_{\bf{E}}
as
{\bf{E}}
ranges over all perfect classes with centers
>a_{\min}.
By Corollary
4.2.3
, the class
{\bf{E}}
must belong to one of the families
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
, and hence its endpoints are unobstructed. Therefore all these sets
J_{\bf{E}}
are disjoint by Proposition
4.2.1
.
∎

The statements in the first three parts of Theorem
1.1.1
are also now easy to prove.

Proof of Theorem
1.1.1
parts (i), (ii), (iii).

We must show that

(i)

Block
is an open dense subset of
[0,1)
that is invariant under the action of the symmetries.

(ii)

There are staircases at each end of each connected component of
Block
.

(iii)

For
n\geq 0
define

\displaystyle{\it Block}_{[2n+6,2n+8]}:=\bigl\{b\in{\it Block}\ \big|\ \mathrm{acc}(b)\in[2n+6,2n+8],\ b>1/3\bigr\}.

For each
n\geq 0
there is a homeomorphism of
{\it Block}_{[2n+6,2n+8]}
onto the complement
[-1,2]{\smallsetminus}C
of the middle third Cantor set
C\subset[0,1]
.

By Propositions
1.2.2
and
4.2.1
,

{\it Block}=\bigcup\bigl\{J_{\bf{E}}\ \big|\ {\bf{E}}\in(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}),i\geq 0,{\delta}\in\{0,1\}\ \big\}.

Since each class
{\bf{E}}
in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
is the center of a generating triple, it has two associated staircases that converge to the end points of
{\partial}J_{\bf{E}}
.
Therefore (ii) holds for the family
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
, and hence it also holds for the image of this family under any symmetry. Next notice that (i) follows from Proposition
2.2.6
and the fact that the image of the interval
[6,\infty)
under the symmetries
\bigl\{S^{i}R^{\delta}:i+{\delta}\;\mbox{ is even}\bigr\}
is dense in

(a_{\min},\infty)=\mathrm{acc}\bigl((1/3,1)\bigr)
, while the image under the symmetries

\bigl\{S^{i}R^{\delta}:i+{\delta}\;\mbox{ is odd}\bigr\}
is dense in
(a_{\min},\tau^{4})=\mathrm{acc}\bigl((0,1/3)\bigr)
.
Finally, we construct the homeomorphism in (iii) in the case
n=0
so that it takes the half-open intervals

I_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{0}}\cap[6,8]
and
I_{{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1}}\cap[6,8]
onto
[-1,0)
and
(1,2]
respectively, and more generally, takes the elements of
I_{{\bf{E}}_{\delta}}
onto the interior of the
interval consisting of all
x\in[0,1]
whose ternary decimal expansion starts with the entries in the decimal
{\delta}
. Here we are using the decimal notation introduced in Lemma
2.2.1
. Thus,

{\bf{E}}_{[7;4]}={\bf{E}}_{.1}
has image
(1/3,2/3)
while

{\bf{E}}_{[7;5,2]}={\bf{E}}_{.01}
has image
(1/9,2/9)
. The definition for the cases
n>0
is analogous.
∎

It remains to prove Proposition
4.2.1
.
Our first lemma describes useful properties of the elements
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}\in I_{{\bf{E}}}:=\mathrm{acc}(J_{{\bf{E}}})
.

Lemma 4.2.4
.

Let
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
. Then for all
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}\in I_{{\bf{E}}}
we have

p^{\prime}\geq p
and
q^{\prime}\geq q
.

Proof.

It is proved in
[
CG-HMP
, §2]
(see also
[
McS
, §2.2]
, or
[
BHM
, Lem.14]
) that
\ell_{wt}(p/q)<\ell_{wt}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
for all (rational)
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}\in I_{{\bf{E}}}{\smallsetminus}\{p/q\}
. In other words
p/q
is the unique point of shortest (weight) length in
I_{{\bf{E}}}
. Suppose that
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}\in I_{{\bf{E}}}
and write

CF(p/q)=[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n}],\quad CF(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})=[s^{\prime}_{0};s^{\prime}_{1},\dots,s^{\prime}_{n},\dots]\quad\ s_{n}\geq 2.

We claim that

\bullet

s_{i}=s_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i<n
.

This holds because otherwise at least one of the points
[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{i}+\varepsilon]
, where
\varepsilon=1,0,-1
would lie strictly between
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
and
p/q
and hence be in the interval
I_{{\bf{E}}}
, even though its length is
<\ell_{wt}(p/q)
. For example,
42
42

42

See the beginning of Section
4.1
for further information on ordering continued fractions.

if
p/q=[1;3,1,2]
and
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}=[1;2,8]
then
p/q<[1;3]<[1;2,8]
.

\bullet

s^{\prime}_{n}\geq s_{n}-1
, and if
s^{\prime}_{n}=s_{n}-1
then
\sum_{i>n}s_{i}^{\prime}\geq 2
. Hence
p^{\prime}>p,q^{\prime}>q
.

If
s^{\prime}_{n}\leq s_{n}-2
we can argue as before that the point

[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n}-1]
lies strictly between
p/q
and
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
. Further we cannot have

p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}=[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n}-1]
(since this has shorter weight
length than
p/q
), and so the sum of the subsequent entries

\sum_{i>n}s_{i}^{\prime}
must be at least two by our convention that the last entry in any continued fraction is at least
2
.

Thus
CF(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
has the stated form. Therefore at least one of the entries of
W(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
that correspond to the last block
1^{\times s_{n}}
of
W(p/q)
is
>1
. It now follows from the recursive definition of the weight sequence (where we construct it starting from the last block) that each entry in
W(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
is at least as large as the corresponding entry in
W(p/q)
. The result now holds because
p/q\neq p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 4.2.5
.

Let
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
be distinct perfect classes such that
J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}\subset J_{\bf{E}}
, and suppose also that both points in
{\partial}J_{\bf{E}}
are unobstructed. Then:

(i)

We must have

p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}\in I_{\bf{E}}
and
m/d<m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
.

(ii)

Further
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Because
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
is center-blocking by
[
MM
, Prop.2.2.9]
,
\mathrm{acc}_{U}^{-1}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})\in J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}\subset J_{\bf{E}}
so that

p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}\in I_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}\subset I_{\bf{E}}
, as claimed. Further, because
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}>J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
, we know that
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}>{\partial}^{-}(J_{{\bf{E}}})
.

Now suppose that
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}<p/q
. Because
{\partial}J_{\bf{E}}
is unobstructed,
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b^{-}}
is live on the interval
z\in I_{{\bf{E}}},z\leq p/q
for
b^{-}:={\partial}^{-}(J_{\bf{E}})
by
[
BHM
, Prop.42]
.
Therefore we must have
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b^{-}}(z)\leq\mu_{{\bf{E}},b^{-}}(z)
for
b=b^{-}
and
z
close to
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
. Moreover, because
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b^{-}}(z)
is constant for
z>p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
while
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b^{-}}(z)
is not, this inequality is strict when
z>p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
. On the other hand, Lemma
3.1.2
shows that
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}
dominates
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}
for
z
near
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
when
b
is sufficiently close to
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
. Therefore
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
must grow faster than
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
as
b
increases to
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
. Hence Lemma
3.2.1
(ii) shows that we must have
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}>m/d
as claimed in (i).

We next prove (i) when
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}>p/q
. If also
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}\leq m/d
, then the obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
changes no faster than
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
. Also we have

\displaystyle\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})\leq\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}),\quad\mbox{ when }b={\partial}^{+}J_{{\bf{E}}},\quad\mbox{ while }

\displaystyle\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})>\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}),\quad\mbox{ when }b\approx m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}.

It follows that we must have
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}<{\partial}^{+}J_{{\bf{E}}}
, since otherwise the obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
, which dominates at
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
, still dominates as
b
decreases from
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
to
{\partial}^{+}J_{{\bf{E}}}
since it decreases more slowly than
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})
as
b
decreases. (Note that
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
is rational and so cannot equal
{\partial}^{+}J_{\bf{E}}
, which is irrational.)
Next observe that because
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
we have

0\leq{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}-qq^{\prime}{\bf{w}}(p/q)\cdot{\bf{w}}(p^{\prime}/q^{\prime})\leq dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}-pq^{\prime}.

by Lemma
4.1.1
.

Thus we find that

\displaystyle\mu_{{\bf{E}},m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}}(\mathrm{acc}(m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{d-m\frac{m^{\prime}}{d^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle>V_{m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}}(\mathrm{acc}(m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}))\quad\mbox{since $\mu_{{\bf{E}},m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}}$ blocks $m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}\in J_{\bf{E}}$}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\mathrm{acc}(m^{\prime}/d^{\prime})}{3-m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}}>\frac{1+p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}}{3-m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}}

where the last inequality uses the fact that
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}<\mathrm{acc}(m^{\prime}/d^{\prime})
; see Remark
2.1.8
.
This simplifies to the strict inequality

\frac{q^{\prime}p}{dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}}>\frac{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}{3d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}}.

But
\frac{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}{3d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}}=1
, while
dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}\geq pq^{\prime}
by positivity of intersections.
Hence this is impossible, so that we must have
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}>m/d
. This proves (i).

It is straightforward to check that the condition
m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}>m/d
implies that
t^{\prime}(p+q)>t(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})
, i.e.

((p^{\prime})^{2}-6p^{\prime}q^{\prime}+(q^{\prime})^{2}+8)(p+q)^{2}>(p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}+8)(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})^{2}.

This simplifies to

(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})^{2}pq-p^{\prime}q^{\prime}(p+q)^{2}>(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})^{2}-(p+q)^{2},

or equivalently

(pp^{\prime}-qq^{\prime})(p^{\prime}q-q^{\prime}p)>(p^{\prime}+q^{\prime})^{2}-(p+q)^{2}.

If
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}<p/q
then
p^{\prime}q-q^{\prime}p<0
. On the other hand, Lemma
4.2.4
(i) implies that
p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}>p+q
, and
pp^{\prime}-qq^{\prime}>0
because
p>q,p^{\prime}>q^{\prime}
. Hence we must have
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
. This proves (ii).
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.2.1
.

Since the endpoints of
J_{{\bf{E}}}
are unobstructed, this interval must either be disjoint from
J_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime}}
or contain it. If the latter,
Lemma
4.2.5
shows that it suffices to consider the case when
p/q<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
and
m/d<m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
. Since
\mu_{{\bf{E}}^{\prime},b}
is live at
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
when
b=m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
while

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}
is live at
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
at the smaller value
b_{\infty}={\partial}^{+}J_{{\bf{E}}}
, there is some
b\in(b_{\infty},m^{\prime}/d^{\prime})
where the two obstruction functions agree. Thus, we have

\frac{p}{d-mb}=\frac{p^{\prime}}{d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}b},\quad i.e.\quad b=\frac{p^{\prime}d-pd^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}m-pm^{\prime}}.

If
p^{\prime}d-pd^{\prime}>0
then
p^{\prime}m-pm^{\prime}>0
and we have

\displaystyle\frac{p^{\prime}d-pd^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}m-pm^{\prime}}<\frac{m^{\prime}}{d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\;\Longrightarrow\;p^{\prime}dd^{\prime}-p(d^{\prime})^{2}<mm^{\prime}p^{\prime}-p(m^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle\;\Longrightarrow\;p^{\prime}(dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime})<p((d^{\prime})^{2}-(m^{\prime})^{2})

\displaystyle\;\Longrightarrow\;p^{\prime}pq^{\prime}<p(p^{\prime}q^{\prime}-1)\;\;\mbox{ by }\eqref{eq:weight},

which is impossible. Hence
p^{\prime}d-pd^{\prime}<0,\;\;p^{\prime}m-pm^{\prime}<0

which implies

p^{\prime}/p<d^{\prime}/d,\qquad p^{\prime}/p<m^{\prime}/m.

With
{\lambda}:=p^{\prime}/p
, write

d^{\prime}=({\lambda}+\varepsilon)d,m^{\prime}=({\lambda}+\varepsilon^{\prime})m
, where
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, and notice that because
p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}>p/q
we also have
q^{\prime}=({\lambda}-\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})q
for some
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}>0
.
Then

3({\lambda}d+\varepsilon d)={\lambda}p+{\lambda}m+\varepsilon^{\prime}m+{\lambda}q-\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}q

so that
3\varepsilon d=\varepsilon^{\prime}m-\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}q
, which implies that

\varepsilon^{\prime}m>3\varepsilon d
. Further,

\displaystyle\frac{pd^{\prime}-p^{\prime}d}{pm^{\prime}-p^{\prime}m}=\frac{p({\lambda}d+\varepsilon d)-{\lambda}pd}{p({\lambda}m+\varepsilon^{\prime}m)-m{\lambda}p}=\frac{\varepsilon d}{\varepsilon^{\prime}m}.

Thus, because
\varepsilon^{\prime}m>3\varepsilon d
, we find that

b_{\infty}<\frac{\varepsilon d}{\varepsilon^{\prime}m}<\frac{\varepsilon d}{3\varepsilon d}=\frac{1}{3}

which is impossible since
b_{\infty}>1/3
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.2.8
(i), (ii).

Claim (i) is proved in Corollary
3.2.3
. To prove (ii) it remains to consider the possibility that
all but finitely many of the steps
({\bf{E}}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
of some descending pre-staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
are not live at the limiting
b
-value
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
. Since there are no overshadowing classes by Lemma
3.2.8
, this can happen only if there are infinitely many exceptional classes
({\bf{E}}^{\prime}_{k})_{k\geq 0},
that are live at
b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
, each of which obscures a finite number of the step centers of
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
.
But then this new set of classes
({\bf{E}}^{\prime}_{k})_{k\geq 0},
forms a staircase for this
b
-value. Indeed, the break points of
these obstructions must converge to
{\alpha}_{\infty}
by
[
CG-HMP
, Thm.1.13]
.
∎

Remark 4.2.6
.

It seems likely that all but finitely many of the steps in
{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
are live at

b_{{\alpha}_{\infty}}
. However, we have not analyzed the properties of exceptional but non-perfect classes in enough detail to be able to make this claim.
{\Diamond}

4.3.
The special rational
b

By arguing as in Remark
2.3.6
(iii), one can show that if
b_{i}
is a special rational value with
\mathrm{acc}(b_{i})=v_{i}
as in (
2.3.10
) then none of the perfect classes with centers
>a_{\min}
are obstructive at
b_{i}
. However, there are other exceptional classes that affect the capacity function.
For example
{\bf{E}}=3L-E_{0}-2E_{1}-E_{2\dots 6}=(3,1;{\bf{m}})
with
{\bf{m}}=(2,1^{\times 5})
is such a class,
which seems to be live for
1/5<b<5/11
on various intervals above the accumulation point
\mathrm{acc}(b)
; see
[
MM
, Rmk.2.3.8]
for a discussion of its properties. Thus there could be some as yet undiscovered staircases that accumulate at these
b_{i}
from above.

We now show that these points
(b_{i},v_{i})
cannot be limits of ascending staircases.
The proof hinges on the properties of the third strand
43
43

43

See Example
2.1.7
for the definition of a strand of a staircase.
of the staircase at
b=1/3
that accumulates at
a_{\min}
.
This staircase is discussed in detail in
[
MM
, Example 2.3.7]
; see also Remark
2.1.14
.
It has a rather different structure from the staircases with accumulation points
>a_{\min}
discussed above, since it has three intertwined strands, all ascending but with alternating values of
\varepsilon
, so that successive steps need not be adjacent. Moreover, the three strands cannot be assembled into one ascending, recursively defined staircase.

We are mostly concerned here with the third strand that has initial seed

{\bf{E}}_{1}=(2,0,5,1)
with center
g_{1}/g_{0}=5/1
and
(t,\varepsilon)=(2,-1)
, and subsequent steps

{\bf{E}}_{i},i\geq 2,
with

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle\mbox{centers }\;\;S^{i-1}(g_{1}/g_{0})=g_{i}/g_{i-1},\quad t=2,\quad\varepsilon=(-1)^{i},i\geq 2

As usual the degree coordinates
d,m
are determined by (
1.2.3
); see
[
MM
, eq.(3.3.2)]
.
Since there seems to be no convenient reference in the literature we begin with the proof that
all the classes in this staircase are perfect.
We also show that these are the only perfect classes with centers
<a_{\min}
.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

The classes that form the steps of the staircase of
H_{1/3}
are perfect classes. Moreover these are the only perfect classes with centers
<a_{\min}
.

Proof.

As shown in
[
MM
, Example 2.3.7]
, the three seed classes for
H_{1/3}
are

{\bf{E}}_{seed,0}=(1,0,2,1,1,-1),\quad{\bf{E}}_{seed,1}=(2,1,4,1,1,1),\quad{\bf{E}}_{seed,2}=(2,0,5,1,2,-1).

The centers of the subsequent classes are given by applying
S
, so that the
next classes in the first two strands are
(5,2,11,2,1,1)
and
(10,3,23,4,1,-1)
.
One can easily check that these classes Cremona reduce.
44
44

44

An integral class
{\bf{E}}
such that
c_{1}({\bf{E}})=1,{\bf{E}}\cdot{\bf{E}}=-1
is exceptional if and only if it Cremona reduces. This is a transformation on the coefficients of the classes, which for a specific class is easily computed. More details about this process can be found in
[
McS
, Prop.1.2.12]
and
[
MM
, §4.1]
.

Thus, the initial classes are perfect. Furthermore, the rest of the classes
{\bf{E}}_{j}
are formed by applying the shift
S
to
{\bf{E}}_{j}
.
We then apply
[
MM
, Lem.4.1.2]
, which states that
S
preserves Cremona equivalence provided that the centers of the classes considered are
\geq 5
. Thus the rest of the classes
{\bf{E}}_{j}
also Cremona reduce, and hence are perfect.

Now suppose that
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
is
a perfect class with center
<a_{\min}
.
Taking
b=1/3
and using (
1.2.2
),
we obtain the inequality

(4.3.2)

\displaystyle c_{H_{1/3}}(p/q)\geq\frac{3p}{3d-m}=\frac{3p}{p+q}=\frac{3z}{1+z}.

On the other hand, the function
c_{H_{1/3}}(z),z\in[1,a_{\min}],
was fully calculated in
[
CG-HMP
, Prop.1.19]
. It is piecewise linear with outer corners at
the step centers on the graph of
z\mapsto\frac{3z}{1+z}
, and inner corners lying strictly below this graph. Therefore the class
{\bf{E}}
must have its center
p/q
at one of the steps of this staircase. Since perfect classes are determined by their centers by
[
MM
, Lem.2.2.1]
, this implies that
{\bf{E}}
must be one of these steps.
∎

We saw in
[
BHM
, Ex.22]
that
the obstruction function given by the class
{\bf{E}}_{1}=(2,0,5,1)
is constant and equal to

\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{1},b}(z)=5/(2-b)
for
z>5
, and
when evaluated at
b=1/5
goes through the point
(6,5/2)=\bigl(6,V_{1/5}(6)\bigr)
. Therefore it obstructs the existence of an ascending staircase at
z=6,b=1/5
. One might wonder
how the function
\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{1},b}
behaves
when
b\approx 1/5
since we know from
[
BHM
]
that there are sequences of values of
b
that converge to
1/5
from both sides that do admit ascending staircases.
45
45

45

These are the staircases in the
{\mathcal{S}}^{E}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{L}
families constructed in
[
BHM
, Thm. 54, 58]
. This behavior generalizes to the other special rational
b
as shown in
[
MM
]
.
However it turns out that for all
b\neq 1/5

the graph of the function
z\mapsto\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{1},b}(z)
, which is constant for
5<a_{\min}<z
, meets the volume curve
(z,V_{b}(z))
at a point
z_{0}(b)
that is strictly
<\mathrm{acc}(b)
and hence is not obstructive. We now apply
S
to show that a similar phenomenon occurs at all the special points
v_{i},i\geq 2
.

Proposition 4.3.2
.

For each
i\geq 1
, the class
{\bf{E}}_{i}
in (
4.3.1
) is not a blocking class, though its obstruction does go through the point
(\mathrm{acc}(b),V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b))
where
b
is the special rational point
b_{i+1}=\mathrm{acc}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(v_{i+1}),
where
\varepsilon=(-1)^{i}
.
In particular, the special rational
b
have no ascending staircase.

Remark 4.3.3
.

By (
4.3.1
), the class
{\bf{E}}_{i}
has
m_{i}/d_{i}>1/3
exactly if
i
is even
so that the corresponding rational
b
is
b_{i+1}>1/3
.
{\Diamond}

We begin the proof with the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3.4
.

Let
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
be a perfect class such that

\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)
is constant on the interval
[p/q,p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}]
and suppose that
p/q<\mathrm{acc}(b)<p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}
for some
b
.
Then
{\bf{E}}
blocks
b
only if
z^{\bf{E}}_{b}>\mathrm{acc}(b)
where

(4.3.3)

\displaystyle z^{\bf{E}}_{b}=\frac{(3p-d)-b(p-m)}{d-mb}.

Proof.

Our assumptions imply that
the function
(b,z)\mapsto\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)=\frac{p}{d-mb}
does not depend on
z
near
z=\mathrm{acc}(b)
,
so that we can think of
\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)
as a function of
b
only.
For each
b
the graph of
z\mapsto V_{b}(z)=\sqrt{\frac{z}{1-b^{2}}}
meets the graph of
z\mapsto\frac{1+z}{3-b}
at the point
z=\mathrm{acc}(b)
. It is easy to check that at this point the line has larger slope than the volume curve.
Thus if
{\bf{E}}
blocks
b
, the point
z^{\bf{E}}_{b}
where
\frac{p}{d-mb}=\frac{1+z}{3-b}
must be larger than
\mathrm{acc}(b)
. But this point is precisely given by the formula in (
4.3.3
). ∎

Proof of Proposition
4.3.2
.

Notice first that
g_{i}/g_{i-1}<v_{i+1}=y_{i+1}/y_{i}
for all
i\geq 1
. Indeed this holds when
i=1
since
g_{1}/g_{0}=5/1
and
v_{2}=6/1
,
and therefore it holds for all larger
i
since the shift
S
preserves orientation and increases the index
i
by
1
on each side.
A similar argument shows that
g_{i}=y_{i+1}-y_{i}
. We next claim that

\ell_{wt}(g_{i}/g_{i-1})<\ell_{wt}(y_{i+1}/y_{i})
for all
i
, since it holds when
i=1
and the calculation

S\bigl([5;x]\bigr)=S\bigl(\frac{5x+1}{x}\bigr)=\frac{29x+6}{5x+1}=[5;1,4,x],

where
x=[x_{0};x_{1},\dots]\geq 0
, shows that
applying
S
increases the weight length of any two numbers
z,z^{\prime}\in[5,6)
by the same amount.
Since
g_{i}/g_{i-1}<a_{\min}
, the hypotheses of
Lemma
4.3.4
holds.
Therefore the obstruction from the perfect class
{\bf{E}}_{i}=(d_{i},m_{i},g_{i},g_{i-1})
in (
4.3.1
) with center
g_{i}/g_{i-1}

is constant near
v_{i+1}
.
Hence by Lemma
4.3.4
it suffices to show that

z^{\bf{E}}_{b}\leq\mathrm{acc}(b)
where

\displaystyle z^{\bf{E}}_{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{(3(y_{i+1}-y_{i})-d_{i})-b((y_{i+1}-y_{i})-m_{i})}{d_{i}-m_{i}b}.

By (
4.3.1
),
8d_{i}=3(y_{i+1}-y_{i-1})+\varepsilon,\ 8m_{i}=(y_{i+1}-y_{i-1})+3\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon=(-1)^{i}
is as in (
2.3.10
). Further because
\mathrm{acc}(b)
is the solution to the equation

\mathrm{acc}(b)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{acc}(b)}=(3-b)^{2}/(1-b^{2})-2,

z^{\bf{E}}_{b}\leq\mathrm{acc}(b)
exactly if

\displaystyle z^{\bf{E}}_{b}+\frac{1}{z^{\bf{E}}_{b}}\leq(3-b)^{2}/(1-b^{2})-2.

That is, if

\displaystyle\frac{(z^{\bf{E}}_{b}+1)^{2}}{z^{\bf{E}}_{b}}\leq\frac{(3-b)^{2}}{1-b^{2}}

Since

\displaystyle z^{\bf{E}}_{b}+1

\displaystyle=\frac{3(y_{i+1}-y_{i})-d_{i})-b((y_{i+1}-y_{i})-m_{i})+d_{i}-m_{i}b}{d_{i}-m_{i}b}

\displaystyle=\frac{3(y_{i+1}-y_{i})-b(y_{i+1}-y_{i})}{d_{i}-m_{i}b}=\frac{(3-b)(y_{i+1}-y_{i})}{d_{i}-m_{i}b},

this simplifies to the inequality

(4.3.4)

\displaystyle(d_{i}-m_{i}b)\Bigl(3(y_{i+1}-y_{i})-d_{i}-b((y_{i+1}-y_{i})-m_{i})\Bigr)-(y_{i+1}-y_{i})^{2}(1-b^{2})\geq 0.

We claim that this inequality is equivalent to
(b-b_{i+1})^{2}\geq 0
where
b_{i+1}
is as in
(
2.3.10
). Thus we must show that for some constant
C
, the
coefficients of
b^{2},b,1
in (
4.3.4
) are respectively

C(3y_{i+1}+3y_{i}+\varepsilon)^{2},\quad-2C(3y_{i+1}+3y_{i}+\varepsilon)(y_{i+1}+y_{i}+3\varepsilon),\quad C(y_{i+1}+y_{i}+3\varepsilon)^{2}.

Since
d_{i},m_{i}
are given by linear expressions in the
y_{i}
and the
y_{i}
are even placed Pell numbers,
these are quadratic expression in the Pell numbers. But by

[
FM
, Lem.6.6]
these hold in general if and only if they hold for three distinct values of
i
. (Note that because the
y_{i}
correspond to even placed Pell numbers the sign
(-1)^{s}
in this result is always
=1
.)
But when
i=1,2,3
the quantities
(d_{i},m_{i},y_{i+1},y_{i},\varepsilon)
are equal to

(2,\ 0,\ 6,\ 1,\ -1),\quad(13,\ 5,\ 35,\ 6,\ 1),\quad(74,\ 24,\ 204,\ 35,\ -1)

and it is straightforward to check that the required identities hold with
C=1/16
. This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.3.5
.

Theorem
1.1.1
(iv) holds.

Proof.

This states that the only rational numbers that can be accumulation points of staircases are
the points
v_{i},i\geq 2
, and if there is such a stair it must descend. The first claim is proved in
Corollary
4.1.9

while the second is proved in Proposition
4.3.2
. ∎

Remark 4.3.6
.

Proposition
4.3.2
only considers the third strand of the staircase at
H_{1/3}
. The other two strands of the staircase at
H_{1/3}
have seeds
{\bf{E}}_{seed,0}=(1,0,2,1)
and
{\bf{E}}_{seed,1}=(2,1,4,1).
The rest of the classes are determined by taking
(S^{k})^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}_{seed,i}).
The classes in these two strands are also not blocking classes, but unlike the third strand, the obstructions from these strands do not go through the point
\bigl(\mathrm{acc}(b),V_{b}(\mathrm{acc}(b)\bigr)
for any
b.
Thus, these perfect classes do not obstruct either ascending or descending staircases for any value of
b.

{\Diamond}

The proof that
b_{2}=1/5
has no descending staircase in
[
BHM
, Thm.94]
shows that for some
{\delta}>0
and
z\in[6,6+{\delta})
, the capacity function
c_{1/5}(z)
equals the obstruction from the 19th ECH capacity, or equivalently, from the nonperfect exceptional class
(d,m;\mathbf{m})=(5,1;2^{\times 6},1)
. Experimental evidence suggests that the class
(3,1;2,1^{\times 5})
plays this role for all higher
i
.

Conjecture 4.3.7
.

For
i>2
,
c_{H_{b_{i}}}
has no descending staircase. Furthermore, for some
{\delta}>0
and
z\in[v_{i},v_{i}+{\delta})
, the capacity function
c_{H_{b_{i}}}(z)
equals the obstruction from the 8th ECH capacity, or equivalently, from the nonperfect exceptional class
(3,1;2,1^{\times 5})
.

4.4.
Stabilization

We now show that all the staircases found in this paper stabilize.

Consider the
stabilized embedding function

c_{H_{b},s}(z)=\inf\ \bigl\{{\lambda}\ \Big|\ E(1,z)\times\mathbb{R}^{2s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\lambda}H_{b}\times\mathbb{R}^{2s}\bigr\},\quad s\geq 0.

We always have
c_{H_{b}}(z)=c_{H_{b},0}(z)\geq c_{H_{b},1}(z)\geq c_{H_{b},2}(z)\geq\dots
because by taking the product with the identity
{\rm id}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
any embedding

{\iota}:E(1,z)\times\mathbb{R}^{2s}\to{\lambda}H_{b}\times\mathbb{R}^{2s}
extends to an embedding

{\iota}\times{\rm id}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}:E(1,z)\times\mathbb{R}^{2s+2}\to{\lambda}H_{b}\times\mathbb{R}^{2s+2}
.

Remark 4.4.1
.

We will base our discussion on the arguments in
[
CGHM
]
that consider the stabilized embedding function for
\mathbb{C}P^{2}
. These arguments extend to the semipositive case, that is to arbitrary six-manifolds and to monotone manifolds of any dimension. Thus, unless
b=1/3
, we consider only the case
s=1
. However, this restriction is purely technical, and our results should hold for all
s
.
{\Diamond}

Lemma 4.4.2
.

Let
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
be a perfect class.
Then,

c_{H_{b},s}(p/q)\geq\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(p/q)
for
s=1
and all
b\in[0,1)
.
When
b=1/3
, this holds for all
s\geq 1
.

Proof.

The analogous result was proved in
[
CGH
]
in the case of the target
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, that is when
b=0
. A rather different proof of this result was outlined in
[
CGHM
, Rem.3.1.8(ii)]
. The latter methods extend almost immediately to the current situation. One simply needs to replace the ambient manifold
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
by the blowup
H_{b}

in the basic stabilization result
[
CGHM
, Prop.3.6.1]
that specifies when an immersed curve in a four-dimensional cobordism of both genus and Fredholm index zero
persists under stabilization.
46
46

46

A detailed proof of a generalized version of this result that applies explicitly to the semipositive case will appear in a forthcoming paper by McDuff–Siegel
[
MSie
]
.

One important point is that the statement in
[
CGHM
, Prop.3.6.1]
permits both the multiplication of the target by
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the perturbation of the symplectic form on the target.
This holds because the crucial compactness statement applies to a generic path
J_{t}
in the space
{\mathcal{J}}(T)
, where
(J_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a path of admissible almost complex structures that are compatible with a path
{\omega}_{t}
of symplectic forms on the target
In particular, one can change
b
during this deformation. However, it is important that the form remain semipositive, since otherwise one would need to use virtual techniques to
control the possible degenerations. Thus we must either fix
b=1/3
or take
s=1
.

To
apply
[
CGHM
, Prop.3.6.1]
, it suffices to
produce a
suitable curve in the negative completion
X_{b_{0}}
of
{\lambda}H_{b_{0}}{\smallsetminus}{\iota}(E(1,p/q+{\delta}))
in class
dL-mE_{0}
that has one negative end on the
p
-fold cover of the short orbit
{\beta}_{1}
on
{\partial}E(1,p/q+{\delta})
, where
b_{0}\in[0,1)
is any suitable value and
{\delta}
is a suitably small constant. This curve necessarily has genus zero because it is constructed by neck-stretching an exceptional sphere.

We will construct this curve for
b_{0}=m/d
, where
(d,m)
are the degree coordinates of
{\bf{E}}
, by using the method explained in

[
CGHM
, §3.1]
. As we will see, the argument given there is much simplified by the fact that when
b=m/d
the class
{\bf{E}}
is live at
z=p/q
, so that
the
capacity function
c_{H_{m/d}}(p/q)
is equal to the obstruction
\mu_{{\bf{E}},m/d}(p/q)
.
Geometrically this means that, for any
\varepsilon>0
,
there is a symplectic embedding
{\iota}:E(1,p/q+{\delta})\to{\lambda}H_{m/d}
, where
{\delta}>0
is sufficiently small and

{\lambda}:=c_{H_{b}}(p/q)+\varepsilon=\frac{p}{d-m^{2}/d}+\varepsilon=\frac{pd}{pq-1}+\varepsilon,

where we use Lemma
1.2.1
(i) and the identities in (
1.2.2
).

Let
(m_{1},\dots,m_{n})=(q,\dots,1)
be the integral weight expansion of
p/q
. By

[
CGHM
, Prop.2.1.2]
, for any
{\delta},{\delta}^{\prime}>0
we may embed the disjoint union of
n
balls of capacities
(1-{\delta}^{\prime})m_{1},\dots,(1-{\delta}^{\prime})m_{n}
into the interior of
{\iota}(E(1,p/q+{\delta}))
and then blow them up to obtain a symplectic form
\tilde{{\omega}}
on
H_{m/d}\#n{\overline{\mathbb{CP}}}\,\!^{2}
in the class Poincaré dual to
{\lambda}(L-(m/d)E_{0})-(1-{\delta}^{\prime})\sum_{i}m_{i}E_{i}
. We now consider what happens to the unique representative
C_{\bf{E}}
of the class
{\bf{E}}
in this blowup manifold as we stretch the neck around the boundary
{\partial}({\iota}(E(1,p/q+{\delta})))
of the ellipsoid. As described in
[
CGHM
, §3.1]
, the curve
C_{\bf{E}}
converges to a limiting building, whose
top component
C_{U}
lies in the negative completion
X_{m/d}
of
H_{m/d}{\smallsetminus}{\iota}(E(1,p/q+{\delta}))
.
Thus
C_{U}
has negative end on some orbit set
\{({\beta}_{1},k)\}\cup\{({\beta}_{2},\ell)\}
, where
this notation means that the ends on the short orbit
{\beta}_{1}
have total multiplicity
k
, while
those on the long orbit
{\beta}_{2}
have total multiplicity
\ell
.
Our aim is to show that (when
\varepsilon,{\delta},{\delta}^{\prime}
are all sufficiently small) there is only one possibility for
C_{U}
, namely
C_{U}
must be connected, embedded, and have one negative end on

{\beta}_{1}
of multiplicity
p
. Granted this, the result follows from
[
CGHM
, Prop.3.6.1]
.

For all
{\delta},\varepsilon>0
, the energy
{\lambda}(d-m^{2}/d)-(k+\ell(p/q+{\delta}))

of the top part
C_{U}
is positive but arbitrarily small. Hence, because
{\lambda}=\frac{p}{d-m^{2}/d}+\varepsilon
, we must have the equality

\frac{p}{d-m^{2}/d}(d-m^{2}/d)=k+\ell(p/q),\quad\mbox{ i.e. }\ p=k+\ell p/q.

Since
p,q
are relatively prime and
k,\ell\geq 0
, the only possibilities are:
k=p,\ell=0
or
k=0,\ell=q
. Further, if
C_{U}
were disconnected or multiply covered, there would be
d^{\prime}<d,m^{\prime}<m
and either
k<p
or
\ell<q
such that

\frac{p}{d-m^{2}/d}(d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}m/d)=k,\;\;\mbox{ or }\;\;\frac{p}{d-mb}(d^{\prime}-m^{\prime}m/d)=\ell(p/q)

Since
d^{2}-m^{2}=pq-1
, the first of these equations simplifies to

p(dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime})=k(pq-1)
, which implies
p|k
and hence has no solution. Similarly, the second equation implies that
q|\ell
, so that it also has no solution.
Therefore
C_{U}
must be connected and somewhere injective.

We can now appeal to the properties of the ECH index
I(C)
. As explained in the survey article
[
Hu
, §3.4]
, this index
I(C)
is a generalization of the quantity
c_{1}(A)+A\cdot A
for a curve in a closed manifold of homology class
A
, and has the property that
I(C)\geq 0
for any immersed curve
C
. Moreover, by
[
CGHM
, (2.2.28)]
and
[
CGH
, (2.4)ff]
,
if the above curve
C_{U}
has negative end on
\{({\beta}_{1},k)\}\cup\{({\beta}_{2},\ell)\}
then

I(C)=d^{2}-m^{2}+3d-m-{\rm gr}({\beta}_{1}^{k}{\beta}_{2}^{\ell}),

where
{\rm gr}({\beta}_{1}^{k}{\beta}_{2}^{\ell})
is twice the number of lattice points
(m,n)
that lie in the nonnegative quadrant of the plane and (strictly) below the line
through
(k,\ell)
with slope
-q/(p+{\delta})
.
Thus, when
\gcd(p,q)=1
,

{\rm gr}({\beta}_{1}^{p})=pq+p+q-1,\quad{\rm gr}({\beta}_{2}^{q})=pq+p+q+1.

Since

d^{2}-m^{2}+3d-m=pq-1+p+q,

there can be no curve in
X_{m/d}
in class
dL-mE_{0}
with negative end on
{\beta}_{2}^{q}
, since such a curve would have
I(C)<0
. Moreover, curves in this class with negative end on

{\beta}_{1}^{p}
have
I(C)=0
. By
[
Hu
, §3.9]
, this implies that it must have ECH partitions, which in the current situation means that it has one negative end: see for example
[
CGHM
, Rmk.2.2.1ff]
. The fact that
C_{U}
has Fredholm index zero follows from
[
Hu
, (3.1)]
,
[
CGHM
, (2.2.28)]
, and the computation of the monodromy angle of
\beta_{1}
(called
\gamma_{1}
) below
[
CGHM
, (2.2.23)]
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.4.3
.

(i)

For all
b\notin{\it Block}
except the special rational
b
, the stabilized embedding function
c_{H_{b},1}
has a staircase. If, in addition,
b\notin{\partial}({\it Block})
and
b\neq 1/3
, then
c_{H_{b},1}
has both ascending and descending staircases.

(ii)

c_{H_{1/3},s}(z)=c_{H_{1/3}}(z)
for all
s\geq 1
and all
1\leq z\leq a_{\min}
.

Proof.

We always have
c_{H_{b},s}(z)\leq c_{H_{b}}(z)
, because any embedding
{\iota}:E(1,z)\to{\lambda}H_{b}
extends to an embedding
{\iota}\times{\rm id}:E(1,z)\times\mathbb{R}^{2s}\to{\lambda}H_{b}\times\mathbb{R}^{2s}
. Lemma
4.4.2
implies that if
H_{b}
has a staircase with steps given by perfect classes
{\bf{E}}_{k}
with centers at the points
p_{k}/q_{k}
then the stabilized function
c_{H_{b},1}(z)
must equal
c_{H_{b}}(z)
at the step centers, and for
z\approx p/q
have
c_{H_{b}}(z)
as an upper bound.
But by the remarks about overshadowing classes before (
3.1.3
), we know that
c_{H_{b}}(z)=\mu_{{\bf{E}}_{k},b}(z)
for
z\approx p/q
. Thus

c_{H_{b}}(z)
is determined for
z\approx p/q
by
the scaling property
c_{H_{b},1}({\lambda}z)\leq{\lambda}c_{H_{b},1}(z)
(as in
[
CG-HMP
, Prop.2.1]
), and by monotonicity (i.e. the fact that
c_{H_{b},1}(z)
is nondecreasing).
Since
c_{H_{b},1}(z)
also has these properties for each
s
it follows that

c_{H_{b},1}(z)=c_{H_{b}}(z)
in some neighborhood of each step center.
Thus all the staircases that we have found stabilize. This proves (i).

The argument (i), now applied with any
s\geq 1
, shows that the staircase in
H_{1/3}
stabilizes. Thus for each
s

c_{H_{1/3},s}(z)=c_{H_{1/3}}(z)
in a neighborhood of the centers of the steps of the
1/3
staircase. Since elsewhere

c_{H_{1/3},s}(z)\leq c_{H_{1/3}}(z)
, it follows from scaling and monotonicity
that these functions must coincide on the whole interval
1\leq z\leq a_{\min}
.
This proves (ii).
∎

Although the calculation of
c_{H_{b},s}
for an arbitrary
b\in(0,1)
seems out of reach at present, the monotone case is more approachable. Indeed, Hind’s folding construction
in
[
Hi
]
implies that

(4.4.1)

\displaystyle c_{H_{1/3},s}(z)\leq\frac{3z}{1+z},\quad z\geq 1,\ s\geq 1.

As in the case when the target is a ball, the graph of
z\mapsto\frac{3z}{1+z}
crosses the volume constraint at the staircase accumulation point
a_{\min}=3+2\sqrt{2}
, and one can conjecture that

(4.4.2)

\displaystyle c_{H_{1/3},s}(z)=\frac{3z}{1+z},\quad z\geq a_{min},\ s\geq 1.

This is known as the
Stabilized embedding conjecture
for
H_{1/3}
.

Corollary 4.4.4
.

The stabilized embedding conjecture holds for the monotone Hirzebruch surface
H_{1/3}
on the closure of the set of all points
z>a_{\min}
that are the centers of perfect classes.

Proof.

For all perfect classes
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q)
,
we saw in the proof of Lemma
4.3.1
that
Proposition
4.4.3
and equation (
4.3.2
) imply that

c_{H_{1/3},s}(z)\geq\frac{3z}{1+z},\quad\mbox{ when }z=p/q

Hence by (
4.4.1
) we must have equality at these points. The statement in the lemma then follows by continuity.
∎

Remark 4.4.5
.

(i) The set of
z
for which we know that

c_{H_{1/3},s}(z)=\frac{3z}{1+z}
has quite complicated structure because the function
b\to\mathrm{acc}(b)
is two-to-one; and it is probably best understood via Figure
2.2
. For example, even though the only step center in the interval
[w_{2},w_{1}]=[41/7,7]
with
b>1/3
is
6
, there are infinitely many step centers corresponding to classes with
b<1/3
— indeed all the steps centers in the complete families
S^{\#}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
and
R^{\#}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U})
.
Nevertheless, one can check that it is nowhere dense.

(ii) It may well not be true that, when
H_{b}
has a staircase,
c_{H_{b}}(z)=c_{H_{b},1}(z)
for
z
in some neighborhood of
z_{\infty}:=\mathrm{acc}(b)
since the capacity function
c_{H_{b}}(z)
might be determined near
z_{\infty}
by curves that do not stabilize.
For example, when
\mathrm{acc}(b)<6
, figures such as
[
BHM
, Fig.5.3.1]
seem to show that the obstruction
z\mapsto\frac{1+z}{3-b}
from the (nonperfect) class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}:=3L-E_{0}-2E_{1}-E_{2\dots 6}
is
b
-live for some values of
z>\mathrm{acc}(b)
that
are arbitrarily close to
\mathrm{acc}(b)
. However,
because
\frac{1+z}{3-1/3}>\frac{3z}{1+z}
when
z>a_{\min}
, it follows from
(
4.4.1
) that this obstruction does not stabilize when
b=1/3
. Hence the curve that gives this embedding obstruction does not persist under stabilization for any
b

since curves that do stabilize
are not sensitive to changes in the parameter
b
.

An explicit example of this kind was worked out in
[
CGHM
]
in the case of
H_{0}
(or the ball) and the
class
{\bf{E}}^{\prime\prime}=3L-2E_{1}-E_{2\dots 7}
. This class, which obstructs the capacity function
c_{H_{0}}
of the ball for
z\in(\tau^{4},7]
, definitely does not stabilize. Indeed, if one blows
H_{0}
up seven times inside the ellipsoid
E(1,7+{\delta})
and stretches the neck as in the proof of Lemma
4.4.2
,
one can show that the top component of the resulting building has two negative ends, one on the long orbit and one on the short orbit, so that the stabilization result
[
CGHM
, Prop.3.6.1]
does not apply.
Further, (
4.4.1
) implies that
c_{H_{0},s}(7)\leq 21/8
, while the obstruction from
{\bf{E}}^{\prime\prime}
at
z=7
, if it did stabilize, would be the larger value
8/3
.

(iii) Here is another example of an obstruction that does not stabilize even though it is given by the “nearly perfect”  class
{\bf{E}}=6L-3E_{0}-2E_{1\dots 7}
. (See Remark
4.2.2
for further discussion of this class.)
When
b=1/3
,
we have
\mu_{{\bf{E}},1/3}(7)=14/5>21/8=c_{H_{1/3},k}(7)
. Therefore the obstruction from this class cannot stabilize. Note that the proof of Lemma
4.4.2
breaks down because we would be considering trajectories with negative end on the short orbit of
{\partial}E(1,7+)
with total multiplicity
14
, and in this case the ECH partition is
(7,7)
.
Thus these curves do not have a single negative end.

{\Diamond}

Appendix A
Continued fractions

For the convenience of the reader, we here collect together some useful facts about continued fractions.
Each rational number
a>1
has a continued fraction representation
[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n}]
, where
s_{i}
is a positive integer, and
n>0
unless
a\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By convention, the last entry in a continued fraction is always taken to be
>1
, since
[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n},1]=[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n}+1]
; for example

[1;3,1]=1+\frac{1}{3+\frac{1}{1}}=1+\frac{1}{4}=[1;4].

Order properties:

If
n
is odd, then
[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n}]>[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n}+1]
, in other words, if the last place is odd, increasing this entry decreases the number represented.
For example, because
\frac{2}{13}<\frac{3}{19}
, we have

\displaystyle[1;3,6,2]=1+\frac{1}{3+\frac{1}{6+\frac{1}{2}}}=1+\frac{1}{3+\frac{2}{13}}>[1;3,6,3]=1+\frac{1}{3+\frac{1}{6+\frac{1}{3}}}=1+\frac{1}{3+\frac{3}{19}}.

Further, although increasing an even place usually increases the number represented, the opposite is true
if one increases an even place
47
47

47

This place would necessarily be the one just after the last; and notice that the initial place is labelled
0
and hence is considered to be even.
from
0
to a positive number. For example

(A.1)

\displaystyle[1;4]=1+\frac{1}{4}>[1;4,2]=1+\frac{1}{4+\frac{1}{2}}=1+\frac{2}{9}>[1;4,1]=[1;5]=1+\frac{1}{5}.

Similarly, if one increases an odd place from
0
, the corresponding number increases even if the new entry
s_{n}
is
1
. Thus

(A.2)

\displaystyle[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n-1}]<[s_{0};s_{1},\dots,s_{n}]\quad\mbox{ if $n$ is odd}\ \forall s_{n}\geq 1.

For example,
2<[2;1,2]<[2;1]=3.

Length:

There are two natural notions of the length of a continued fraction, namely

(A.3)

\displaystyle\ell_{CF}([s_{0};\dots,s_{n}]):=n+1,\quad\ell_{wt}([s_{0};\dots,s_{n}]):=\sum_{i=0}^{n}s_{i}.

The first notion is common in the theory of continued fractions, while in our previous papers we used the second notion since this describes the length of (or number of nonzero entries in) the corresponding
weight expansion
W(p/q)
and hence plays a central role in the theory; witness the fact proved in
[
CG-HMP
, Prop.2.30]
that if the function
z\mapsto\mu_{{\bf{E}},b}(z)
is obstructive (that is, greater than the volume) on some interval
I
, then there is a unique point
48
48

48

In the case of a quasi-perfect class, this point is the center; cf. the formulas in (
1.2.2
).

z_{0}\in I
of minimal weight length at which its slope changes.
Since we will now use both these notions, for clarity we will call
\ell_{CF}(p/q)
the
CF-length
of
p/q
, while
\ell_{wt}(p/q)
is its
weight length
.

Notice that by Remark
2.1.3
(i), the accumulation points of our staircases are quadratic irrationals and therefore have infinite continued fractions that are eventually periodic.

Weight decomposition:
The
integral weight decomposition

W(p/q)
of a rational number

p/q=[s_{0};\dots,s_{n}]
is an array of numbers that are recursively defined
49
49

49

Note that this recursion can be read either as defining
q_{{\alpha}+1}
in terms of
q_{\alpha},q_{{\alpha}-1}
, or as defining

q_{{\alpha}-1}
in terms of
q_{\alpha},q_{{\alpha}+1}
where the last nonzero entry is
1
.

as follows:

(A.4)

\displaystyle W(p/q)

\displaystyle=\bigl(q_{0}^{\times s_{0}},q_{1}^{\times s_{1}},\dots,q_{n}^{\times s_{n}}\bigr),\quad\mbox{ where }

\displaystyle q_{0}=q,\ q_{1}=p-s_{0}q_{0},\dots,q_{\alpha}=q_{{\alpha}-2}-s_{{\alpha}-1}q_{{\alpha}-1},{\alpha}\geq 2,\;\;q_{n}=1.

For example,
5/3=[1;1,2]
and
W(5/3)=(3;2,1,1)=:(3;2,1^{\times 2})
. If
W(p/q)
is
(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})
then

(A.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}=p+q-1,\quad\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}^{2}=pq,\quad w_{1}=q,\;\;w_{N}=1.

Using the fact that the first entry of
W(p/q)
is
q
, we can interpret the other entries as the denominators
q_{\alpha}
of the
{\alpha}
th “tail”
p_{\alpha}/q_{\alpha}
of
p/q=[s_{0};\dots,s_{n}]
.
Namely, if we define
p_{\alpha}/q_{\alpha}:=[s_{\alpha};\dots,s_{n}]
, then we have

(A.6)

\displaystyle W(p_{\alpha}/q_{\alpha}):=W([s_{\alpha};\dots,s_{n}])=\bigl(q_{\alpha}^{\times s_{\alpha}},\dots,q_{n}^{\times s_{n}}\bigr),\quad 0\leq i\leq n.

Moreover,
q_{n}=1,q_{n-1}=s_{n},
and the other terms
q_{n-1},q_{n-2},\dots
can be calculated from the recursion

q_{n-{\alpha}-1}=s_{n-{\alpha}}q_{n-{\alpha}}+q_{n-{\alpha}+1}
. In particular,

q=q_{0},\quad p=p_{0}=s_{0}q_{0}+q_{1}.

Each group of terms
q_{\alpha}^{\times s_{\alpha}}
is called a block.

Note that the entries of
W(p/q)
are integers. We also sometimes use the weight decomposition of
p/q
defined as follows:

(A.7)

\displaystyle w(p/q):=\frac{1}{q}W(p/q)=(1,\dots,1/q)

More generally, the weight expansion of
z\approx p/q
is defined by the same recursion that defines
w(p/q)
. For example if
6<z<7
,
w(z)=(1^{\times 6},z-6,\dots)
.
For more information, see
[
McS
, Lem.2.2.1]
.

Appendix B
Computations on triples

We now gather together various computations that are needed for the main argument.
Note that for any index
\bullet={\lambda},\mu,\rho
we define
r_{\bullet}:=p_{\bullet}+q_{\bullet}
.

Lemma B.1
.

The following identities and inequalities hold in any triple
\mathcal{T}
.

(i)

t_{\rho}r_{\mu}-t_{\mu}r_{\rho}=8p_{\lambda}
.

(ii)

t_{\rho}d_{\mu}-t_{\mu}d_{\rho}=3p_{\lambda}
,
t_{\lambda}d_{\mu}-t_{\mu}d_{\lambda}=3q_{\rho}

(iii)

m_{\rho}d_{\mu}-m_{\mu}d_{\rho}=\varepsilon p_{\lambda}
,
m_{\mu}d_{\lambda}-m_{\lambda}d_{\mu}=\varepsilon q_{\rho}

(iv)

t_{\lambda}r_{\mu}-t_{\mu}r_{\lambda}=8q_{\rho}
.

(v)

t_{\lambda}t_{\rho}<2t_{\mu}
; in particular
3\leq t_{\lambda},t_{\rho}<t_{\mu}
.

Proof.

The formulas (i) and (iv) are proved in
[
M1
, Lem.4.6]
by direct computation using the adjacency and
t
-compatibility conditions.
50
50

50

One could also check them for the basic triples
\mathcal{T}^{n}_{*},n\geq 0,

and show that they are preserved by symmetries and mutations.

Formulas (ii) and (iii) can be deduced from (i) since
8d_{\bullet}=3r_{\bullet}+\varepsilon t_{\bullet}
while
8m_{\bullet}=r_{\bullet}+3\varepsilon t_{\bullet}
by (
1.2.3
).

Using the formulas in (
3.1.10
), it is straightforward to check
that (v) holds for the basic triples in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
. Moreover (v) continues to holds under mutation.
Indeed, if (v) holds for
\mathcal{T}
then it holds for
x\mathcal{T}
, provided that

(t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}-t_{\rho})^{2}+t_{\mu}^{2}<t_{\lambda}(t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}-t_{\rho})t_{\mu}
. But this
simplifies to
t_{\mu}^{2}<t_{\rho}(t_{\lambda}t_{\mu}-t_{\rho})
, which holds by assumption.
A similar argument works for the mutation
y\mathcal{T}
. Therefore (v) holds for all
triples in
{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}
.
It follows that (v) holds for all triples since the variable
t_{\bullet}
is preserved by the symmetries
S,R
and the given expression is symmetric in
{\lambda},\rho
(which are interchanged by
R
.)
∎

Corollary B.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be any triple and denote
r_{\bullet}:=p_{\bullet}+q_{\bullet}.

Then

(B.1)

\frac{r_{\mu}}{t_{\mu}}>\max\bigl(\frac{r_{\lambda}}{t_{\lambda}},\frac{r_{\rho}}{t_{\rho}}\bigr),

except in the case
of
y^{k}R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
, when we have

(B.2)

\frac{r_{\lambda}}{t_{\lambda}}=\frac{r_{\mu}}{t_{\mu}}=3>\frac{r_{\rho}}{t_{\rho}}=1/3.

Proof.

The inequality (
B.1
) follows from Lemma
B.1
(i) and (iv) whenever
p_{\lambda},q_{\rho}>0
. This holds in all cases except for the triples
y^{k}R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
since they have
q_{\rho}=0
. But they satisfy (
B.2
); indeed, this holds for
R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
by the formulas in (
3.1.11
),
and the fact that
q_{\rho}=0
in
R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
implies that the ratio
r/t
is constant for all terms in the ascending stairs in this triple.
∎

We next turn to the details need to complete the proof of Lemma
3.1.10
. To this end, we must
verify the inequality (
3.1.5
)

\frac{t_{k+1}^{2}-8}{t_{k+1}r_{k+1}}>\frac{t_{k}^{2}-8}{t_{k}r_{k}},

where
k
labels the staircase step, in the situations stated below.

Lemma B.3
.

(i)

The inequality (
3.1.5
) holds for the first two steps in the descending pre-staircases associated to
yx^{i}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}
for all
i\geq 0

(ii)

The inequality (
3.1.5
) holds for the second and third steps in the descending pre-staircases associated to
y^{i}R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
,
i\geq 0
.

Proof.

To prove (i), first note that it holds for
i=0
since the first two steps in
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathcal{T}_{y}}_{u}
are

{\bf{B}}^{U}_{1},{\bf{E}}_{[7;3,6]}
with
(r,t)=(9,5),(158,62)
. In general, the descending staircase
{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{0}
has steps
{\bf{E}}_{x^{i}[7;4]},i\geq 0
, and we denote their
(r,t)
coefficients as
(r_{i},t_{i})
. They satisfy a recursion with parameter
3
and
seeds
(9,5),(r_{0},t_{0})=(33,13)
so that

(r_{1},t_{1})=(90,34),\quad(r_{2},t_{2})=(237,89).

By Remark
2.1.5
(ii) the ratios
r_{i}/t_{i}
increase, and using (
2.1.12
) one finds that
for
i\geq 2
,

(B.3)

\displaystyle\frac{5}{2}<\frac{237}{89}<\frac{r_{i}}{t_{i}}<\lim\frac{r_{i}}{t_{i}}=:\frac{R}{T}=\frac{45+39\sqrt{5}}{25+11\sqrt{5}}<\frac{8}{3}.

When
i\geq 1
, the descending pre-staircase associated to
yx^{i}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*}
has first two steps
{\bf{E}}_{x^{i-1}[7;4]},

{\bf{E}}_{yx^{i}[7;4]}
where the
(r,t)
components of

{\bf{E}}_{yx^{i}[7;4]}
are
\bigl(t_{i-1}r_{i}-7,t_{i-1}t_{i}-3\bigr)
. Thus it suffices to show that

\frac{(t_{i-1}t_{i}-3)^{2}-8}{(t_{i-1}t_{i}-3)(t_{i-1}r_{i}-7)}>\frac{t_{i+1}}{r_{i+1}}>\frac{t_{i-1}^{2}-8}{t_{i-1}r_{i-1}}.

We now prove by induction on
i\geq 1
that each of these inequalities holds.

The base case
i=1
is readily checked. With
i\geq 2
, we assume these inequalities known for
i-1
.
To simplify notation let us write

t^{\prime\prime}:=t_{i},\ t^{\prime}:=t_{i-1},\ t:=t_{i-2}\mbox{ so that }\ \ t_{i+1}=3t^{\prime}-t=8t^{\prime}-3t,

and similarly for
r
.
Then the second inequality is equivalent to

(8t^{\prime}-3t)tr>(8r^{\prime}-3r)(t^{2}-8),\quad i.e.\quad 64r^{\prime}-24r>3t^{\prime}(tr^{\prime}-rt^{\prime})=144t^{\prime},

where we have used the fact that
tr^{\prime}-rt^{\prime}=t_{i-2}r_{i-1}-r_{i-2}t_{i-1}
is constant and equal to
48
. This will hold if
8r^{\prime}-3r>18t^{\prime}
, i.e. if
8r_{i-1}-3r_{i-2}>18t_{i-1}
.
It is easy to check that
r_{i-2}<\frac{2}{5}r_{i-1}
for all
i
, so that
8r_{i-1}-3r_{i-2}>\frac{34}{5}r_{i-1}
. Thus it suffices to verify that
\frac{34}{5}r_{i-1}\geq 18t_{i-1}
for
i\geq 2
. But this holds when
i=2
, and holds for
i>2
because
r_{i}/t_{i}
increases.

Now consider the first inequality, which in the current notation is equivalent to

(3r^{\prime\prime}-r^{\prime})\bigl((t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}-3)^{2}-8\bigr)>(3t^{\prime\prime}-t^{\prime})(t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}-3)(t^{\prime}r^{\prime\prime}-7).

Putting the terms of highest order on the left and simplifying the LHS as above, we obtain

\displaystyle(t^{\prime})^{2}t^{\prime\prime}\bigl((3r^{\prime\prime}-r^{\prime})t^{\prime\prime}-(3t^{\prime\prime}-t^{\prime})r^{\prime\prime}\bigr)=48(t^{\prime})^{2}t^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad>(3r^{\prime\prime}-r^{\prime})(6t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}-1)-(3t^{\prime\prime}-t^{\prime})\bigr(7t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}+3t^{\prime}r^{\prime\prime}-21\bigl).

By (
B.3
), we have
t_{i}>\frac{3}{8}r_{i}>\frac{15}{16}t_{i}
for all
i
. Therefore

3r^{\prime\prime}-r^{\prime}<\frac{8}{3}(3t^{\prime\prime}-t^{\prime})
(since
x_{i+1}=3x^{\prime\prime}-x^{\prime}
) and

7t^{\prime\prime}+3r^{\prime\prime}>\frac{29}{2}t^{\prime\prime}
. Hence

\displaystyle(3r^{\prime\prime}-r^{\prime})(6t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}-1)-(3t^{\prime\prime}-t^{\prime})\bigr(7t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}+3t^{\prime}r^{\prime\prime}-21\bigl)

\displaystyle\qquad<(3t^{\prime\prime}-t^{\prime})\bigl(16t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}-\tfrac{29}{2}t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}+21\bigr)<2(3t^{\prime\prime}-t^{\prime})t^{\prime}t^{\prime\prime}<48(t^{\prime})^{2}t^{\prime\prime}.

where the first inequality forgets the
-1
, the second holds because
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\geq 5
and the last holds because
t^{\prime\prime}<3t^{\prime}
.

To prove (ii), note first that the descending stairs in
y^{i}R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{*})
has first two steps
with
(p,q,t)
coordinates
(2,0,3)
and
(p_{i},q_{i},t_{i})
and recursion parameter
t_{i-1}
, where
(p_{i},q_{i},t_{i})

is defined recursively with recursion parameter
3
and first two terms
(p_{-1},q_{-1},t_{-1})=(13,2,5),(p_{0},q_{0},t_{0})=(34,5,13)
.
Therefore, we
need to show that

\frac{(t_{i-1}t_{i}-3)^{2}-8}{(t_{i-1}t_{i}-3)(t_{i-1}r_{i-1})}>\frac{t_{i}^{2}-8}{t_{i}r_{i}}.

It suffices to show that the left hand side is always greater than
1/3
and the right hand side is always less than
1/3
. The latter is immediate from the fact that
t_{i}=3r_{i}
by (
B.2
). For the former, again using the fact that
t_{i-1}=3r_{i-1}
, we need to show

\displaystyle(t_{i-1}t_{i}-3)^{2}-8

\displaystyle>(t_{i-1}t_{i}-3)(t_{i-1}^{2}),

or, equivalently
t_{i-1}^{2}t_{i}^{2}-6t_{i-1}t_{i}+1>t_{i-1}^{3}t_{i}-3t_{i-1}^{2}
.
It suffices to show the much weaker inequality
t_{i-1}^{2}t_{i}^{2}-6t_{i-1}t_{i}>t_{i-1}^{3}t_{i}
,
or, equivalently,
t_{i-1}(t_{i}-t_{i-1})>6
.
But this holds because
t_{k}\geq 5
and
t_{k}-t_{k-1}>1
. This completes the proof.
∎

The next inequalities are needed in the proof of Lemma
3.2.5
.

Lemma B.4
.

The following inequality holds for all triples.

(B.4)

\displaystyle\frac{d_{\mu}}{d_{\rho}}-\frac{d_{\rho}}{d_{\mu}}>t_{\lambda}-1.

Proof.

We first check that this holds for
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{n},R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{n}),n\geq 0
, where in the first case we may use the formulas in (
2.1.14
), and in the second we use that, by (
1.2.3
) and (
3.1.11
), the
(d,t)
coordinates of

R^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{n})
are

(d_{\lambda},t_{\lambda})=(5(n+1),2n+5),(d_{\rho},t_{\rho})=(5n,2n+3),\ (d_{\mu},t_{\mu})=(10n^{2}+25n+13,4n^{2}+16n+13).

To see that they hold for the images of the symmetries, first consider
(S^{i})^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{*}_{n})
so
t_{\lambda}=2n+3
. Letting
x:=d_{\mu}
and
y:=d_{\rho}
of
(S^{i})^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{*}_{n})
, (
B.4
) is equivalent to

x^{2}-y^{2}-xy(2n+2)>0.

Considering this as a quadratic in
x
for fixed
y,
this holds if

(B.5)

x>(n+1+\sqrt{n^{2}+2n+2})y.

Further, by Remark
2.1.14
, we have that
x=(2n+3)y-d_{seed}
where
d_{seed}
is the degree coordinate of
(S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}^{U}_{seed,u})=(S^{i})^{\sharp}(-2,0,-5,-1)
and hence
d_{seed}<0.
Therefore, (
B.5
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle(2n+3)y-d_{seed}

\displaystyle>(n+1+\sqrt{n^{2}+2n+2})y

\displaystyle(2+n-\sqrt{n^{2}+2n+2})y

\displaystyle>d_{seed},

which holds as
d_{seed}
is negative and the left hand size is positive. A similar argument holds for
(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}(\mathcal{T}^{*}_{n}).

Finally we check that they remain true under mutation.
If (
B.4
) holds for
\mathcal{T}
then for
x\mathcal{T}
we have

\frac{t_{\lambda}d_{\mu}-d_{\rho}}{d_{\mu}}-\frac{d_{\mu}}{t_{\lambda}d_{\mu}-d_{\rho}}=t_{\lambda}-\frac{d_{\rho}}{d_{\mu}}-\frac{d_{\mu}}{t_{\lambda}d_{\mu}-d_{\rho}}>t_{\lambda}-1,

where the inequality holds because each of the last two terms is
<1/2
. To show this, notice that any two degree values of sequential classes
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime},{\bf{E}}^{\prime\prime}
in a staircase satisfy
d^{\prime\prime}=td^{\prime}-d
for
t\geq 3
(see Lemma
B.1
(v)) and
d<d^{\prime}
. This is even true if
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
is the first geometric step in the staircase, thinking of
(S^{i}R^{\delta})^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell/u,seed})
as the algebraic first step
{\bf{E}}
if necessary (see Remark
2.1.14
).

For
y\mathcal{T}
we need

\frac{t_{\rho}d_{\mu}-d_{\lambda}}{d_{\rho}}-\frac{d_{\rho}}{t_{\rho}d_{\mu}-d_{\lambda}}>t_{\mu}-1.

But
t_{\rho}d_{\mu}-t_{\mu}d_{\rho}=3p_{\lambda}
by Lemma
B.1
(ii). So by similar reasoning we have

\frac{t_{\rho}d_{\mu}-d_{\lambda}}{d_{\rho}}-\frac{d_{\rho}}{t_{\rho}d_{\mu}-d_{\lambda}}=\frac{3p_{\lambda}-d_{\lambda}}{d_{\rho}}+t_{\mu}-\frac{d_{\rho}}{t_{\rho}d_{\mu}-d_{\lambda}}>t_{\mu}-1,

because for any triple
3p-d>0
by combining (
1.2.3
), the fact that
p/q>5
, and induction using Corollary
B.2
, where we prove the base case
r/t>1/3
using the formulas for
(S^{i}R^{\delta})(2n+6,1)
in terms of the
y_{i}
in the proof of Lemma
B.8
.
∎

We next find suitable constants
x_{0},x_{1}
to use in Lemma
3.2.5
(iii).

Lemma B.5
.

(i)

For each
n\geq 0
and
i\geq 0
, the limiting
b_{\infty}
-values of the descending staircases
{\mathcal{S}}
in the family
(S^{i})^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}\cap[2n+6,2n+8])
satisfy

\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,n-1}/d_{i,n-1})<\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})<\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,n}/d_{i,n}),

where
d_{i,n},m_{i,n}
are the degree components of
{\bf{E}}_{i,n}:=(S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n})
. Here
{\bf{E}}_{0,-1}={\bf{B}}^{U}_{-1}=(2,1,4,1,1,1)
.

(ii)

Similarly, the limiting
b_{\infty}
-values of the descending staircases in the family
(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}({\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{S}}^{U}\cap[2n+6,2n+8])
satisfy

\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,n+2}^{\prime}/d_{i,n+2}^{\prime})<\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})<\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,n+1}^{\prime}/d_{i,n+1}^{\prime}),

where
d_{i,n}^{\prime},m_{i,n}^{\prime}
are the degree components of
{\bf{E}}_{i,n}^{\prime}:=(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n})
.

Proof.

Notice that we have

(B.6)

\frac{p_{i,n}}{q_{i,n}}<\partial^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{i,n}})\leq\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})<\partial^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{i,n+1}})

because
\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})
is the limit of the step centers of a descending staircase. To obtain the upper bound in case (i), notice that the class
{\bf{E}}_{i,n}
is a step in a staircase accumulating to
\partial^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{i,n+1}})>\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})
. By Corollary
2.3.3
, for every staircase (whether
b>1/3
or
b<1/3
) the values
\mathrm{acc}(m_{k}/d_{k})
always descend to the
z
-coordinate
\mathrm{acc}(b)
of the accumulation point, thus

\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})<\partial^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{i,n+1}})<\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,n}/d_{i,n}).

To obtain the lower bound in (i), we would like to apply Lemma
2.1.10
to
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
the middle and
{\bf{E}}
the right entry of

(S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n-1},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n})=((S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed}),{\bf{E}}_{i,n-1},{\bf{E}}_{i,n}).

Thus we would obtain
\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,n-1}/d_{i,n-1})<p_{i,n}/q_{i,n}<\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})
, where for the second inequality we use (
B.6
). The only hypothesis of Lemma
2.1.10
that does not automatically hold for
{\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
(because they are the middle and right entries of a quasi-triple, the other hypotheses hold) is
\sqrt{2}p^{\prime}<p
. Because
S^{i}(2n+6,1)=(2ny_{i+1}+y_{i+2},2ny_{i}+y_{i+1})
, this holds if

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(2(n-1)y_{i+1}+y_{i+2})

\displaystyle>2ny_{i+1}+y_{i+2}

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;ny_{i+1}>(\sqrt{2}-1)y_{i+1}+\tfrac{1}{2}y_{i}.

Therefore if
n\geq 1
, we obtain the lower bound
\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,n-1}/d_{i,n-1})<p_{i,n}/q_{i,n}
.

When
n=0
, it is not true that
\mathrm{acc}(m_{0,-1}/d_{0,-1})<6
, where
6=p_{0,0}/q_{0,0}
, so we instead show

(B.7)

\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,-1}/d_{i,-1})<S^{i}(7,1)<\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty}).

For the first inequality in (
B.7
), the analogue of (
2.1.17
) with
(p_{i},q_{i})=S^{i}(7,1)=(y_{i+2}+y_{i+1},y_{i+1}+y_{i})
is

\frac{p_{i,-1}^{2}+q_{i,-1}^{2}+2}{p_{i,-1}q_{i,-1}-1}<\frac{(y_{i+1}+y_{i+2})^{2}+(y_{i}+y_{i+1})^{2}}{(y_{i}+y_{i+1})(y_{i+1}+y_{i+2})}.

We may use the fact that
t^{2}=p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}+8
and
t_{i,-1}=1
to simplify the LHS to
6+1/(p_{i,-1}q_{i,-1}-1)
. Now because
p^{2}-6pq+q^{2}
is invariant under
S
and
(y_{1}+y_{2})^{2}-6(y_{1}+y_{2})(y_{0}+y_{1})+(y_{0}+y_{1})^{2}=8
, subtracting 6 from both sides gives us

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(-2y_{i+1}+y_{i+2})(-2y_{i}+y_{i+1})-1}

\displaystyle<\frac{8}{(y_{i}+y_{i+1})(y_{i+1}+y_{i+2})},

which simplifies to

\displaystyle y_{i+1}y_{i}+2y_{i+1}^{2}-1+y_{i+2}y_{i+1}

\displaystyle<32y_{i+1}y_{i}-32y_{i+1}^{2}+8y_{i+2}y_{i+1}+8,

where in the second line we have used
y_{i+1}^{2}=y_{i+2}y_{i}+1
, which is easy to prove by induction. It is enough to show a stronger inequality with the constants removed, thus we can divide by
y_{i+1}
to obtain

y_{i}+2y_{i+1}+y_{i+2}<32y_{i}-32y_{i+1}+8y_{i+2},\text{ i.e. }34y_{i+1}<7y_{i+2}+31y_{i},

which follows immediately from the definition of the
y_{i}
sequence.

For the second inequality, in (
B.7
), it suffices to show that
(S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{0})={\bf{E}}_{i,0}
blocks
S^{i}(7,1)
. This is shown in the second conclusion of Corollary
2.3.4
; see also
[
MM
, Cor.4.1.5]
.
In case (ii), notice that

(B.8)

\frac{p_{i,n+1}^{\prime}}{q_{i,n+1}^{\prime}}<\partial^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{i,n+1}^{\prime}})\leq\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})<\partial^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{i,n}^{\prime}}).

We obtain
\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})<\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,n+1}^{\prime}/d_{i,n+1}^{\prime})
as in (i): notice that
{\bf{E}}_{i,n+1}^{\prime}
is a step in a staircase accumulating to
\partial^{-}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{i,n}^{\prime}})>\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})
; finish as in the proof of the analogous inequality in (i).

To obtain
\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})>\mathrm{acc}(m_{i,n+2}^{\prime}/d_{i,n+2}^{\prime})
, we can apply Lemma
2.1.10
with
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}
the left and
{\bf{E}}
the middle entry of

(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+1},{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n+2})=({\bf{E}}_{i,n+2}^{\prime},{\bf{E}}_{i,n+1}^{\prime},(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}({\bf{E}}^{U}_{\ell,seed})).

Because
S^{i}R(2n+6,1)=(2ny_{i+2}+y_{i+1},2ny_{i+1}+y_{i})
, the
p
coordinates satisfy
p^{\prime}>p
, which is stronger than the hypothesis
\sqrt{2}p^{\prime}>p
of Lemma
2.1.10
. Because
p_{i,n+1}^{\prime}/q_{i,n+1}^{\prime}<\partial^{+}(I_{{\bf{E}}_{i,n+1}^{\prime}})\leq\mathrm{acc}(b_{\infty})
by (
B.8
), we obtain the desired inequality.
∎

Corollary B.6
.

Let
d_{i,n}
and
m_{i,n}
be the degree coordinates of
(S^{i})^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n})
, let
d_{i,n}^{\prime}
and
m_{i,n}^{\prime}
be the degree coordinates of
(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}({\bf{B}}^{U}_{n})
, and let
b_{\infty}
be as in Lemma
B.5
.

(i)

If
i
is even then
m_{i,n-1}/d_{i,n-1}<b_{\infty}<m_{i,n}/d_{i,n}
and
m_{i,n+1}^{\prime}/d_{i,n+1}^{\prime}<b_{\infty}<m_{i,n+2}^{\prime}/d_{i,n+2}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

If
i
is odd then
m_{i,n}/d_{i,n}<b_{\infty}<m_{i,n-1}/d_{i,n-1}
and
m_{i,n+2}^{\prime}/d_{i,n+2}^{\prime}<b_{\infty}<m_{i,n+1}^{\prime}/d_{i,n+1}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The conclusions follow immediately from Lemma
B.5
and the fact that
\mathrm{acc}
is orientation preserving/reversing according to whether
b>1/3
or
<1/3
, or equivalently, according to whether
i+\delta
is even/odd.
∎

Our final results complete
the proof of Lemma
3.2.5
(iv).

Lemma B.7
.

Given two classes
{\bf{E}}=(d,m,p,q,t,\varepsilon)
and
{\bf{E}}^{\prime}=(d^{\prime},m^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},t^{\prime},\varepsilon)
, let
x=m/d
and
x^{\prime}=m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}
.

(i)

If
({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}^{\prime},{\bf{E}})
is a quasi-triple, then

\frac{1-xx^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}-x}=\frac{\varepsilon p^{\prime}q}{p_{\lambda}}
; while

(ii)

if

({\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
is a quasi-triple, then

\frac{1-xx^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}-x}=-\frac{\varepsilon pq^{\prime}}{q_{\rho}}.

Proof.

We have

\frac{1-(m/d)(m^{\prime}/d^{\prime})}{m^{\prime}/d^{\prime}-m/d}=\frac{dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}}{m^{\prime}d-md^{\prime}}.

If
({\bf{E}}_{\lambda},{\bf{E}}^{\prime},{\bf{E}})
is a quasi-triple, then, we have (
2.1.10
) and Lemma
B.1
(iii) give
dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}=p^{\prime}q
and
m^{\prime}d-md^{\prime}=\varepsilon p_{\lambda}.

Similarly if
({\bf{E}},{\bf{E}}^{\prime},{\bf{E}}_{\rho})
is a quasi-triple, we have
dd^{\prime}-mm^{\prime}=pq^{\prime}
and, by Lemma
B.1
(iii),
m^{\prime}d-md^{\prime}=-\varepsilon q_{\rho}.

∎

Lemma B.8
.

(i)

For fixed
i>0
and
n\geq 0
,
let

(S^{i})^{\sharp}{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n}:=(d_{1},m_{1},p_{1},q_{1}),\;\;(S^{i})^{\sharp}{\bf{B}}^{U}_{n-1}:=(d_{0},m_{0},p_{0},q_{0}),\quad x_{i}=\frac{m_{i}}{d_{i}},\;\;i=0,1.

Assume
(p_{\lambda},q_{\lambda},t_{\lambda})\in(S^{i})^{\sharp}(C{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{n})
is such that
(p_{\lambda},q_{\lambda})\neq S^{i}(2n+6,1)
. Then

(B.9)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1-x_{0}x_{1}}{x_{1}-x_{0}}\right|>\frac{p_{\lambda}}{t_{\lambda}-1}.

(ii)

For fixed
i,n
, let

(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}(2n+8,1):=(d_{0},m_{0},p_{0},q_{0}),\quad(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}(2n+10,1):=(d_{1},m_{1},p_{1},q_{1}),

and set
x_{i}=\frac{m_{i}}{d_{i}},i=0,1
.
Then, the inequality (
B.9
) also holds for any
(p_{\lambda},q_{\lambda},t_{\lambda})\in(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}(C{\mathcal{S}}^{U}_{n})
.

Proof.

By Remark
2.1.14
, we have
\bigl((S^{i})^{\sharp}(1,1),(S^{i})^{\sharp}(2n+6,1),(S^{i})^{\sharp}(2n+8,1)\bigr)
is a quasi-triple.
Applying Lemma
B.7
, we have

\left|\frac{1-x_{0}x_{1}}{x_{1}-x_{0}}\right|=\frac{p_{0}q_{1}}{y_{i+1}-y_{i}}

since
(S^{i})(1,1)=(y_{i+1}-y_{i},y_{i}-y_{i-2}).
It remains to show that

\frac{p_{0}q_{1}}{y_{i+1}-y_{i}}>\frac{p_{\lambda}}{t_{\lambda}-1}.

By assumption, we have

S^{i}\left({2n+7}/{1}\right)<p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}<S^{i}\left({2n+8}/{1}\right).

Further,
S^{i}(2n+7,1)=S^{i}(w_{1})=(2ny_{i+1}+y_{i+2}+y_{i+1},2ny_{i}+y_{i+1}+y_{i}):=(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
by (
2.3.7
). Let
r=p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}.
So
r<p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}.

We have

(B.10)

t_{\lambda}^{2}/p_{\lambda}^{2}=1+q_{\lambda}^{2}/p_{\lambda}^{2}-6q_{\lambda}/p_{\lambda}+8>1-6/r+1/r^{2}=\frac{r^{2}-6r+1}{r^{2}}

as
1-6/r+1/r^{2}
is an increasing function for
r>a_{min}
.
A proof by induction verifies that

r^{2}-6r+1=\frac{4n^{2}+16n+8}{(q^{\prime})^{2}},

so

t_{\lambda}^{2}/p_{\lambda}^{2}>\frac{4n^{2}+16n+8}{(p^{\prime})^{2}}.

Therefore,

p_{\lambda}/(t_{\lambda}-1)<\frac{p^{\prime}}{\sqrt{4n^{2}+16n+8}}\frac{2n+3}{2n+2}.

It remains to show that

\frac{p_{0}q_{1}}{y_{i+1}-y_{i}}>\frac{p^{\prime}}{\sqrt{4n^{2}+16n+8}}\frac{2n+3}{2n+2}.

Substituting in

p_{0}=2(n-1)y_{i+1}+y_{i+2},q_{1}=2ny_{i}+y_{i+1},\quad p^{\prime}=2ny_{i+1}+y_{i+2}+y_{i+1}

and simplifying by taking
n=0
to the terms on the right hand side that decrease in
n
, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle(2(n-1)y_{i+1}+y_{i+2})(2ny_{i}+y_{i+1})

\displaystyle>\frac{3}{4\sqrt{2}}(2ny_{i+1}+y_{i+2}+y_{i+1})(y_{i+1}-y_{i}).

This holds as for
n\geq 0,
we have
2ny_{i}+y_{i+1}>y_{i+1}-y_{i}
and

2(n-1)y_{i+1}+y_{i+2}>\frac{3}{4\sqrt{2}}(2ny_{i+1}+y_{i+2}+y_{i+1}).

This proves (i).

Towards (ii), notice that
\bigl((S^{i}R)^{\sharp}(2n+8,1),(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}(2n+6,1),(S^{i}R)^{\sharp}(1,1,1,1)\bigr)
is also a quasi-triple, so that

\left|\frac{1-x_{1}x_{0}}{x_{1}-x_{0}}\right|=\frac{p_{1}q_{0}}{y_{i+2}-y_{i}}.

Further, we have

S^{i}R\left({2n+8}/{1}\right)<p_{\lambda}/q_{\lambda}<S^{i}R\left({2n+6}/{1}\right)
where

(S^{i}R)(2n+8,1)=(2(n+1)y_{i+2}+y_{i+1},2(n+1)y_{i+1}+y_{i})
.
The rest of the argument is very similar to (i) and is again left to the reader.
∎
